Genericity of Polyak–Lojasiewicz Inequalities for Entropic Mean-Field Neural ODEs. Thanks: S. Daudin and F. Delarue acknowledge the financial support of the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon Europe research and innovation program (ELISA project, Grant agreement No. 101054746). Views and opinions expressed are however those of the author(s) only and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Union or the European Research Council Executive Agency. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.
Samuel Daudin, François Delarue

Abstract
. We address the behavior of idealized deep residual neural networks (ResNets), modeled via an optimal control problem set over continuity (or adjoint transport) equations. The continuity equations describe the statistical evolution of the features in the asymptotic regime where the layers of the network form a continuum. The velocity field is expressed through the network activation function, which is itself viewed as a function of the statistical distribution of the network parameters (weights and biases). From a mathematical standpoint, the control is interpreted in a relaxed sense, taking values in the space of probability measures over the set of parameters. We investigate the optimal behavior of the network when the cost functional arises from a regression problem and includes an additional entropic regularization term on the distribution of the parameters. In this framework, we focus in particular on the existence of stable optimizers –that is, optimizers at which the Hessian of the cost is non-degenerate. We show that, for an open and dense set of initial data, understood here as probability distributions over features and associated labels, there exists a unique stable global minimizer of the control problem. Moreover, we show that such minimizers satisfy a local Polyak–Lojasiewicz inequality, which can lead to exponential convergence of the corresponding gradient descent when the initialization lies sufficiently close to the optimal parameters. This result thus demonstrates the genericity (with respect to the distribution of features and labels) of the Polyak–Lojasiewicz condition in ResNets with a continuum of layers and under entropic penalization.



1.
Introduction

This paper is dedicated to a control problem of mean-field type modeled on the training of certain deep neural networks. Mathematically it takes the following form:

(1.1)

\mbox{ minimize }J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}\bigr)\mbox{ with respect to }\bm{\nu},

where the total cost
J
is defined by

(1.2)

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}(x,y)+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt,

and the infimum is taken over flows of probability measures
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
on a
parameter
space
A
. In the expression above,
\gamma_{T}
is an element of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, the set of Borel probability measures on the product space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
(for two integers
d_{1},d_{2}
), and is determined from
\bm{\nu}
via the continuity equation

(1.3)

\partial_{t}\gamma_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\Bigl(\int_{A}b(\cdot,a)d\nu_{t}(a)\gamma_{t}\Bigr)=0\quad\mbox{ in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},

with initial condition
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
at
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and final horizon
T>0
. Importantly, the vector field
b:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
driving the solution in (
1.3
) is assumed to satisfy some type of universal approximation property. In the machine learning interpretation,
(\gamma_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
describes the evolution of the joint distribution of the
features
and
labels
along the layers of the neural network whose depth is indexed by the parameter
t\in[t_{0},T]
.

In the control problem (
1.2
), the terminal cost
L:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is determined by the underlying regression task while
\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})
, in the running cost, denotes the relative entropy of the parameter distribution
\nu_{t}
at layer-time
t
with respect to some prior measure
\nu^{\infty}
and serves as a regularization. The intensity of this entropic penalization is given by the (small) parameter
\epsilon>0
.

1.1.
From Deep
ResNets
to Mean-Field Optimal Control.

The cost functionnal
(
1.2
)
together with the continuity
equation
(
1.3
)
should indeed be regarded as a
mathematical idealization of
some regression tasks achieved by
a certain
type of deep neural
networks in machine learning.
Those networks are usually referred to as
‘Residual Neural Networks’
(
ResNets
for short)
and were primarily
introduced by
He et al.
[
24
]

in the analysis of learning procedures for image recognition.
The very purpose of
ResNets
is
to overcome the degradation in accuracy that may occur when the number of layers increases.
To explain such issues, the main hypothesis in

[
24
]
, which is supported by numerical evidences,
is that mappings like the identity may not be well approximated by iterating non-linear ones.
In turn, the principle of

ResNets

is to precondition the problem by decomposing each layer of the network as the sum of the identity mapping and of a residual
standard parametrized activation function.

Mathematically, this decomposition can be easily reformulated as the elementary step in the discretization of a continuous dynamical system. This is the starting point of a series of works in the literature, initiated
by E in
[
20
]

and Haber and Ruthotto in

[
23
]
, in which regression methods based on a
ResNets
architecture
are studied with tools from optimal control theory in continuous time.
In this approach, the
time parameter in the controlled system represents the layer index in the network.
See for instance
Agrachev and Sarychev

[
1
]
,
Li et al.

[
30
]
and Scagliotti
[
37
]

for further examples.

A first idealization in this framework is therefore to replace the

a priori
discrete in time structure of the network by a continuous in time
architecture, as done here. Formally, this amounts to say that the number of layers is infinite. The resulting controlled system is sometimes called a
Neural ODE
.

A second idealization is to assume that there are infinitely many neurons per layer
and that only their common statistical distribution enters the instantaneous dynamics of the network. This is exactly what we do here through the formalism of relaxed controls, see in particular
(
1.3
) where
\nu_{t}
is interpreted as a control. This idea is consistent with the approach introduced
by
Mei et al.

[
33
]
,
Bach and Chizat
[
15
]
,
and
Rotskoff and Van den Eijnden

[
35
]

to study mathematically the gradient descent in one-layer neural networks.
Intuitively, the passage from
a finite to an infinite number
of neurons relies on an averaging
principle that is typical of mean-field models (but which is not studied here).
In the one-layer case, the very benefit of it is to convexify the loss function.

Let us stress again that in this framework, the probability measure
\gamma_{t}
in (
1.3
) can be viewed as the joint law of the features after layer-time
t
and the labels (that remain fixed along the layers of the network). In other words,
if one writes
X_{0}

for the random variable modelling the feature in entry of the neural network and

Y_{0}
for the random variable
modeling the label that has to be regressed on
X_{0}
,

\gamma_{t}
is the law of

(X_{t},Y_{0})
, i.e.,

(1.4)

\gamma_{t}={\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{t},Y_{0})^{-1},

where
X_{t}
is the value at time
t
of the ODE

(1.5)

\dot{X}_{t}=\int_{A}b(X_{t},a)d\nu_{t}(a)

initialized from
X_{0}
.
In turn, if
L(x,y)
is thought as a cost between
x
and
y
, then the integral
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}(x,y)

is nothing but the mean cost between
the outputs of the network and the labels.
As for the entropic term, it forces a form of strict convexity of the Hamiltonian
associated with the
control problem and also guarantees that optimal solutions inherit some properties (regularity, concentration and functional inequalities) from the measure
\nu^{\infty}
. The same idea can be found in some of the aforementioned references on the one-layer case: therein,
the underlying optimization problem becomes strictly
convex in presence of an additional entropy.
That said, it is worth emphasizing that, in comparison with the one-layer case, our problem is not at all convex
because of the successive iterations of the nonlinear activation function.
This makes the analysis much more challenging.
In particular, this is one of our objective here to show that, despite the lack of convexity, important local stability results
remain, at least
for
generic
(that is ‘many’ in a suitable sense) initial conditions.

1.2.
Main Assumptions and Example.

Throughout this work, the following assumptions are in order. The parameter space
A
is the euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
for some
d^{\prime}\geq 1
.

Assumption (Regularity).
The functions
b
,
\ell
and
L
introduced above
satisfy the conditions below:

(i)

The function

b:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
is smooth in the sense that all the derivatives
\nabla_{x}^{k}\nabla^{l}_{a}b
with
0\leq k+l\leq 4
exist and are (jointly) continuous. Moreover, there exists a constant
C\geq 0
such that for all
(x,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times A
and all integers
k,l
with
0\leq k+l\leq 4
, it holds

|\nabla_{x}^{k}\nabla_{a}^{l}b(x,a)|\leq C(1+|a|^{k+1})(1+|x|^{l}).

We also assume that
b(x,0)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
.

(ii)

The prior measure
\nu^{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}(A)
takes the form

(1.6)

\nu^{\infty}(da):=\frac{1}{z^{\infty}}\exp{\bigl(-\ell(a)\bigr)}da,\quad\quad z^{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}:}=\int_{A}e^{-\ell(a)}da,

for some twice continuously differentiable convex potential
\ell:A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying, for a constant
c>0
and for all
a\in A
,

(1.7)

\nabla^{2}_{aa}\ell(a)\geq c(1+|a|^{2})I_{d^{\prime}}.

In particular this means that
\ell
grows at least
as
|a|^{4}
.

(iii)

The final cost
L:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is bounded from below
and three times differentiable and, together with its derivatives, satisfies the growth assumption

\left\|L\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{2,1}}:=\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\frac{|L(x,y)|}{1+|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}}+\frac{|\nabla L(x,y)|}{1+|x|+|y|}+\frac{|\nabla^{2}L(x,y)|}{1+|x|+|y|}+\frac{|\nabla^{3}L(x,y)|}{1+|x|+|y|}<+\infty.

We also assume
b
to be discriminating in the following sense:

Assumption (Discriminating Property).
For any probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and any two
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
-valued random variables
X,Z
on this probability space with
Z\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
,
the following implication holds true:

\biggl(\forall a\in A,\quad\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X,a)\cdot Z\bigr]=0\biggr)\quad\Rightarrow\Bigl(\mathbb{P}\mbox{-almost-surely},\quad\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z|X\bigr]=0\Bigr),

where
\mathbb{E}
denotes the expectation under
\mathbb{P}
.

Example 1.1
.

Here is the prototypical example for the vector field
b
.
The condition (i) in

Assumption (
Regularity
)
is satisfied if

A
is taken as

{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
and

b

has the following structure:

(1.8)

b(x,a)=\sigma(a_{1}\cdot x+a_{2})a_{0},\quad\bigl(x,a=(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})\bigr)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times A,

for a so-called
activation
function
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
that is bounded and smooth with bounded derivatives.

Moreover,
b
satisfies
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
if
\sigma
verifies a type of universal approximation property typical of activation functions in machine learning. In particular, all our assumptions are satisfied if
\sigma
is the hyperbolic tangent or the logistic function. See Section
4.5
for the details.

Our assumptions on
L
are satisfied if
d_{1}=d_{2}
and
L
is the quadratic loss ie,
L(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}|x-y|^{2}
. The assumptions on
\ell
are satisfied if
\ell(a)=c_{1}|a|^{4}+c_{2}|a|^{2}
for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.

Remark 1.2
.

Some classical sets of data
(\ell,L,b)
do not fit our assumptions. Within our prototypical example for the vector field
b
, the assumptions are not satisfied if
\sigma
is the rectified linear unit because it is neither
\mathcal{C}^{1}

nor bounded. The convexity condition on
\ell
is not satisfied if
\ell(a)=\frac{1}{2}|a|^{2}
. In our setting, we need some stronger form of convexity and coercivity for
\ell
to cope with the growth of
b
and its derivatives when we derive some log-Sobolev inequalities for optimal solutions of the control problem, see Lemma
2.11
and Remark
2.12
. If

b
had the form
b(x,a)=\sigma(a_{1}x+a_{2})
with
a_{1}\in M_{d_{1}}(\mathbb{R}),a_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
, for
\sigma
a smooth and bounded activation function acting component-wise,
then our analysis could go through with
\ell=\frac{1}{2}|a|^{2}
. In such a case,
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
would still be satisfied for classical activation functions verifying a universal approximation property.
Lastly,
the assumption that
b(x,0)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}

(see item (i) of
Assumption (
Regularity
)
)
is used to prove Propositions
4.2

and
4.3
, see Step 3 in each case. This assumption is always satisfied in the framework of Example
1.1
.

1.3.
Main Results

We now expose the main results of the paper. To ease the understanding and in particular to lighten the notation, we have chosen to provide informal statements at this stage of the document.
For this reason, we call them
‘meta-statements’.
Full results are given
in the following sections,
once the mathematical objects
supporting our analysis
have been introduced.

In a nutshell, our objective is to understand the
properties of the minimizers
of
(
1.1
)
together with the behavior
of the related gradient descent initialized near these minimizers, and this for

generic
initial conditions.
Here,
the word
generic

has a rather vague meaning.
Below, we give a topological definition to it.
That said, the philosophy is quite simple to explain.
Due to the lack of convexity,
the control problem
(
1.1
)
cannot be expected to
have a unique minimizer
for any initial condition. To wit, it is well-know that non-convex
finite dimensional control problems may develop singularities
in finite time.
However,
it is also known
that, still in finite dimension,
singularities
cannot be in fact too numerous.
In particular,
from a topological point of view, they have an empty interior.
The first main result of this paper is to show that,
thanks to
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
, this picture remains true in our setting. In other words,
the Universal Approximation Theorem of neural networks translates, in the mean-field
ResNets
setting, in
the form of a robustness property that is true for many initial conditions.

Meta-Theorem 1.3
.

There exists an open dense subset

{\mathcal{O}}
of
[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
(with

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})

denoting the space of probability measures on

{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}
with a finite third moment)
such that, for any initial condition

(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
the control problem (
1.1
)
has a unique minimizer and this minimizer is
stable
.

We do not provide a precise definition of stability at this stage and only mention that it is related to the non-degeneracy of the Hessian of the cost (
1.2
). The exact definition is given in Section
2
and the complete version of
Meta-Theorem
1.3
is stated in
Theorem
4.1
. Instead, we explain here
two main consequences of these stability properties, which we believe are relevant to machine learning.

To give the reader a clearer picture, it is worth recalling that, in practice, the challenge is to numerically find the optimal parameters of the neural network.
Usually, this is done by means of a gradient descent algorithm (or a variant thereof).

In the multi-layer setting,
the algorithm takes the form of a collection of intertwined gradient descents
indexed by the successive layers of the network. Each of these
descents returns a (possibly inaccurate) approximation of the
optimal state of the
neurons
at the corresponding layer.
Mathematically, this principle
can be formalized at follows:
at any time

t\in[t_{0},T]
, we can construct
a flow
(\nu_{t}^{s})_{s\geq 0}
,
depending on a new time parameter

s

and following a gradient flow in the space

{\mathcal{P}}(A)
(in the sense of Ambrosio et al.
[
2
]
).
In the mean-field approach to
ResNets
, this formalism
was introduced
in Jabir et al.
[
28
]
.
Of course, one relevant objective is to obtain guarantees under which

(\nu_{t}^{s})_{s\geq 0}
converges
to
\nu_{t}^{*}
, the optimal state of the network
at time
t
.
This is precisely where the stability properties of the minimizers become especially useful.

The analysis of the descent goes through
an explicit formula for
the
s
-derivative of the cost along the descent:

(1.9)

\frac{d}{ds}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0},{\bm{\nu}}^{s}\bigr)=-\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{s}\bigr),

for some (non-negative) functional
\mathcal{I}
that is made explicit in Section
5
. Above the symbol

{\bm{\nu}}^{s}
denotes the entire curve
(\nu^{s}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
at descent-time
s
.
Thanks to the aforementioned stability properties on the minimizers of

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
we manage to prove the following functional inequality, which is sometimes referred to as
a (here local)
Polyak–Lojasiewicz condition
:

Meta-Theorem 1.4
.

For every compact subset
{\mathcal{K}}
of
\mathcal{O}
,
there exists a constant
c>0
such that, for
any
initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in{\mathcal{K}}

and any curve
{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
that is close enough to the unique
minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}
of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
,

{\mathcal{I}}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)\geq c\Bigl(J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\Bigr).

The rigorous version of
Meta-Theorem

1.4

is stated in
Section

5
, see in particular
Theorem

5.1
. Therein,
we clarify in which sense the curve

{\bm{\nu}}

has to be close to
the
minimizer

{\bm{\nu}}^{*}
.

As expected,
Meta-Theorem

1.4

becomes especially relevant in the analysis of the aforementioned gradient descent, provided the latter one be
initialized in the neighborhood of the optimal control
\bm{\nu}^{*}
.
To make this precise, a reasonable conjecture is that, for any initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
of the optimal control problem (
1.1
), and for any initial condition
\bm{\nu}^{0}=(\nu_{t}^{0})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
of the gradient descent flow
\bm{\nu}^{s}=((\nu_{t}^{s})_{t\in[t_{0},T]})_{s\geq 0}
, provided
\bm{\nu}^{0}
is close enough to the unique minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}
of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
, the cost
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{s})
converges exponentially fast to the optimal value
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*})
as
s\to\infty
.
We emphasize that the result would follow from a standard argument once Meta-Theorem
1.4
has been established, and is omitted here for reasons of length. Here is the underlying principle: Proposition

4.5
establishes that the unique minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}
is an isolated critical point of the cost
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
; combined with the identity
(
1.9
) (the proof of which would however deserve to be expanded), it follows from standard results in dynamical systems (usually referred to as LaSalle’s principle) that the gradient descent flow remains in a neighborhood of the minimizer when initialized sufficiently close to it. The conclusion is then obtained by invoking Meta-Theorem

1.4
, which is the central result in this context, to deduce the exponential convergence of the cost.

1.4.
Comparison with Existing Literature

The control problem (
1.1
)
is a mean-field control problem – see Lions
[
31
]
, Chapter
6
in Carmona and Delarue
[
14
]
, and Section 3.7 in Cardaliaguet et al.
[
11
]
for introductory material on the subject together with Lacker
[
29
]
for the use of relaxed controls in this context. Indeed,
the controlled dynamics given by
the continuity
equation
(
1.3
)
take values in the space
of probability measures over
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}
. However, it is non-standard in the sense that the control
(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
(even taken in a
relaxed form) is not a vector field taking values
in
{\mathcal{P}}(A)
–in which case it would take the form
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\ni(t,x)\mapsto\nu_{t}(x,da)\in\mathcal{P}(A)
–, but just an element of

{\mathcal{P}}(A)
.

Stability properties of optimal solutions for more
classical
mean-field optimal control problems have recently drawn significant attention, see Briani and Cardaliaguet
[
8
]
,
Cardaliaguet and Souganidis
[
13
]

and Cardaliaguet et al.
[
12
]
. Part of the analysis relies on a classical phenomenon in optimal control theory and calculus of variations: the so-called
Jacobi necessary optimality condition
(see Cannarsa and Sinestrari
[
10
]
Chapter 6, and Remark 6.3.7 therein for the terminology). Generally speaking, this condition asserts that, for some optimal control problems with linear dynamics and strictly convex Hamiltonians, there is no
conjugate
point along optimal trajectories. In our framework, this is exactly the statement that, given an optimal trajectory
(t,\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
starting from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
, then
(t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}}^{*})
belongs to the set
\mathcal{O}
of Meta-Theorem
1.3
for any later time
t_{1}>t_{0}
.
In the setting of Problem (
1.1
) and for general vector fields
b
satisfying
Assumption (
Regularity
)
, we do not expect to recover this property. However,
one of the main contributions of our work is to prove that
this is indeed the case when
b
satisfies
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
(see the next sub-section for some details).

To gain further intuition about the difference with standard mean-field control, it is helpful to revisit the particle interpretation of the classical setting. In the latter, the controlled trajectory
(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

describes
the statistical (or macroscopic) evolution of a continuum of agents. At the microscopic (or individual) level,
agents evolve according to ODEs that are driven by a common velocity field but are initialized from possibly different initial conditions.
This picture remains true in our framework: the velocity field is

b(x,\nu_{t})
and the initial conditions are statistically distributed according to
\gamma_{0}
.
What changes here is that two agents, in the same continuum but at different individual locations, play the control
\nu_{t}
at time
t
in exactly the same manner. This is consistent from a machine learning perspective and contrasts with the standard rule in mean-field control, where the control applied by each agent explicitly depends on its own state. The reader may have noticed another subtlety in our
control problem: the ‘variable
y
’ in
(
1.2
)
is not impacted by the dynamics
(
1.3
). From a microscopic
point of view, it says that an agent has two main features, in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
: whilst the first one evolves according to the ODE driven by the field
(t,x)\mapsto b(x,\nu_{t})
, the second remains constant with time.

Control problems of the same kind as Problem (
1.1
) (with possibly different types of regularizations) have already been studied in the machine learning literature as an idealized model for the training phase of deep neural networks. To the best of our knowledge,
this model goes back to the pioneering works
of
E et al.

[
21
]
(including the derivation of the Pontryagin maximum principle). The use of entropic penalization can be found in Hu et al,
[
25
]

Jabir et al.

[
28
]
. We also refer to Lu et al.
[
32
]
, Isobe
[
26
]

Bonnet et al.
[
7
]
, Barboni et al.
[
4
]

and Ding et al.

[
19
]

for a more recent works in the same vein. In essence, what sets our approach apart from other works involving entropic regularization is that none of our results requires the penalization intensity
\epsilon
to be large.

Establishing a Polyak–Lojasiewicz inequality (PL inequality) is regarded as a key step in the analysis of gradient descent for neural networks. In the context of mean-field Deep ResNets, the following results have been established. In the work
[
26
]
, a PL inequality is shown
under a strong enough moment (instead of entropic) regularization. The authors of
[
4
]
establish a PL inequality without regularization, but degenerating as the number of features (assumed finite) used during training increases. This latter condition can be interpreted as a structural smallness assumption.
The convergence of the gradient descent (which, in our case, could be derived using Meta-Theorem
1.4
, as previously explained, the descent taking the same form as in
[
28
]
) and
of the generalization error (i.e., the mean field limit) have also been studied in
[
28
,
7
]
, in the presence of a penalization—by entropy in the former, and by moments in the latter—both assumed to be sufficiently strong.
We emphasize again that, by contrast, our approach imposes no minimal threshold on the intensity
\epsilon
(of the entropic penalty).
The interested reader may find further recent developments on PL inequalities in (among others) the following works: in the context of ODE control, Gassiat and Suciu
[
22
]
establish a randomized PL inequality for gradient descent under highly oscillatory initialization; furthermore, Monmarché and Reygner
[
34
]
investigate the long-time behavior of (uncontrolled) mean-field diffusions using local PL inequalities.

In these models, the passage from discrete to continuous time raises particularly subtle questions, which we do not address here. We refer the reader to Chizat and Netrapalli

[
16
]
, Cont et al.
[
17
]
and Gassiat and Suciu
[
22
]
.

Another meaningful aspect of the problem, which we leave for future investigation, concerns the
expressivity
of the network: given an initial distribution
\gamma_{0}
, what is the minimal loss achievable in the associated control problem? This question is related to the controllability of the system. In the context of deep neural networks—albeit in more or less idealized settings—, this issue has been addressed, among others, by Agrachev and Sarychev
[
1
]
, Cuchiero et al.
[
18
]
, Li et al.
[
30
]
, and Ruiz-Balet and Zuazua
[
36
]
. Although we do not address the controllability properties of our system, it is worth stressing that
none of our results depend on the
efficiency
of the network. In other words, it might very well be that the labels (represented by the variable

Y_{0}
in (
1.4
)) are far from any function of the features (represented by the variable
X_{0}
in (
1.4
)). Equivalently, the initial distribution
\gamma_{0}
might be
far
from any measure of

diagonal
form
\mu_{0}\circ(i_{d},F)^{-1}
for some
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
describing the law of
X_{0}
and some measurable function
F:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
. In this framework, the optimal cost (i.e., the optimal loss)
is not small but our result remains relevant. This is in contrast with some known convergence results, see
[
4
,
28
]
.

1.5.
Method of Proof

1.5.1.
First order condition and Jacobi principle.

Much of our analysis revolves around first and second order optimality conditions for the control problem (
1.1
). The first order conditions state that an optimal control
\bm{\nu}
necessarily takes the form

(1.10)

\nu_{t}(a)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\Bigr),\quad t\in[t_{0},T],

where "
\propto
" means "proportional to" and
(\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})=(\gamma_{t},u_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
solves the forward-backward system

(1.11)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}u_{t}(x,y)-b\bigl(x,\nu_{t}\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)=0,&\quad(t,x,y)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad u_{T}(x,y)=L(x,y),&\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\gamma_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu_{t})\gamma_{t}\bigr)=0,&\quad\textrm{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad\gamma_{t_{0}}=\gamma_{0}&\quad\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

If this system had a unique solution, it would characterize the (necessarily unique) optimal solution to the control problem. However, as previously mentioned, such uniqueness is not to be expected for arbitrary initial conditions
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
, due to the lack of any global convexity condition ensuring the uniqueness of critical points in the control problem. From a technical perspective, establishing uniqueness for the system satisfied by
(\bm{\nu},\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
is delicate, owing to the forward–backward structure of the continuity and transport equations: the equation for
\bm{\gamma}
is equipped with an initial condition, while the equation for
\bm{u}
is subject to a terminal condition.

Meta-Theorem
1.3
then relies on the aforementioned Jacobi principle: for any initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
, any (between the possibly many) optimal solution
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
with associated optimal trajectory
\bm{\gamma}^{*}=(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
, and any later time
t_{1}>t_{0}
,
(\nu_{t}^{*})_{t\in[t_{1},T]}
is the unique minimizer of
J((t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}}^{*}),\cdot)
and, furthermore, this solution is
stable
. In particular, for
t_{1}
close to
t_{0}
, we have found an initial condition
(t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}})
, nearby
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
, for which the control problem has a unique (and stable) optimal solution. To show that
(\nu_{t}^{*})_{t\in[t_{1},T]}
is the only optimal solution, we prove that optimal solutions for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
cannot
bifurcate
, in the sense that any two optimal solutions
(\nu^{*,i}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
,
i=1,2
, that coincide at the initial time must
remain identical over the entire time horizon.
This implies the uniqueness of optimal controls for
J((t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}),\cdot)
since, otherwise, by dynamic programming, we could construct two solutions for
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
that coincide up to time
t_{1}
but bifurcate afterward.

Importantly, the fact that optimal controls cannot bifurcate relies in a subtle way on the discriminating property
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
. To explain this, let us assume for simplicity, that the state dynamic and the terminal cost do not depend on the
y
-variable. In this case, the controlled dynamic is simply a trajectory in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}})
. Consider two optimal solutions
(\nu_{t}^{*,i})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
,
i=1,2
, for
the initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
, such that
\nu_{t_{0}}^{*,1}=\nu_{t_{0}}^{*,2}
.
If we forget about the normalizing constant in (
1.10
), the first order conditions imply that

(1.12)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t_{0}}^{1}(x)d\gamma_{0}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t_{0}}^{2}(x)d\gamma_{0}(x),\quad\forall a\in A,

where
\bm{u}^{1}
and
\bm{u}^{2}
are the solutions to the backward transport equation in (
1.11
) associated to
\bm{\nu}^{*,1}
and
\bm{\nu}^{*,2}
respectively. This is where
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
comes into play. After properly reformulating (
1.12
) as an equality between expectations of random variables, it tells us that
\nabla_{x}u^{1}_{t_{0}}
and
\nabla_{x}u^{2}_{t_{0}}
must coincide on the support of
\gamma_{0}
. Therefore we end up with two solutions
(\bm{\nu}^{*,i},\bm{\gamma}^{*,i},\bm{u}^{*,i})
,
i=1,2
to the system of optimality conditions (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) such that
\gamma_{t_{0}}^{1}=\gamma_{t_{0}}^{2}=\gamma_{0}
and
\nabla_{x}u_{t_{0}}^{1}=\nabla_{x}u^{2}_{t_{0}}
in the support of
\gamma_{0}
.
This transforms
(
1.11
) into a forward-forward system, from which we can infer
that

(\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\gamma}^{1},\bm{u}^{1})=(\bm{\nu}^{2},\bm{\gamma}^{2},\bm{u}^{2})
.

The optimality conditions (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) are stated in Theorem (
2.7
) of Section
2
and proved in Section
6
. We emphasize that similar forms of the Pontryagin maximum principle were already known see e.g.
[
7
,
25
,
28
]
. The proof of the Jacobi condition and the rigorous version of Meta-Theorem
1.3
are given in Section
4
.

1.5.2.
Stable solutions

Before we explain the proof of the local Polyak–Lojasiewicz condition of Meta-Theorem
1.4
we need to clarify what we mean by
stable
solution. Let
\bm{\nu}^{*}
be an optimal solution for the initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
with associated curve and multiplier
(\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
. For a sequence of admissible controls
(\bm{\nu}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with associated curves and multipliers
(\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the optimality conditions (
1.10
)-(
1.11
), we can define the integrated relative entropy between the measures
\bm{\nu}^{n}
and
\bm{\nu}^{*}
:

(1.13)

\lambda^{2}_{n}:=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)dt,

where, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
\mathcal{E}(\nu^{n}_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*})
is the usual relative entropy between two probability measures, see (
1.26
).
Let us assume that
\lambda_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\lambda_{n}=0
. Then, by means of a compactness argument, we can show that weak limit points
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
of
\lambda_{n}^{-1}(\bm{\nu}^{n}-\bm{\nu},\bm{\gamma}^{n}-\bm{\gamma},\bm{u}^{n}-\bm{u})
solve the linearized equations

(1.14)

{\eta}_{t}(a)=-\frac{\nu^{*}_{t}(a)}{\epsilon}\Bigl[\bigl\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr(x,y)-c_{t}\Bigr]\quad\mbox{in }[t_{0},T]\times A,

and

(1.15)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}v_{t}-b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}=b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}&\textrm{\rm in }[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad v_{T}=0\quad\textrm{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\rho_{t}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\eta_{t})\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr)&\textrm{\rm in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad\rho_{t_{0}}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
c_{t}
, in (
1.14
), is a normalizing constant to ensure that
\eta_{t}
integrates to
0
. In the equations above,
\rho_{t}
lies in the dual of a space of differentiable functions with some growth at infinity and
\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle
denotes the corresponding duality bracket. A stable solution
\bm{\nu}^{*}
is precisely a solution to the control problem (
1.1
) for which
(0,0,0)
is the only solution to the system (
1.14
)–(
1.15
).
Of course, stability is related to second-order optimality conditions. Indeed, given a minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}
for
J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
, we always have

(1.16)

\frac{d^{2}}{dh^{2}}\Big|_{h=0}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}+h\bm{\eta}\bigr)\geq 0

for all admissible perturbations
\bm{\eta}
. For a fixed
\bm{\eta}
, we can show that equality holds in (
1.16
) if and only if there is
(\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
such that
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
solves the linearized system (
1.14
)-(
1.15
). Stable solutions are then precisely those for which

\frac{d^{2}}{dh^{2}}\Big|_{h=0}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}+h\bm{\eta}\bigr)>0

for any non-trivial (ie non-zero) admissible perturbation
\bm{\eta}
.

We introduce stable solutions in Section
2
, where we properly define
\mathcal{O}
as the set of initial conditions
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
for which there is a unique and stable global minimizer. In the same section, we state the second-order optimality conditions associated to Problem (
1.1
) in Theorem
2.39
. The proof of these conditions is postponed to Section
8
after we give an exhaustive analysis of the linearized equations appearing in (
1.15
) in Section
7
.

1.5.3.
PL inequality.

Let us now provide a sketch of proof for Meta-Theorem
1.4
when the compact set
\mathcal{K}
is just taken as a single element
\left\{(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\right\}\subset\mathcal{O}
. The detailed proof is given in Section
5
. We argue by contradiction and assume that we can find a positive sequence
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging to
0
together with a sequence of admissible controls
(\bm{\nu}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(1.17)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\lambda_{n}^{2}=0,\quad\mbox{ and}\quad\mathcal{I}((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{n})<c_{n}\Bigl(J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{n}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\Bigr),

where we used the notations introduced in (
1.13
) and (
1.9
) for
\lambda_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}
respectively.

Notice
by non-negativity of
{\mathcal{I}}

that the second equation in (
1.17
) implies that

{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\neq\bm{\nu}^{*}
and therefore
\lambda_{n}>0
, which
makes it possible to let

(1.18)

\eta_{t}^{n}:=\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}-\nu^{*}_{t}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad\rho_{t}^{n}:=\frac{\gamma^{n}_{t}-\gamma^{*}_{t}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad v_{t}^{n}:=\frac{u_{t}^{n}-u^{*}_{t}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],

where
(\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})
and
(\bm{u}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*})
are the solutions to (
1.11
) associated to
\bm{\nu}^{n}
and
\bm{\nu}^{*}
(the optimal control for
the initial condition

(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
) respectively.
By making the difference between the equations satisfied by
(\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})
and
(\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
, we get

(1.19)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}{v}_{t}^{n}-b\bigl(x,{\nu}_{t}^{n}\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}{v}_{t}^{n}=b(x,\eta_{t}^{n})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}&\textrm{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad{v}_{T}^{n}(x,y)=0&\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rho}_{t}^{n}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,{\nu}_{t}^{n}){\rho}_{t}^{n}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\eta_{t}^{n})\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr)&\textrm{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad{\rho}_{t_{0}}^{n}=0&\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

The following two observations are in order.
On the one hand, it is not too difficult to see that the right-hand side of the second equation in (
1.17
) is (at most) of order
\lambda_{n}^{2}
(see Lemma
3.4
). That is, there exists
C>0
independent of
n
such that

(1.20)

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\leq C\lambda_{n}^{2}.

On the other hand, thanks to an explicit formula for the functionnal
\mathcal{I}
given in Section
5
and to the equation satisfied by
\bm{\nu}^{*}
we can rewrite
\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)
as

(1.21)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)\\
&=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu^{*}_{t}}(a)+\lambda_{n}\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d(\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*}\rho_{t}^{n})(x,y)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt,\end{split}

and get, by triangular inequality

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu^{*}_{t}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt

\displaystyle-\lambda_{n}^{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d(\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}\rho_{t}^{n})(x,y)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu^{n}_{t}(a)dt.

Using log-Sobolev inequality, which we show in Lemma
2.11
is satisfied by
\nu_{t}^{*}
,
we can handle the first term on the right-hand side to obtain

(1.22)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{n}\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\geq C\lambda_{n}^{2}\Bigl(1-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\bigl|\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d(\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}\rho_{t}^{n})(x,y)\bigr|^{2}d\nu^{n}_{t}(a)dt\Bigr).\end{split}

The result will follow if we justify that
(\bm{v}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
vanishes to
(0,0)
(in an appropriate sense) as
n\rightarrow+\infty
. Indeed, in this case, we can hope that the integral term in the right-hand side of (
1.22
) vanishes with
n
large, thanks to (
1.20
) and (
1.17
),
which would contradict the fact that
c_{n}
tends to
0
and obtain
the desired contradiction. Therefore, the next step is to
prove that
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges toward a solution to the linearized system (
1.14
)–(
1.15
) and conclude by stability of
\bm{\nu}^{*}
that the limit
is necessarily
(0,0,0)
. While it is not too hard to see from (
1.19
) that
(\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges toward a solution to (
1.15
) (see Proposition
3.5
), it is more difficult to prove that
\bm{\eta}^{n}
converges toward a solution to (
1.14
). Indeed, the challenge is that we have no equation for
\bm{\nu}^{n}-\bm{\nu}^{*}
. Our idea is first to justify from the second equation of (
1.17
) together with (
1.20
) that

(1.23)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{n}\bigr)=0,

and then
to deduce that the right-side of (
1.21
), normalized by
\lambda_{n}^{2}
, tends to
0
. By passing to the limit inside the terms on the right-hand side of (
1.21
), we would be led to

\displaystyle 0=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\epsilon\nabla_{a}\frac{\eta_{t}}{\nu^{*}_{t}}(a)+\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)+\nabla_{a}\bigl\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle\Bigr|^{2}d\nu^{*}_{t}(a)dt.

Therefore,

\bm{\eta}
would solve (
1.14
) and then

(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
would solve the linearized system
(
1.14
)–(
1.15
). This would complete the proof. Unfortunately, passing to the limit in (
1.21
) (after normalizing by
\lambda_{n}^{2}
) is not so straightforward, and we have to proceed a little bit differently to conclude (see Section
5
for the details).

1.6.
Limitations of the Methods and Possible Extensions

All our main results rely, in one way or another, on the entropic regularization. Among other things, the resulting Gibbs form of the optimal control, see (
1.10
), is essential to prove the
injectivity
property used to derive the Jacobi condition and therefore to obtain Meta-Theorem
1.3
. We also heavily rely on this penalization to
establish the PL
inequality, the proof of Meta-Theorem
1.4
stemming from a perturbative argument for a log-Sobolev inequality. We also stress that all the quantitative results of the paper depend implicitly on the regularization parameter
\epsilon
,
though certainly not uniformly as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. Moreover, the set
\mathcal{O}
of
good
initial conditions also depends on this parameter. A perspective of research would be to understand the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
at various places in the argument. This could be done while possibly keeping a moment penalization. We also mention that the control problem exhibits some intriguing features when there is no regularization, see
Lu et al.
[
32
]
: in that case, any local minimum –whose existence is not clear in the absence of penalization– is global. This makes the regime
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
all the more interesting.

As we already mentioned, Meta-Theorem
1.3
is especially relevant to understand the convergence of the gradient descent associated to the control problem (
1.1
). The convergence was obtained in a similar setting by
[
28
]
and
[
4
]
but under certain additional structural conditions detailed in
Subsection
1.4
. As explained following the statement of Meta-Theorem
1.4
, the descent is anticipated to converge when initialized in a neighborhood of the optimal control
\bm{\nu}^{*}
, provided the initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
belongs to the set
\mathcal{O}
introduced in Meta-Theorem
1.3
. In light of the PL inequality, the costs are expected to decrease at an exponential rate along the descent. Actually, the recent results of
[
34
]
, concerning a static optimization problem over the space of probability measures, suggest that exponential convergence of the control trajectories could also be established. We leave the verification of these conjectures for future research.

We do not address generalization bounds, that is, the improvement in accuracy achieved by training the network with larger feature samples in the training set. This is very much connected to the convergence of the optimal value and of the optimal distribution of parameters when the initial data consists of an empirical measure of the form

N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{(X_{0}^{i},Y_{0}^{i})}
,
where
(X_{0}^{i},Y_{0}^{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
are independent random variables sampled from some
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, and
N\to\infty
.
In the mean-field optimal control literature, this corresponds to looking for mean-field limits and this is sometimes referred to as the
convergence problem
. When the initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
belongs to the set
\mathcal{O}
, we expect to obtain sharper rates of convergence for the minimal values as well as convergence of the control distributions. In the context of
classical
mean-field control, this problem is addressed in
[
12
]
. In our setting, we intend to tackle this problem in a future contribution.

1.7.
Organization of the Paper

The paper is organized as follows. Section

2

reviews the first- and second-order conditions associated with problem
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
). This, in particular, allows us to specify the functional spaces in which the various equations are posed. We also provide a complete definition of the notion of stable solution. However, the proofs of the results are postponed to the second part of the paper. This structure allows the reader to directly access the proofs of the main results in Sections
3
,
4
, and

5
.
Section
3
formalizes the compactness argument presented in paragraph
1.5.2
. The proof of Meta-Theorem
1.3
is given in Section
4
(including a discussion on the role of the discriminating property), with Theorem
4.1
providing a more precise version of the result. The proof of Meta-Theorem
1.4
is given in Section
5
, where the PL inequality is stated in Theorem
5.1
. Sections
6
,
7
, and
8
return to the material introduced in Section
2
. Section
6
focuses primarily on the first-order conditions for the control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
). The analysis of second-order conditions is split in two parts: the linearized equations, which serve as a preliminary step, are studied in Section
7
, while the second-order conditions themselves are derived in Section
8
.
In Section
9
, we elaborate on some of the auxiliary compactness arguments used in Section
3
. Finally, a number of auxiliary results are stated in Appendix
A
.

1.8.
Notation

Throughout the text we use the following set of notations.

Spaces of regular functions and their duals

For some integers
d_{1},d_{2}\geq 1
and some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
the space of
k
-times continuously differentiable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
. Variables in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
will be denoted by
(x,y)
. The gradient and Hessian of
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are denoted by
\nabla\varphi
and
\nabla^{2}\varphi
, while the partial gradient and Hessian with respect to the
x
variable are denoted by
\nabla_{x}\varphi
and
\nabla^{2}_{x}\varphi
. For
p\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
the subset of
\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})

consisting of functions whose growth, as well as that of their derivatives up to order
k
, is at most polynomial of order
p
, endowed with the norm

\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}:=\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\sum_{|(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta})|\leq k}\frac{\Bigl|\partial^{|(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta})|}_{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{d_{1}}^{\alpha_{d_{1}}}y_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots y_{d_{2}}^{\beta_{d_{2}}}}\varphi(x,y)\Bigr|}{1+|x|^{p}+|y|^{p}},

where
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta})=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d_{1}},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{d_{1}})\in\mathbb{N}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
, and
|(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta})|=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{d_{1}}+\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{d_{2}}
.
It will be convenient to distinguish between the growth of
\varphi
itself and that of its derivatives: for
p,q\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, we define
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
as the set of functions such that
\varphi
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and
\nabla\varphi
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{k-1}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, endowed with the norm

\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}:=\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}+\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}.

The space of
k
-times continuously differentiable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
with bounded derivatives is denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
(not to be confused with
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
defined later on) and it is endowed with the norm

\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}:=\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\sum_{|(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta})|\leq k}\Bigl|\partial^{|(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta})|}_{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{d_{1}}^{\alpha_{d_{1}}}y_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots y_{d_{2}}^{\beta_{d_{2}}}}\varphi(x,y)\Bigr|.

We also use the notation
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{1,b}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
for
the space of functions of linear growth with bounded derivatives of order
1\leq j\leq k
. The associated norm is denoted by
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{1,b}}
.
The subset of
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
consisting of functions that, together with all their partial derivatives up to order
k
, vanish at infinity is denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
. It is endowed with the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}:=\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})}.

The topological dual spaces are denoted with a “
*
” as in
(\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))^{*}
,
(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))^{*}
, etc… They are implicitly endowed with the dual norms and the corresponding duality brackets are generaly denoted by
\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle
.

When the underlying space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
is clear from context, we omit it in notations such as
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p}
,
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q}
,
(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p})^{*}
,
(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q})^{*}
… When
k=0
, we omit the superscript and simply write
\mathcal{C}_{b}
,
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
\mathcal{C}_{p}
…

The control space
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
is denoted by
A
, and similarly, we define
\mathcal{C}^{k}(A)
,
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{0}(A)
,
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{b}(A)
,
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p}(A)
and
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p,q}(A)
, along with their corresponding norms and dual spaces.

The following lemma is used regularly throughout the paper. Its understanding at this stage is not essential. The proof is left as an exercise to the reader and relies essentially on the definitions of the various spaces and duality brackets introduced above. In this respect, the statement itself illustrates the repeated use of these notations throughout the rest of the paper. Thanks to
Assumption (
Regularity
)
(for the vector field
b:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
)
and with
\vee

being the usual notation for the maximum of two real numbers, we have

Lemma 1.5
.

For
k\geq 0
and
p\geq q\geq 0
with
k+q\leq 4
, there exists a constant
C_{b}
, depending on the vector field
b
and
k,p
and
q
, such that, for
\rho^{i}\in(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p})^{*}
and
\varphi^{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\nabla_{x}\varphi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p-q}
, for
i=1,2
, and with the notation
f^{i}(a):=\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi^{i};\rho^{i}\rangle
for all
a\in A
, it holds

(1.24)

\left\|f^{i}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k+1}(A)}\leq C_{b}\left\|\nabla_{x}\varphi^{i}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p-q}}\left\|\rho^{i}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{k}_{p})^{*}}.

Now, for
k_{1},k_{2},q\geq 0
and
p_{1},p_{2}\geq q
with
k_{1}\vee k_{2}+q\leq 4
, there exists a constant
C_{b}>0
, depending on
b
and
k_{1},k_{2},q,p_{1},p_{2}
, such that, for
\nabla_{x}\varphi^{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{k_{2}}_{p_{2}-q}\cap\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}}_{p_{1}-q}
and
\rho^{i}\in(\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}}_{p_{1}})^{*}\cap(\mathcal{C}^{k_{2}}_{p_{2}})^{*}
, for
i=1,2
,

(1.25)

\begin{split}&\left\|f^{2}-f^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{q}_{k_{1}\vee k_{2}+1}(A)}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C_{b}\Bigl(\left\|\nabla_{x}\varphi^{2}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k_{2}}_{p_{2}-q}}+\left\|\rho^{1}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}}_{p_{1}})^{*}}\Bigr)\Bigl(\left\|\rho^{2}-\rho^{1}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{k_{2}}_{p_{2}})^{*}}+\left\|\nabla_{x}\varphi^{2}-\nabla_{x}\varphi^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k_{1}}_{p_{1}-q}}\Bigr).\end{split}

Measure theory

We identify
(\mathcal{C}_{0}(A))^{*}
with the set of finite Radon measures on
A
, denoted by
\mathcal{M}(A)
. The subset of positive Radon measures is denoted by
\mathcal{M}^{+}(A)
, and the subspace of finite Radon measures
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(A)
such that
(1+|a|^{k})\nu
belongs to
\mathcal{M}(A)

is denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{(1+|\cdot|^{k})}(A)
and endowed with the norm

\left\|\nu\right\|_{\mathcal{M}_{(1+|\cdot|^{k})}:=\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{C}(A),|\varphi(a)|\leq(1+|a|^{k})}}\int_{A}\varphi(a)d\nu(a)=\int_{A}(1+|a|^{k})d|\nu|(a),

where
|\nu|

is the total variation of the (finite) measure

\nu
.

For
p\geq 1
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{p}(A)
the space of Borel probability measures on
A
with finite moment of order
p
(so that

{\mathcal{P}}_{p}(A)\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{(1+|\cdot|^{p})}(A)
), endowed with the Monge-Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance of order
p

d_{p}(\mu,\nu)^{p}:=\inf_{\pi\in\Gamma(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|x-y|^{p}d\pi(x,y),

where
\Gamma(\mu,\nu)\subset\mathcal{P}_{p}(A^{2})
is the subset of couplings between
\mu
and
\nu
.
As usual, the space of probability measures on
A
(without any integrability condition) is denoted by
{\mathcal{P}}(A)
.
We define similarly
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
(and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
).

For two probability measures
\nu,\widetilde{\nu}
on some Polish space
E
, we define the relative entropy of
\nu
with respect to
\widetilde{\nu}
by

(1.26)

\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu|\widetilde{\nu}\bigr):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\int_{E}\log\frac{d\nu}{d\widetilde{\nu}}d\nu&\mbox{ if }\nu\mbox{ is absolutely continuous with respect to }\widetilde{\nu},\\
+\infty&\mbox{ otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

where
\frac{d\nu}{d\widetilde{\nu}}
denotes the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\nu
with respect to
\widetilde{\nu}
. When a probability measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(A)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure over
A
, we denote its density by the same symbol
\nu
and we write
\nu(a)da
for
d\nu(a)
.

In the same spirit of simplifying notation, we will often write
b(x,\nu)
instead of the full expression
\int_{A}b(x,a)\,d\nu(a)
.

Families indexed by a time parameter.

For some time interval
[t_{0},T]
and some metric space
(E,d_{E})
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],E)
the space of continuous curves from
[t_{0},T]
to
E
endowed with the metric

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{E}(e^{1}_{t},e^{2}_{t}),\quad\quad\mbox{for}\hskip 5.0pt\bm{e}^{1}=(e^{1}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]},\ \bm{e}^{2}=(e^{2}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],E).

Throughout, we use
\bm{bold}
characters to denote family of functions parametrized by the time parameter
t
. We also define
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}([t_{0},T],E)
, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the subset of
\alpha
-Hölder continuous curves. We denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}([t_{0},T],E)
the set of bounded functions from
[t_{0},T]
to
E
(without any measurability condition).

Additional functional spaces.

Throughout, the text we introduce several additional functional spaces. For some
t_{0}\in[0,T]
, the spaces of controls and control perturbations
\mathcal{A}(t_{0}),\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})
are given respectively in Definitions
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.17
. Similarly, the space
\mathcal{R}(t_{0})
of solutions to the linearized continuity equation in (
1.15
) is given in Definition
2.18
.

2.
Optimality Conditions and Stable Solutions

In this section, we introduce the first and second-order necessary conditions
satisfied by any minimizer
of
(
1.1
)
and then define properly the notion of
stable minimizer. Even though these
preliminary
results play a fundamental role in the rest of the paper, we feel it sufficient to postpone their proofs
to Sections
6
,
7
and
8
.

2.1.
Admissible Controls and Existence of a Minimizer

We begin by clarifying the definition of the control problem
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
and in particular the choice of the admissible controls.
We consider controls
{\bm{\nu}}
as trajectories taking values in
{\mathcal{P}}(A)
, with finite mean relative entropy with respect to the Gibbs density associated with the potential
\ell
:

Definition 2.1
.

For a fixed initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T]
,

{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
is defined as the collection of
measurable mappings
{\bm{\nu}}:t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu_{t}\in{\mathcal{P}}(A)
such that

(2.1)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt<+\infty,

where
\nu^{\infty}
is the log-concave probability defined in (
1.6
).

The notion of measurability used for the mapping
{\bm{\nu}}
is explained in Remark
2.3
.
Moreover,
observe that the entropy penalization can be rewritten as the sum of a, somehow standard, moment penalization and the entropy with respect to the Lebesgue measure

(2.2)

\mathcal{E}(\nu|\nu^{\infty})=\int_{A}\ell(a)d\nu(a)+\int_{A}\log\nu(a)d\nu(a)-\log\int_{A}e^{-\ell(a)}da.

In fact, we will prove in Lemma
6.2

that there
exists a constant
C>0
such that, for any
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
,

(2.3)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt+\sup_{t_{0}\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T}\biggl\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}|a|^{2}d\nu_{t}(a)dt\biggr\}\leq C\biggl(1+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt\biggr).

This motivates the introduction of the following bigger vector space that will contain any linear combinations of elements of
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
. This will become especially relevant to state the second-order optimality conditions of Subsection
2.4
and to define the stable solutions in Subsection
2.5
.

Definition 2.2
.

We call
{\mathcal{D}}(t_{0})
the set of measurable maps
\bm{\nu}:t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu_{t}\in\mathcal{M}(A)
(where
\mathcal{M}(A)
is the set of finite measures over
A
) such that

(2.4)

\left\|\bm{\nu}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}:=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{4})d|\nu_{t}|(a)dt+\sup_{t_{2}>t_{1}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d|\nu_{t}|(a)dt<+\infty.

Remark 2.3
.

By contrast with elements of
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
, elements of
\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
are neither required to be positive nor to have unit mass for all
t
.
Measurability of the mapping
{\bm{\nu}}

is understood in the sense that the map

t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\int_{A}fd\nu_{t}

is measurable for every
f\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(A)
. Using the representation
of the positive part

\nu_{t}^{+}(E)=\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(A)}\Bigl\{\int_{A}fd\nu_{t};\quad 0\leq f\leq{\mathrm{1}}_{E}\Bigr\},\quad E\in{\mathcal{B}}(A),

together with the separability of
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(A)
, this implies that, for any Borel subset
E
of
A
, the mapping
t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu_{t}^{+}(E)
is measurable, and similarly for
\nu_{t}^{-}
. The converse is true in the sense that any mapping
{\bm{\nu}}
, such that
t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu_{t}^{\pm}(E)

is measurable
for any Borel subset
E
of
A
, is
measurable in the former sense.
When the arrival space of
{\bm{\nu}}
is
restricted to
{\mathcal{P}}(A)
(as in the case in Definition (
2.1
)),
measurability can be viewed either by equipping

{\mathcal{P}}(A)
with
the Borel
\sigma
-field induced by the weak convergence topology (i.e., when probability measures are tested with respect to continuous and bounded functions on
A
)
or by requiring

t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu_{t}(E)
to be measurable for any
E\in{\mathcal{B}}(A)
.
Both are equivalent,
see
[
5
, Proposition 7.25]
.

Back to the setting of Definition
2.2
,
the mapping
t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto|\nu_{t}|=\nu_{t}^{+}+\nu_{t}^{-}\in{\mathcal{M}}(A)
is measurable. This guarantees that
the norm in
(
2.4
) is well-defined. Equivalently,

{\bm{\nu}}
can be regarded as a finite measure on

[t_{0},T]\times A
defined by

{\bm{\nu}}(I\times B)=\int_{I}\nu_{t}(B)dt,

for any two Borel subsets
I
and
B
of
[t_{0},T]
and
A
respectively.
The measure
{\bm{\nu}}
has the Lebesgue measure as first marginal on
[t_{0},T]

and
the collection

(\nu_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
can be regarded as the kernel
resulting from the disintegration of
{\bm{\nu}}

with respect to
{\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}
.
We sometimes write

{\bm{\nu}}={\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\otimes(\nu_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
,
or
d\nu(t,a)=d\nu_{t}(a)dt
.
In the framework of Definition
2.1
,
this point of view permits to
regard

\bm{\nu}
as an element of

(T-t_{0})\cdot{\mathcal{P}}([t_{0},T]\times A)

and then to
interpret
(
2.1
) as a relative entropy on the wider space

[t_{0},T]\times A
. Indeed, by
[
9
, Corollary 2.7]
(with the definition of the relative entropy being extended in a trivial way to positive measures with the same mass),

(2.5)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt={\mathcal{E}}\bigl({\bm{\nu}}|{\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\times\nu^{\infty}\bigr),

which provides another interpretation of the cost
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)

as the sum of the original regression cost and a time-space entropic penalty.

Lastly, note for future reference that, up to a redefinition of the mapping
t\mapsto\nu_{t}
on a Lebesgue-negligible subset of
[t_{0},T]
, we may always assume that
\nu_{t}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{1+|a|^{4}}(A)
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
–that is, the measure
(1+|a|^{4})\nu_{t}
is finite on
A
.

For any element
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
(so, in particular, for any admissible control in
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
), it follows from
Assumption (
Regularity
)
that the velocity field
(t,x)\mapsto b(x,\nu_{t})
in
the continuity equation
(
1.3
) is well-defined and three times continuously
differentiable in the variable
x
, with

(2.6)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left\|b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{b}}dt+\sup_{t_{1}<t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left\|b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{b}}dt\leq C\left\|\bm{\nu}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})},

where we recall the convenient notation
b(x,\nu)=\int_{A}b(x,a)d\nu(a)
for any
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(A)
.
Estimate (
2.6
) makes it possible to solve the continuity equation (
1.3
). For
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
, we say that
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))
is a solution to (
1.3
) if, for all test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{b}([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
(i.e., the function and its first time and space derivatives are continuous and bounded) and all
t_{1}\in[t_{0},T]
,

(2.7)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{t_{1}}(x,y)d\gamma_{t_{1}}(x,y)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{t_{0}}(x,y)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\\
&+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl\{\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(x,y)+b(x,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)\bigr\}d\gamma_{t}(x,y)dt.\end{split}

Thanks to the regularity of the vector field driving the equation, well-posedness of the continuity equation is standard. The solution is obtained by pushing the initial condition
\gamma_{0}
along the flow of the ODE (
1.5
).
In Appendix
A.1
, we gather a sequence of statements leading to the following result:

Proposition 2.4
.

Take
{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
. Then, for any given
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, there is a unique distributional solution
{\bm{\gamma}}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))
to the continuity equation

(2.8)

\partial_{t}\gamma_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}(b(x,\nu_{t})\gamma_{t})=0\quad\mbox{ in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\quad\gamma(t_{0})=\gamma_{0}.

It is given by

(\gamma_{t}=(X_{t}^{t_{0},\cdot},i_{d})\#\gamma_{0})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

where
(X_{t}^{t_{0},\cdot})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

is the flow of
(
1.5
), solution to

\dot{X}^{t_{0},x}_{t}=b(X_{t}^{t_{0},x},\nu_{t})\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad X_{t_{0}}^{t_{0},x}=x.

Moreover, there exists a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, independent of
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and

{\bm{\nu}}
, such that, for each
p\in[1,3]
such that
\gamma_{0}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
,

(2.9)

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}\bigr)^{p/2}d\gamma_{t}(x,y)}{1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}\bigr)^{p/2}d\gamma_{0}(x,y)}+\sup_{t\neq s\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{d_{p}(\gamma_{t},\gamma_{s})}{\sqrt{|t-s|}}\leq\Lambda\bigl(\left\|\bm{\nu}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}\bigr).

By an approximation argument, we can prove that, whenever
\gamma_{0}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
for some
p\geq 2
, then equation (
2.7
) is satisfied for any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
such that
\varphi,\partial_{t}\varphi
and
\nabla_{x}\varphi
have growth of order
p
in
(x,y)
at infinity.

Notice that, since
\gamma_{0}
lies in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
then, for all
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
,
\gamma_{T}
belongs to

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
which guarantees in the end that the total cost
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})
is finite.

The control problem
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
is studied in an exhaustive manner in
Section
6
.
Results can be summarized as follows:

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
.
Then, for any
M\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the sub-level set

\{{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0}):J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})\leq M\}
is relatively compact for the weak topology on

(T-t_{0})\cdot{\mathcal{P}}([t_{0},T]\times A)

and the functional
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})\mapsto J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})
is lower-semicontinuous for
the weak topology (cf. Remark
2.3
for the definition of the latter). In particular, the optimal control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) has at least one solution (i.e., one minimizer) in

{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
.

2.2.
First-Order Condition and Consequences

We now address the first-order condition associated with the control problem
(
1.1
)–(
1.2
).
We start with the following definition: For
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{D}}(t_{0})
, we call equation adjoint to
{\bm{\gamma}}
(at least, when
{\bm{\nu}}
is the control driving
\bm{\gamma}
)
the (backward) transport
equation

(2.10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}{u}_{t}(x,y)-b\bigl(x,{\nu}_{t}\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}{u}_{t}(x,y)=0,&\quad(t,x,y)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad{u}_{T}(x,y)=L(x,y),&\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Solvability of equation
(
2.10
) is
addressed
in the space

\mathcal{C}^{1/2}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})
. We say that
{\bm{u}}=(u_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}\in\mathcal{C}^{1/2}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})
is a solution to the transport equation if, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
and all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,

u_{t}(x,y)=L(x,y)+\int_{t}^{T}b(x,\nu_{s})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{s}(x,y)ds.

Similarly to Proposition
2.4
, the following result
is presented in
Appendix
A.1
:

Proposition 2.6
.

Take
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
.
Then, there exists a unique solution
{\bm{u}}=(u_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}\in\mathcal{C}^{1/2}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})
to the transport equation (
2.10
). Moreover, for every
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
the mapping
(x,y)\mapsto u_{t}(x,y)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and there exists a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, independent of
t_{0}
and
{\bm{\nu}}
, such that

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|u_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}_{2,1}}+\sup_{t_{2}>t_{1}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{\left\|u_{t_{2}}-u_{t_{1}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\leq\Lambda\bigl(\left\|\bm{\nu}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}\bigr).

By
(
2.3
), the above bound remains true for
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
if one replaces the right-hand side by

\Lambda(\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt)

(for a possibly different choice of the function
\Lambda
).

The following
result provides the form
of the
forward-backward
system satisfied at optimality.
A similar result can be found in

[
25
,
28
]
:

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
.
Then,
any minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})

to

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
admits a jointly continuous density (still denoted
(t,a)\mapsto\nu^{*}_{t}(a)
) given, for all
(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A
, by

(2.11)

\nu^{*}_{t}(a)=\frac{1}{z^{*}_{t}}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\Bigr),

where
z^{*}_{t}
is a normalizing constant,
\bm{\gamma}^{*}=(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

is the solution to the continuity equation
(
1.3
) starting from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})

and driven by

\bm{\nu}^{*}
,
and
\bm{u}^{*}=(u^{*}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

solves the transport equation
(
2.10
)
driven by

\bm{\nu}^{*}
.

In particular, the pair
(\bm{u}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*})
solves the forward-backward system

(2.12)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)-b\bigl(x,\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)=0,&\quad(t,x,y)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad u^{*}_{T}(x,y)=L(x,y),&\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\gamma^{*}_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr)=0,&\quad\textrm{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad\gamma^{*}_{t_{0}}=\gamma_{0}&\quad\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Remark 2.8
.

Theorem
2.7
is proven in
Subsection
6.2
. At this stage,
the following remarks are in order:

(i)

Although the optimization problem is taken over the space of measures
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
we will often identify an optimal solution
\bm{\nu}^{*}
with its continuous density given by (
2.11
).

(ii)

Notice that
z^{*}_{t}

in (
2.11
)
is a normalization constant that
guarantees that
\nu^{*}_{t}(A)=1
, namely

z^{*}_{t}

is given, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
, by

(2.13)

z^{*}_{t}=\int_{A}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\Bigr)da.

By (i) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
and because
\gamma^{*}_{t}
belongs to
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})

and

\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}
has linear growth in
(x,y)
,
the integral inside the exponential is finite
and less than
C(1+|a|)

for a constant
C
independent of
t
.

(iii)

The probability measure
\nu^{*}_{t}

is defined as a Gibbs measure (which we may compare to

\nu^{\infty}
in (
2.1
)). Equivalently,
it is the unique
density solving the stationary Fokker-Planck equation

(2.14)

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta_{a}\nu_{t}(a)-\operatorname{div}_{a}\Bigl[\Bigl(\epsilon\nabla_{a}\ell(a)+\nabla_{a}\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\Bigr)\Bigr)\nu_{t}(a)\Bigr]=0,\ a\in A.

(iv)

The reader will easily recognize that

\bm{\gamma}^{*}=(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

in
(
2.7
)
is the optimal curve associated
with the optimal control
{\bm{\nu}}^{*}
. The function

\bm{u}^{*}=(u^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
is referred to as the adjoint trajectory or as the multiplier.

From the study of the system (
2.11
)-
(
2.12
), we are able to establish further regularity properties
of the optimal control

\bm{\nu}^{*}
and the multiplier
\bm{u}^{*}
. In particular optimal solutions
\bm{\nu}^{*}
are continuous in time (by contrast with generic elements of
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
).
The following result is proven in Subsection

6.3
:

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
and assume that
({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\gamma}},{\bm{u}})
is a solution to the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
). Then,
u
has the following regularity

(2.15)

\displaystyle t\mapsto u_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2}\mbox{ is bounded and }t\mapsto u_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}\mbox{ is continuous},

(2.16)

\displaystyle t\mapsto\partial_{t}u,\nabla_{x}u\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}\mbox{ are bounded and }t\mapsto\partial_{t}u{{}_{t}},\nabla_{x}u{{}_{t}}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}\mbox{ are continuous. }

Moreover, there exists a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow[1,+\infty)
, independent of
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and

({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\gamma}},{\bm{u}})
, such that, for all
(t,a)
in
[t_{0},T]\times A
, we have the exponential bounds

(2.17)

\Lambda^{-1}_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}e^{-2\ell(a)}\leq\nu_{t}(a)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}e^{-\ell(a)/2},

as well as the time regularity

(2.18)

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)+\sup_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{\bigl\|e^{3\ell/4}(\nu_{t_{2}}-\nu_{t_{1}})\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}{|t_{2}-t_{1}]}+\sup_{t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t_{2}}|\nu_{t_{1}}\bigr)}{|t_{2}-t_{1}|}\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}},

where we wrote for simplicity

\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}:=\Lambda\biggl(\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|+|y|)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr).

Remark 2.10
.

The proofs leading to our main results will rely on some compactness arguments, requiring careful tracking of how the quantitative estimates depend on the initial point
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and on the control
\bm{\nu}
. This is the purpose of these additional functions
\Lambda
appearing throughout the text. Since all the
estimates
used in these compactness arguments will be uniform in
the parameter
t_{0}
, we consistently omit it in notations such as
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}
.

We recall
from (
2.3
)
that

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt
can be bounded by

C(1+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt)
.
Notice also that we have not put
a
star
on

({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\gamma}},{\bm{u}})
because, at this stage,

{\bm{\nu}}
may
not be a minimizer of

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
. We show in Lemma
6.4
that,
when
\bm{\nu}^{*}
is optimal is for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
,
\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt
can be bounded by
C(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y))
for some
C>0
independent of
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
. Then, the non-decreasing function
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}
provided by Proposition
2.9
can be replaced by a non-decreasing function of
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)
only.

From Theorem
2.7
, we can also deduce that the measure
\nu_{t}^{*}
satisfies, for all
t
in
[t_{0},T]
, some functional inequalities. More generally, for any element
\bm{\nu}
of
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
, let us define the associated element
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}]
in
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
by, for all
t
in
[t_{0},T]
,

(2.19)

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}]\propto\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\Bigr),

where
(\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
is the solution to

(2.20)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}u_{t}(x,y)-b\bigl(x,\nu_{t}\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)=0,&\quad(t,x,y)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad u_{T}(x,y)=L(x,y),&\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\gamma_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu_{t})\gamma_{t}\bigr)=0,&\quad\textrm{\rm in}\ [t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad\gamma_{t_{0}}=\gamma_{0}&\quad\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Because the two equations are completely decoupled, there is no difficulty in solving each of them, see Propositions
2.4
and
2.6
. The next result, proven in Section
6.3
, states that
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}]
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality. Notice that the result applies in particular to any solution of (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) since, in this case,
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}]
is equal to
\bm{\nu}.

Lemma 2.11
.

Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})

and
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
. Then, there exists a constant

C_{\rm LSI}(\Gamma[\bm{\nu}])>0
such that, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
and every smooth enough
f:A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
\int_{A}f(a)d\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}](a)=1
, it holds

(2.21)

\int_{A}f(a)\log\bigl(f(a)\bigr)d\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}](a)\leq C_{\rm LSI}(\Gamma[\bm{\nu}])\int_{A}|\nabla_{a}\log f(a)|^{2}f(a)d\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}](a).

Moreover, there exists a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
independent from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and
\bm{\nu}
such that
