C_{\rm LSI}(\Gamma[\bm{\nu}])\leq\Lambda\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}\bigr)^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}(x,y)+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt\Bigr).

Remark 2.12
.

It is important in the statement of Lemma
2.11
that the initial measure has a bounded third-order moment. In short, our strategy to obtain the log-Sobolev inequality relies on a perturbation argument, deriving it from the inequality satisfied by the reference measure
\nu^{\infty}\propto e^{-\ell}
. Due to the linear growth of
b(x,a)
in
a
, simpler proofs based on the Holley-Stroock lemma do not apply.
In particular, we need to control the Hessian of the mapping

a\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)

which ultimately requires ensuring that the term

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(1+|x|^{2})|\nabla_{x}L(x,y)|\,d\gamma_{0}(x,y)

is bounded.
Because the terminal cost
L
is allowed to have quadratic growth, we thus require
\gamma_{0}
to belong to
\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
.

In the same vein, we use the exponential bound (
2.17
) to prove, in Section
6.3
, the following inequality, which relies on a generalization of Pinsker’s inequality, see
[
6
]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
. Then, there exists a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}
of
J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot\bigr)
and any
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
,

\left\|\bm{\nu}-\bm{\nu}^{*}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}\leq\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}\biggl(\sqrt{\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*})dt}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*})dt\biggr),

where
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}
is short-hand notation for

\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}=\Lambda\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\Bigr).

Another consequence of Theorem
2.7
is that we can derive some stability properties for the minimal value in the control problem as a function of the initial point
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
. If we define the value function
U:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

by

(2.22)

U(t_{0},\gamma_{0}):=\inf_{\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}\bigr),

then we have the following result, also proven in Section
6.3
.

Proposition 2.14
.

U
is locally Lipschitz continuous over
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
. More precisely, there is a non decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any
(t_{1},\gamma_{1}),(t_{2},\gamma_{2})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, it holds

\bigl|U(t_{2},\gamma_{2})-U(t_{1},\gamma_{1})\bigr|\leq\sup_{i=1,2}\Lambda\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{i}(x,y)\biggr)\bigl(|t_{2}-t_{1}|+d_{2}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\bigr).

The estimates from Proposition
2.9
together with Proposition
2.14
also make it
possible to obtain the following stability
result by means of a compactness argument
(whose proof is given in Subsection

6.3
).

Lemma 2.15
.

Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
such that

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)

has a unique minimizer

\bm{\nu}^{*}
. Denote by
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
and
\bm{u}^{*}
the corresponding curve and multiplier. Assume that
(t^{n}_{0},\gamma^{n}_{0})_{n\geq 1}

converges in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
toward
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and take, for all
n\geq 1
, a minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
of
J((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot)
with corresponding curve and multiplier
\bm{\gamma}^{*,n}
and
\bm{u}^{*,n}
. Then
(\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})_{n\geq 1}
converges toward
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
in the following sense (with the standard notation
t\vee t^{\prime}
for
\max(t,t^{\prime})
):

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\biggl[\sup_{t\in[t^{n}_{0}\vee t_{0},T]}\Bigl\{\left\|e^{\ell/2}(\nu^{*,n}_{t}-\nu^{*}_{t})\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{*,n}|\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)+\bigl\|u_{t}^{*,n}-u^{*}_{t}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2}}+d_{2}\bigl(\gamma_{t}^{*,n},\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr)\Bigr\}\biggr]=0.

2.3.
Lagrangian Representation

We now introduce an alternative formulation of the optimality conditions
stated in Theorem

2.7
, but in terms of
a forward-backward system of two Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs). This representation is easily derived from Lemmas

2.4

and
A.13
:

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})

and consider also a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
equipped with a pair
(X_{0},Y_{0})

of random variables with values in

{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}

such that
{\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{0},Y_{0})^{-1}=\gamma_{0}
. Then,
any triple

({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\gamma}},{\bm{u}})
solving the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
can be represented by means of
the forward-backward system of ODEs

(2.23)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{X}_{t}=b(X_{t},\nu_{t}),&t\in[t_{0},T];\qquad X_{t_{0}}=X_{0};\\
\dot{Z}_{t}=-\nabla_{x}b(X_{t},\nu_{t})Z_{t},&t\in[t_{0},T];\qquad Z_{T}=\nabla_{x}L(X_{T},Y_{0});\end{array}\right.

with
\mathbb{E}
denoting the expectation under
\mathbb{P}
, in the sense that

(2.24)

\forall(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A,\quad\nu_{t}(a)=\frac{1}{z_{t}}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{t},a)\cdot Z_{t}\bigr]\Bigr),

and

(2.25)

\begin{split}&\forall t\in[t_{0},T],\quad{\gamma}_{t}={\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{t},Y_{0})^{-1},\\
&{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\bigl\{\forall t\in[t_{0},T],\quad Z_{t}=\nabla_{x}{u}_{t}(X_{t},Y_{0})\bigr\}\bigr)=1.\end{split}

Notice that (
2.24
)–(
2.23
) read as the optimality conditions when the cost functional is formulated over controlled ODEs instead of
controlled continuity equations. In this case, the problem (
1.1
) is replaced by

\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})}\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[L(X_{T},Y_{0})\bigr]+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt\Bigr\},

where
X_{t}
solves the ODE

\dot{X}_{t}=b(X_{t},\nu_{t}),\quad t\in[t_{0},T];\quad{\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{0},Y_{0})^{-1}=\gamma_{0}.

2.4.
Second-Order Optimality Conditions

We now address second-order optimality conditions, the analysis of which requires an appropriate set of control perturbations:

Definition 2.17
.

For an
initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T]
, we let
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})
(the superscript
\ell
standing for
linearized
) be the subspace of
\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
consisting of elements
\bm{\eta}
such that, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
\eta_{t}(A)=0
.

The key idea in the analysis of the second-order conditions is to associate, with any optimal control, a linearized problem. For an initial condition
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
, an element
\bm{\nu}
of
\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
with
(\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
as associated curve and multiplier, and another element
{\bm{\eta}}\in{\mathcal{D}}(t_{0})
, we consider the advection equation

(2.26)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu_{t})\rho_{t}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\eta_{t})\gamma_{t}\bigr)&\quad\mbox{in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\rho_{t_{0}}=0,&\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Equation (
2.26
) is obtained by differentiating with respect to
\lambda
the solution
\bm{\gamma}^{\lambda}
to the continuity equation (
1.3
) with control
\bm{\nu}+\lambda\bm{\eta}
.
To make sense of equation (
2.26
), we will use test functions
\varphi:[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(2.27)

\displaystyle t\mapsto\varphi_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2}\mbox{ is bounded and }r\mapsto\varphi_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}\mbox{ is continuous},

(2.28)

\displaystyle t\mapsto\partial_{t}\varphi_{t},\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}\mbox{ are bounded and }t\mapsto\partial_{t}\varphi_{t},\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}\mbox{ are continuous. }

We show in Lemma
7.4
that, for any such test function
\bm{\varphi}
and any
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*})
satisfying
\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2},\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}\langle\varphi;\rho_{t}\rangle<+\infty
, the maps
t\mapsto\langle\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle
and
t\mapsto\langle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle
are continuous. Moreover, by Proposition
7.1
, the map
t\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle
is integrable and coincides with
t\mapsto\int_{A}\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle d\nu_{t}(a)
.

Then, we say that

(2.29)

\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*})\quad\mbox{ with }\quad\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2},\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}\langle\varphi;\rho_{t}\rangle<+\infty,

is solution to (
2.26
) if, for every test function
\varphi:[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
2.27
)–(
2.28
), it holds, for any
t_{1}\in[t_{0},T]
,

(2.30)

\langle\varphi_{t_{1}};\rho_{t_{1}}\rangle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\langle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)dt.

Solvability of this equation is studied in the smaller space

{\mathcal{R}}(t_{0})
defined below:

Definition 2.18
.

For
t_{0}\in[0,T]
, we define
\mathcal{R}(t_{0})
as the subset of
\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*})
consisting of elements
\bm{\rho}
such that

\left\|\bm{\rho}\right\|_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0})}:=\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi,\rho_{t}\rangle|+\sup_{t_{1}<t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{\left\|\rho_{t_{2}}-\rho_{t_{1}}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}<+\infty.

The following result is proven in Section
7
.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and
\bm{\nu},\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
. Let
\bm{\gamma}
be the solution to the continuity equation (
2.8
) starting from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
with control
\bm{\nu}
. Then, there is a unique solution
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{R}(t_{0})
to the linearized equation (
2.26
). It is given by

(2.31)

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda=0}\bm{\gamma}^{\lambda}=\bm{\rho}\quad\mbox{\rm in }\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}),

where
\bm{\gamma}^{\lambda}
is the solution to the continuity equation starting from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
with control
\bm{\nu}+\lambda\bm{\eta}
. It satisfies the estimate

(2.32)

\left\|\bm{\rho}\right\|_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0})}\leq\Lambda\biggl(\left\|\bm{\nu}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr)\left\|\bm{\eta}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})},

for some non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
independent from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0},\bm{\nu},\bm{\eta})
. Moreover, for all
t_{1}\in[t_{0},T]
,
\rho_{t_{1}}
extends uniquely to
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}
and it is given, for any
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}
by

(2.33)

\langle\phi;\rho_{t_{1}}\rangle=\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\phi(x,y)d\gamma_{t_{1}}^{\lambda}(x,y)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)dt,

where
\varphi:[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the solution to

-\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}-b(x,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}=0\quad\mbox{\rm in }[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\quad\varphi_{t_{1}}=\phi\quad\mbox{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.

Remark 2.20
.

In the proposition above, the meaning of condition (
2.31
) is

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|\frac{\gamma_{t}^{\lambda}-\gamma_{t}}{\lambda}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}=0.

If
\bm{\nu}^{*}
is an optimal control for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
and
\bm{\eta}
is a perturbation in
\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})
, we can now define the
auxiliary cost functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\eta}\bigr)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\frac{|\eta_{t}(a)|^{2}}{\nu^{*}_{t}(a)}dadt+2\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl\langle b(\cdot,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t};\rho_{t}\Bigr\rangle dt&\mbox{if }\bm{\eta}\in L^{2}\bigl((\bm{\nu}^{*})^{-1}\bigr),\\
+\infty&\mbox{otherwise, }\end{array}\right.

where, in the first line,

\bm{\rho}

solves
(
2.26
)
and
the condition

\bm{\eta}\in L^{2}((\bm{\nu}^{*})^{-1})
means that
\bm{\eta}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
[t_{0},T]\times A
with the Radon-Nikodym derivative

(t,a)\mapsto\eta_{t}(a)
belonging to
L^{2}((\bm{\nu}^{*})^{-1})
. Notice that, for any
\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})
, the second term in the right-hand side of (
2.4
) is finite, see Proposition
7.1
, and therefore the total cost
\mathcal{J}
is defined without ambiguity with values in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
.
The relation of the functional
\mathcal{J}
to the control problem will become apparent in Section
8
. In brief, for any perturbation
\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})
such that
\bm{\nu}+\lambda\bm{\eta}^{*}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
for
\lambda
small enough, we have

\frac{d^{2}}{d\lambda^{2}}\Big|_{\lambda=0}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}+\lambda\bm{\eta}\bigr)=\mathcal{J}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\eta}\bigr).

Optimality conditions for minimizers of (
2.4
) will involve an adjoint variable, solution to the linearized backward transport equation

(2.36)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\partial_{t}v_{t}(x,y)-b(x,\nu_{t}^{*})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)=b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*}(x,y)\quad\mbox{ in }[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
v_{T}(x,y)=0\quad\mbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

We say that
v\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
is a solution to (
2.36
) if, for all
t_{1}<t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]
and all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,

v_{t_{1}}(x,y)-v_{t_{2}}(x,y)=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\bigl\{b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)+b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)\bigr\}dt.

Section
7.2
is devoted to the analysis of Equation (
2.36
), where we state in particular the next result:

Proposition 2.21
.

Take
t_{0}\in[0,T]
,
\bm{\nu}^{*}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
with associated solution
\bm{u}^{*}
to the backward transport equation (
2.10
). Take
\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
. Then, there is a unique solution to the linearized transport equation (
2.36
). For all
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
v_{t}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{2}
and we have the estimate

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|v_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}}+\sup_{t_{1}<t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{\left\|v_{t_{2}}-v_{t_{1}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\leq\Lambda\bigl(\left\|\bm{\nu}^{*}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}\bigr)\left\|\bm{\eta}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})},

for some non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
independent from
(t_{0},\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\eta})
. Moreover, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
and all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, the solution is given by

(2.37)

v_{t}(x,y)=\int_{t}^{T}b\bigl(X_{s}^{*,t,x},\eta_{s})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{s}\bigl(X_{s}^{*,t,x},y\bigr)ds,

where
(X_{s}^{t,x})_{s\in[t,T]}
is the flow of the ODE, solution to

\dot{X}^{*,t,x}_{s}=b(X_{s}^{*,t,x},\nu^{*}_{s})\quad s\in[t,T],\quad X_{t}^{*,t,x}=x.

Within this framework, the second-order optimality conditions, whose proof is given in Section
8
, take the following form:

Theorem 2.22
.

Given

(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
,
let
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
be a minimizer of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
(completed with its optimal curve and
multiplier) and
t_{1}\in[t_{0},T)
. Then, for any
{\bm{\eta}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{1})
with associated solution
{\bm{\rho}}\in\mathcal{R}(t_{1})
to the linearized continuity equation (
2.26
),

\mathcal{J}\bigl((t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}}),\bm{\nu}^{*},{\bm{\eta}}\bigr)\geq 0.

Moreover,
\mathcal{J}((t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}}),\bm{\nu}^{*},{\bm{\eta}})=0
if and only if there exists
{\bm{v}}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{1},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
such that

(2.38)

{\eta}_{t}(a)=-\frac{\nu^{*}_{t}(a)}{\epsilon}\Bigl[\bigl\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)-c_{t}\Bigr]

for almost all

(t,a)\in[t_{1},T]\times A
, where
c_{t}
is a normalizing constant to ensure that
\eta_{t}
integrates to
0
,
and
({\bm{v}},{\bm{\rho}})
solves the linearized system

(2.39)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}v_{t}-b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}=b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}&\textrm{\rm in }[t_{1},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad v_{T}=0\quad\textrm{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\rho_{t}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\eta_{t})\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr)&\textrm{\rm in }(t_{1},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad\rho_{t_{1}}=0.\end{array}\right.

Remark 2.23
.

(i) Notice that
{\bm{\eta}}
solves

(2.40)

\begin{split}-\epsilon\Delta_{a}&\eta_{t}-\operatorname{div}_{a}\Bigl[\Bigl(\epsilon\nabla_{a}\ell+\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\Bigr)\eta_{t}\Bigr]\\
&=\operatorname{div}_{a}\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla_{a}\Bigl[\bigl\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\Bigr]\Bigr)\nu^{*}_{t}\Bigr],\end{split}

in
A
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
, which is the linearized version of (
2.14
).
Indeed, computing the derivative of the log and then inserting the expression for

\eta_{t}
, one has

\displaystyle\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\eta_{t}}{\nu^{*}_{t}}(a)=-\frac{\nu^{*}_{t}(a)}{\eta_{t}(a)}\nabla_{a}\Bigl[\bigl\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\Bigr].

Moreover, using the expression for
\nu_{t}^{*}
, one can rewrite the left-hand side in the form

\displaystyle\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\eta_{t}}{\nu^{*}_{t}}(a)=\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\eta_{t}(a)-\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\nu^{*}_{t}(a)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\eta_{t}(a)+\epsilon\nabla_{a}\ell(a)+\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y).

Identifying the right-hand sides of the last two displays,
multiplying both sides by
\eta_{t}
and taking the divergence in
a
, we conclude that
\bm{\eta}
solves (
2.40
).

(ii) In Lemma
7.5
we prove that, for
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
a\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*};\rho_{t}\rangle
is continuously differentiable and its gradient with respect to
a
is given by
\langle\nabla_{a}[b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*}];\rho_{t}\rangle
.

(iii) We also notice that the constant
c_{t}
is given by

c_{t}=\bigl\langle b(\cdot,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y).

Similarly to Proposition
2.9
, we can use the second order conditions to infer more regularity on
\bm{(\eta,\rho,v)}
. The proof is also given in Section
8
.

Proposition 2.24
.

Assume that
(\bm{\eta},\bm{v},\bm{\rho})
is a solution to (
2.38
)–(
2.39
) around a solution
(\bm{\nu},\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
of (
2.11
)-(
2.12
). Then,
t\mapsto\eta_{t}\in\mathcal{M}_{1+|a|^{3}}(A)
is continuous and
\bm{v}
and
\nabla_{x}\bm{v}
are jointly (in
(t,x,y)
)
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

2.5.
Stability conditions

Based on these second-order conditions, it is natural to introduce the notion of stable solutions characterized by the unique solvability of the linearized system. The interpretation is that, for a stable solution
\bm{\nu}^{*}
for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
, we have

\frac{d^{2}}{d\lambda^{2}}\Big|_{\lambda=0}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}+\lambda\bm{\eta}\bigr)>0

for any non trivial (i.e.,
\bm{\eta}\neq 0
) perturbation
\bm{\eta}\in\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})
such that
\bm{\nu}^{*}+\lambda\bm{\eta}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
for small enough
\lambda
.

Definition 2.25
.

For
\gamma_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and
t_{0}\in[0,T)
,
we say that
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
is a stable minimizer of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
if
(0,0,0)
is the only solution to the linearized system (
2.38
)–(
2.39
) (starting from
t_{0}
, around the solution
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
solution to (
2.11
)–(
2.12
)), with uniqueness being understood
among the triples
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}},{\bm{v}})

in

\mathcal{A}^{\ell}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{R}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
.

We can now define properly the set

{\mathcal{O}}
introduced in Subsection

1.3
, on which all our main
results are constructed. In words,

{\mathcal{O}}

is the set of pairs
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})

of initial time
t_{0}
and initial distribution of features

\gamma_{0}
for which there is a unique stable optimal solution, namely

(2.41)

\mathcal{O}:=\Bigl\{(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}});\mbox{ there is a unique stable minimum for }J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot\bigr)\Bigr\}.

We emphasise that by ‘unique stable’, we mean that
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
admits a unique solution and that this solution is stable. At this stage, it is not clear that the set
\mathcal{O}
is not empty (which property will be proven in Section
4
).

3.
Perturbation Analysis around Minimizers

The goal of this Section is to provide quantitative and qualitative stability properties of the cost
J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu})
as well as the couple
(\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
solution to (
2.20
) and the measure
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}]
solution to (
2.19
) with respect to perturbations of the control
\bm{\nu}
around an optimal control
\bm{\nu}^{*}
. The results will be necessary to prove that the set
\mathcal{O}
is open in Section
4
and to prove the PL inequality in Section
5
. The technical proof of the compactness argument of Proposition
3.5
and the subsequential convergence statements of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
are postponed to Section
9
.

Throughout this section we consider the following situation. We take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and
\bm{\nu}^{*}
an optimal control for
J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot\bigr)
with associated optimal trajectory and multiplier
(\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
. We also consider a sequence of tuples
(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n},\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\nu}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the following properties.

Property (
{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
).

(i)

The sequence
(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
;

(ii)

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
is an optimal solution for
J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot\bigr)
with associated trajectory and multiplier
(\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})
;

(iii)

The sequence
(\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{v}^{*})
in the sense of Lemma
2.15
;

(iv)

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{\nu}^{n}
is an element of
\mathcal{A}(t_{0}^{n})
with associated curve and multiplier
(\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})
solution to (
2.20
) starting from
(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})
and
distinct
from
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
;

(v)

The following convergence holds

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu_{t}^{*,n})dt=0.

3.1.
Quantitative Properties

Thanks to items
i)
and
ii)
, by Proposition
2.9
, Proposition
2.6

and Lemma
2.4
together with the growth assumption on
\ell
,
we obtain

Lemma 3.1
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
,
There exist constants
C,c>0
such that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\int_{A}e^{c(1+|a|^{4})}

\displaystyle d\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)+\left\|\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})}

(3.1)

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}}+\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)\leq C.

Define
(\lambda_{n},\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
by

(3.2)

\lambda_{n}^{2}:=\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu_{t}^{*,n})dt,\quad\bm{\eta}^{n}:=\frac{\bm{\nu}^{n}-\bm{\nu}^{*,n}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad\bm{\rho}^{n}:=\frac{\bm{\gamma}^{n}-\bm{\gamma}^{*,n}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad\bm{v}^{n}:=\frac{\bm{u}^{n}-\bm{u}^{*,n}}{\lambda_{n}}.

Notice that, since
\bm{\nu}^{n}\neq\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
,
\lambda_{n}>0
and the quantities above are well-defined.

It is convenient to introduce the auxiliary variable
\bm{k}^{n}:[t_{0}^{n},T]\times A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
(t,a)\in[t_{0}^{n},T]\times A
by

(3.3)

k_{t}^{n}(a):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d(\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}u^{*,n}_{t}\rho_{t}^{n})(x,y).

Notice that
k_{t}^{n}
is nothing but the normalized difference between the arguments appearing in the exponentials defining
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
and
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
, ie,

k_{t}^{n}(a)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x,y)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)\Bigr].

We can express the cost of
\bm{\nu}^{n}
as well as the Gibbs measure
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
defined in (
2.19
) with respect to these new variables and we have

Lemma 3.2
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
and with the variables introduced in (
3.2
), it holds, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.4)

J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)=\epsilon\lambda_{n}^{2}+\lambda_{n}^{2}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\eta_{t}^{n})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*,n}_{t}(x,y)d\rho_{t}^{n}(x,y)dt,

and, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t\in[t^{n}_{0},T]
,

(3.5)

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]\propto\nu_{t}^{*,n}e^{-\lambda_{n}k_{t}^{n}}.

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

Step 1. Expression for the costs.
We start with the proof of (
3.4
). We first use the elementary relation

a\log\frac{a}{c}-b\log\frac{b}{c}=a\log\frac{a}{b}+(a-b)\log\frac{b}{c}\quad\mbox{for all }a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}^{*}_{+}

to obtain

\displaystyle J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle-J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)=\epsilon\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}(a)}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}(a)}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt

(3.6)

\displaystyle+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)}{\nu^{\infty}(a)}d(\nu_{t}^{n}-\nu_{t}^{*,n})(a)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d(\gamma_{T}^{n}-\gamma_{T}^{*,n})(x,y).

On the one hand, the explicit expression
(
2.11
)
for
\nu_{t}^{*,n}
, for
t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]
, leads to

(3.7)

\epsilon\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)}{\nu^{\infty}(a)}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)+c_{t}^{n},\quad a\in A,

where
c_{t}^{n}
is independent from
a
. On the other hand, using the equations satisfied by
\bm{u}^{*,n}
,
\bm{\gamma}^{*,n}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}^{n}
,
see
(
2.20
), we have, by Lemma
A.12
,

(3.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d(\gamma_{T}^{n}-\gamma_{T}^{*,n})(x,y)=\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu_{t}^{n}-\nu_{t}^{*,n})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x,y)dt.

Inserting (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) in (
3.6
)
and recalling
that
\nu^{n}_{t}(A)=\nu^{*,n}_{t}(A)=1
for every

t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]
, we obtain

\displaystyle J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),

\displaystyle\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)=\epsilon\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}(a)}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}(a)}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt

(3.9)

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu^{n}_{t}-\nu^{*,n}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*,n}_{t}(x,y)d(\gamma^{n}_{t}-\gamma^{*,n}_{t})(x,y)dt.

Recalling the definition of
\lambda_{n},\bm{\eta}^{n}
and
\bm{\rho}^{n}
we obtain (
3.4
).

Step 2. Expression for
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
.
We go on with the expression for the probability measure
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
defined in (
2.19
) for some
t
in
[t_{0}^{n},T]
. Recalling the explicit expression (
2.11
) for
\nu_{t}^{*,n}
we know that there are two constants
z_{t}^{n},z_{t}^{*,n}
such that

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)=\frac{1}{z_{t}^{n}}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x,y)\Bigr),

\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)=\frac{1}{z_{t}^{*,n}}\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)\Bigr),

and, as a consequence, combining these two expressions

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{t}^{*,n}}{z_{t}^{n}}\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d\bigl(\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}-\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}\gamma_{t}^{*,n}\bigr)(x,y)\Bigr).

Rewriting

\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}-\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}\gamma_{t}^{*,n}=(\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{n}-\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n})\gamma_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(\gamma_{t}^{n}-\gamma_{t}^{*,n})

and recalling that
\rho_{t}^{n}=\lambda_{n}^{-1}(\gamma_{t}^{n}-\gamma_{t}^{*,n})
,
v_{t}^{n}=\lambda_{n}^{-1}(u_{t}^{n}-u_{t}^{*,n})
we obtain

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)=\frac{z_{t}^{*,n}}{z_{t}^{n}}\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)e^{-\lambda_{n}k_{t}^{n}(a)},

with
k_{t}^{n}
defined by (
3.3
).
∎

By making the difference between the system
(
2.20
)
(which is satisfied by

({\bm{\gamma}}^{n},{\bm{u}}^{n})
)
and
the system (
2.12
)
(which is satisfied by

(\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})
)
and then by dividing by
\lambda_{n}
, we get

(3.10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle-\partial_{t}{v}_{t}^{n}(x,y)-b\bigl(x,{\nu}_{t}^{n}\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}{v}_{t}^{n}(x,y)=b(x,\eta_{t}^{n})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*,n}_{t}(x,y)&\textrm{\rm in}\ [t_{0}^{n},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad{v}_{T}^{n}(x,y)=0&\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rho}_{t}^{n}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,{\nu}_{t}^{n}){\rho}_{t}^{n}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\eta_{t}^{n})\gamma^{*,n}_{t}\bigr)&\textrm{\rm in}\ (t_{0}^{n},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\qquad{\rho}_{t_{0}^{n}}^{n}=0&\textrm{\rm in}\ \mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

Invoking Lemma
2.13
and recalling the definition of
\lambda_{n}
we can control
\bm{\nu}^{n}
in
\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})
. Then, applying Lemma
7.6
for
\bm{\rho}^{n}
and Lemma
7.16
for
\bm{v}^{n}

together with Lemma
3.1
, we get the following uniform estimate:

Lemma 3.3
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
, there is
C>0
such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\|\bm{\eta}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})}+\left\|\bm{\rho}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0}^{n})}+\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|v_{t}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}}+\sup_{t_{1}<t_{2}\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\frac{\left\|v_{t_{2}}^{n}-v_{t_{1}}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}+\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|k_{t}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}(A)}\leq C.

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

By Lemma
2.13
and recalling the definition of
\lambda_{n}
we can control
\bm{\eta}^{n}
in
\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})
. The estimates on
\bm{\rho}^{n}
and
\bm{v}^{n}
then follow from Lemmas
7.6
and
7.16
respectively together with Lemma
3.1
which guarantees that
\left\|\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}^{n}(x,y)
are bounded independently from
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The estimate on
\bm{k}^{n}
then follows from the regularity assumptions on
b
through the formula (
1.25
) with
q=1
,
k_{1}=0
,
p_{1}=3
,
p_{2}=3
,
k_{2}=1
since it gives

\left\|k_{t}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}(A)}\leq C_{b}\bigl(\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x,y)\bigr)\bigl(\left\|\rho_{t}^{n}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}+\left\|\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{3}}\bigr).

Notice that the term involving
\bm{\gamma^{n}}
is bounded independently from
n
by Lemma
2.4
.
∎

A direct consequence is

Lemma 3.4
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
, we have the following expansion of the costs, as
n\rightarrow+\infty

J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{n}\bigr)=J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)+O(\lambda_{n}^{2}).

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

We use the expression of the cost from Lemma
3.5
and then formula (
1.24
) with
q=0,k=1,p=3
which gives, after an application of Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\eta_{t}^{n})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)d\rho_{t}^{n}(x,y)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A}\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}(x,y)d\rho_{t}^{n}(x,y)\Bigr|d|\eta_{t}^{n}|(a)

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3}}\left\|\rho^{n}_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d|\eta_{t}^{n}|(a).

Integrating in time and recalling the estimates from Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.1
we obtain the result.
∎

3.2.
Weak Limits

Thanks to the estimates of Lemma
3.3
we can also find weak limit points for the sequence
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and pass to the limit in the system (
3.10
). The proof is given in Section
9
.

Proposition 3.5
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
, there is
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})\in\mathcal{A}^{l}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{R}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
such that
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges, up to a sub-sequence, to
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
in the following sense:

i)

(convergence of
(\bm{\eta}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
)

For any
[t_{1},t_{2}]\subset(t_{0},T]
and for any
f\in\mathcal{C}([t_{1},t_{2}]\times A)

satisfying
|f_{t}(a)|\leq C(1+|a|^{3+\delta})
for some
(C,\delta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times(0,1)
and all
(t,a)\in[t_{1},t_{2}]\times A
,

(3.11)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}f_{t}(a)d(\eta_{t}^{n}-\eta_{t})(a)dt=0;

ii)

(convergence of
(\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
)

(3.12)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\Bigl\{\sup_{t\in[t_{0}\vee t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n}-\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{2})^{*}}+\sup_{t\in[t_{0}\vee t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|v_{t}^{n}-v_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\Bigr\}=0.

Moreover, for any such limit point,
(\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
is solution to

(3.13)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\partial_{t}v_{t}-b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}=b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}&\mbox{\rm in }[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad v_{T}=0&\mbox{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\rho_{t}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}(b(x,\eta_{t})\gamma^{*}_{t})&\mbox{\rm in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad\rho_{t_{0}}=0&\mbox{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

The convergence of
(\bm{\nu}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
translates into a convergence for
(\bm{k}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined through (
3.3
). For a given limit point
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
we define, for all
(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A

(3.14)

k_{t}(a):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle.

The next convergence statement for
(\bm{k}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
together with the result of Proposition
3.7
follow from Proposition
3.5
, the uniform estimates from Lemma
3.1
and the growth and regularity assumptions on the vector field
b
. The detailed proofs are given in Section
9
.

Proposition 3.6
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
and with the limit point
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
given by Proposition
3.5
, the map
t\mapsto k_{t}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}(A)
is bounded and
\bm{k}^{n}
converges to
\bm{k}
in the following ways:

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0},T]}\left\|k_{t}^{n}-k_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{3}(A)}=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0},T]}\int_{A}|k_{t}^{n}(a)-k_{t}(a)|d\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)=0.

If the limit point
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
is the triple
(0,0,0)
, then the convergence can be strengthened to

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0},T]}\left\|k^{n}_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3}(A)}=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}|\nabla_{a}k_{t}^{n}(a)|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt=0.

As a consequence, and recalling the expression (
3.5
) for
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
, we get

Proposition 3.7
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
and for the limit point
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
given by Proposition
3.5
, we have

(3.15)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\int_{A}\Bigl|\frac{\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)-\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)}{\lambda_{n}}+\nu_{t}^{*}(a)\bigl(k_{t}(a)-\int_{A}k_{t}(a^{\prime})d\nu_{t}^{*}(a^{\prime})\bigr)\Bigr|da\Bigr)^{2}dt=0,

where
\bm{k}
is defined in (
3.14
).

In particular, when
(\bm{\nu}^{n},\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})
is a solution to (
2.11
)-(
2.12
) we can replace
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
by
\bm{\nu}^{n}
in (
3.15
) and we deduce the following result.

Proposition 3.8
.

In the setting of
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
, if we assume as well that the triple
(\bm{\nu}^{n},\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})
is solution to (
2.11
)-(
2.12
) for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then any weak limit point
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
of
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in the sense of Proposition
3.5
solves the linearized system (
2.38
)-(
2.39
).

4.
Discriminating Property and the Jacobi Condition

This section is dedicated to the
proof of
Meta-Theorem

1.3
, a more precise and rigorous version of which is given below. Recall that stable solutions were introduced in Definition
2.25
and the set
\mathcal{O}
in (
2.41
).

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

be a (non-necessarily unique) minimizer of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
with
corresponding curve

\bm{\gamma}^{*}=(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
. Then, for all
t_{1}\in(t_{0},T]
,

(t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}})\in\mathcal{O}
.
Moreover, the set
\mathcal{O}
is open and dense in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
.

The first part of Theorem

4.1

can be referred to as a
Jacobi condition
, or
Jacobi no conjugate point optimality condition
, (see
[
10
]
Chapter 6 for similar results in finite dimension and Remark 6.3.7 therein for the terminology). The proof is
given in Subsection

4.1
, except for the (more expected) proof of the openness of the set
\mathcal{O}
, which is given in Section
4.4
. The main ingredients are the dynamic programming principle and
the following two
Propositions
4.2

and
4.3
, which
follow themselves from
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T)\times{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
.
Assume that
({\bm{\nu}}^{1},{\bm{\gamma}}^{1},{\bm{u}}^{1})=(\nu^{1}_{t},\gamma^{1}_{t},u_{t}^{1})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
and
({\bm{\nu}}^{2},{\bm{\gamma}}^{2},{\bm{u}}^{2})=(\nu^{2}_{t},\gamma^{2}_{t},u_{t}^{2})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
are two solutions of the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) with
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
as initial condition. Then,

\nu^{1}_{t_{0}}=\nu^{2}_{t_{0}}\Rightarrow\Bigl(\forall t\in[t_{0},T],\quad\nu^{1}_{t}=\nu^{2}_{t}\Bigr).

In particular, when
\nu_{t_{0}}^{1}=\nu_{t_{0}}^{2}
, then
(\gamma_{t}^{1},u_{t}^{1})=(\gamma_{t}^{2},u_{t}^{2})
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

In words, the result above can be described as a
no bifurcation
property: if two optimal controls coincide at the initial time they must coincide throughout the whole time horizon. We have an analogous result for the linearized system:

Proposition 4.3
.

Assume that
(\eta_{t},\rho_{t},v_{t})_{t\geq t_{0}}
is a solution to the linearized system (
2.38
)-(
2.39
) for some given triple
(\nu^{*}_{t},\gamma^{*}_{t},u^{*}_{t})_{t\geq t_{0}}
solution to (
2.11
)-(
2.12
) . Then,

\eta_{t_{0}}=0\Rightarrow\Bigl(\forall t\in[t_{0},T],\quad\eta_{t}=0\Bigr).

In particular, when
\eta_{t_{0}}=0
, then
(\rho_{t},v_{t})=(0,0)
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

Subsections
4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
are dedicated to the proofs of Theorem
4.1
, Proposition
4.2
and Proposition
4.3
respectively. In Subsection
4.4
we prove that
\mathcal{O}
is open and that stable solutions are
isolated
. In Subsection
4.5
we explain the link between the discriminating property imposed on the vector field
b
and the universal approximation property of typical activation functions in machine learning.

4.1.
Proof of Theorem

4.1

Taking for granted Propositions

4.2

and

4.3
, we prove
Theorem

4.1
.

Proof.

Step 1.
Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and an arbitrary minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
with corresponding curve
\bm{\gamma}^{*}=(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
.
We claim that for any
t_{1}\in(t_{0},T]
,

(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{1}\leq t\leq T}
is the unique minimizer of
J((t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}),\cdot).

By dynamic programming, it is indeed
a minimizer of

J((t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}),\cdot)
.
Uniqueness is shown as follows.

Let
{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{t})_{t_{1}\leq t\leq T}
be an (arbitrary) optimal control for
J((t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}),\cdot)
with
corresponding curve
{\bm{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{t})_{t_{1}\leq t\leq T}
. We extend
(\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})_{t_{1}\leq t\leq T}
to
[t_{0},t_{1})
by setting

(4.1)

(\overline{\nu}_{t},\overline{\gamma}_{t}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\nu^{*}_{t},\gamma^{*}_{t})&\mbox{ if }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}),\\
(\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})&\mbox{ if }t\in[t_{1},T].\end{array}\right.

Since
\gamma_{t_{1}}=\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}
,
we can easily recombine the two
continuity equations
(i.e., the second equation in (
2.12
))
solved by
{\bm{\gamma}}

and
\bm{\gamma}^{*}

respectively and then deduce that

\overline{\bm{\gamma}}=(\overline{\gamma}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
solves the continuity equation associated with the control

\overline{\bm{\nu}}=(\overline{\nu}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
. Moreover, by dynamic programming,
\overline{\bm{\nu}}=(\overline{\nu}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
is an optimal control for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
.
By construction,
\overline{\nu}_{t_{0}}=\nu^{*}_{t_{0}}
. Therefore, we deduce from Proposition
4.2
that
\overline{\nu}_{t}=\nu^{*}_{t}
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
. In particular, we have
\nu_{t}=\nu^{*}_{t}
for all
t\in[t_{1},T]
, which proves the claim.

Step 2.
Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})

and
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*})

as in the first step.
We claim that for any
t_{1}\in(t_{0},T]
,
(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{1}\leq t\leq T}
is a stable solution of
J((t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}),\cdot).

Indeed, call

\bm{u}^{*}=(u^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

the second component of the
solution

(\gamma^{*}_{t},u^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
to the system (
2.12
) starting from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and associated with
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
. Take now a solution
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}},{\bm{v}})=(\eta_{t},\rho_{t},v_{t})_{t_{1}\leq t\leq T}
to the linearized system (
2.38
)– (
2.39
) starting from
t_{1}
, around the triple

(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
.
By Theorem
2.39
,
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}})
is a minimum of
\mathcal{J}((t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}}),\bm{\nu}^{*},\cdot)
and the corresponding value is
0
. We extend
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}})
to
[t_{0},T]
by setting

(4.2)

(\overline{\eta}_{t},\overline{\rho}_{t}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(0,0)&\mbox{ if }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}),\\
(\eta_{t},\rho_{t})&\mbox{ if }t\in[t_{1},T].\end{array}\right.

We easily check that

\mathcal{J}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*},\overline{\bm{\eta}}\bigr)=0+\mathcal{J}\bigl((t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}}^{*}),\bm{\nu}^{*},{\bm{\eta}}\bigr)=0.

By Theorem
2.39
again, we deduce that
(\overline{\eta}_{t},\overline{\rho}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
is a minimum of
\mathcal{J}(t_{0},(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*}),\cdot)
. In particular, there is a multiplier
\overline{\bm{v}}=(\overline{v}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
such that the triple
(\overline{\eta}_{t},\overline{\rho}_{t},\overline{v}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
solves the linearized system (
2.38
)–(
2.39
) starting from
t_{0}
, around the solution
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
. By construction,
\overline{\eta}_{t_{0}}=0
. Therefore, we can use Proposition
4.3
to infer that
(\overline{\eta}_{t},\overline{\rho}_{t},\overline{u}_{t})=(0,0,0)
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
. In particular
(\eta_{t},\rho_{t})=(0,0)
for all
t\in[t_{1},T]
, from which we easily deduce that
(\eta_{t},\rho_{t},v_{t})=(0,0,0)
for all
t\in[t_{1},T]
.
This shows that
(t_{1},\gamma^{*}_{t_{1}})\in{\mathcal{O}}.

Step 3.

As a consequence, we can prove that
\mathcal{O}
is dense.
Take indeed
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})

and an optimal control
(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
with associated curve
(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
. By Steps 1 and 2, we know that
(t,\gamma^{*}_{t})
belongs to
\mathcal{O}
for any
t\in(t_{0},T]
. Since
t\mapsto\gamma^{*}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
is continuous, we easily get the result. The proof that
\mathcal{O}
is open is the object of Subsection
4.4
.
∎

4.2.
Proof of Proposition

4.2

Proof.

Following the statement, we consider

(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T)\times{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})

and
({\bm{\nu}}^{1},{\bm{\gamma}}^{1},{\bm{u}}^{1})
and
({\bm{\nu}}^{2},{\bm{\gamma}}^{2},{\bm{u}}^{2})
two solutions of (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) with
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
as initial condition.

Step 1. ODE representation.

Following Proposition
2.16
, we represent
(\bm{\gamma}^{i},\bm{u}^{i})
for
i=1,2
by means of a system of ordinary differential equations with random initial conditions. On some probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
,
consider a pair
(X_{0},Y_{0})

of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
-valued random variables with
{\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{0},Y_{0})^{-1}=\gamma_{0}
. For
i=1,2
,
we call

{\bm{X}}^{i}

the solution to the ODE

\dot{X}_{t}^{i}=b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i}),\quad t\in[t_{0},T];\quad X_{t_{0}}^{i}=X_{0},

which is indeed uniquely solvable thanks
to
Assumption (
Regularity
)

and to Proposition

2.9
. We then let

(4.3)

Z_{t}^{i}=\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{i}(X_{t}^{i},Y_{0}),\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

By
differentiating in
x

the first equation
in
(
2.11
)
and then
expanding (in time) the right-hand side
in (
4.3
) (which is possible thanks to the regularity of
{\bm{u}}^{i}

ensured by Proposition
2.9
), we deduce that
{\bm{Z}}^{i}=(Z_{t}^{i})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
solves the
(uniquely solvable)
backward linear ODE

\dot{Z}_{t}^{i}=-\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i})Z_{t}^{i},\quad t\in[t_{0},T];\quad Z_{T}^{i}=\nabla_{x}L(X_{T}^{i},Y_{0}).

Equivalently,
({\bm{X}}^{i},{\bm{Z}}^{i})
is the solution to
the forward-backward ODE system:

(4.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{X}_{t}^{i}=b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i}),&\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad X_{t_{0}}^{i}=X_{0};\\
\dot{Z}_{t}^{i}=-\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i})Z_{t}^{i},&\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad Z_{T}^{i}=\nabla_{x}L(X_{T}^{i},Y_{0}).\end{array}\right.

We easily check that, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
{\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{t}^{i},Y_{0})^{-1}=\gamma_{t}^{i}
.

Step 2. Conditional expectation of the backward component upon
X_{0}
.
For
i=1,2
,
{\bm{X}}^{i}
solves an ODE with a Lipschitz continuous velocity field. Therefore, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
we can find two homeomorphisms
F_{t}^{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
,
i=1,2
such that

X_{t}^{i}=F_{t}^{i}\bigl(X_{0}\bigr).

In particular, the
\sigma
-fields

(\sigma(X_{t}^{i}))_{i=1,2}
generated by

X_{t}^{1}
and
X_{t}^{2}
respectively are each
equal to
\sigma(X_{0})
.

We then introduce, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
, the notation

(4.5)

\widehat{Z}_{t}^{i}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{t}^{i}|X_{0}\bigr].

Notice that, for each
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

\widehat{Z}_{t}^{i}
is just uniquely defined

{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely.
However, using the time continuity
of
{\bm{Z}}^{i}
, it is standard to have a version of all these conditional expectations
such that
{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely,
t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\widehat{Z}_{t}^{i}

is continuous.

Since
X_{t}^{i}
is
\sigma(X_{0})
-measurable for each
t\in[t_{0},T]
, we have

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i})Z_{t}^{i}|X_{0}\bigr]=\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i})\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{t}^{i}|X_{0}\bigr]=\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i})\widehat{Z}_{t}^{i}.

We deduce that, for
i=1,2
,
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{i}=(\widehat{Z}^{i}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
solves

\tfrac{d}{dt}\widehat{Z}_{t}^{i}=-\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i})\widehat{Z}_{t}^{i},\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

Step 3. Injectivity property.

Recalling formula
(
2.11
), we can rewrite
\nu_{t}^{i}
, for
i=1,2
as

\nu_{t}^{i}(a)=\frac{1}{z_{t}^{i}}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{t}^{i},a)\cdot Z_{t}^{i}\bigr]\Bigr),\quad a\in A.

The above identity is true for
any
t\in[t_{0},T]
. In particular, we can choose
t=t_{0}
.
By assumption, we already have

\nu_{t_{0}}^{1}=\nu_{t_{0}}^{2}
.
Then, taking
a=0

(which is possible since
\nu_{t_{0}}^{i}
is a
smooth function of
a
, see Proposition

2.9
)
and using the fact that

b(x,0)=0
for all
x

(see item (i) in

Assumption (
Regularity
)
), we obtain

z_{t_{0}}^{1}=z_{t_{0}}^{2}
.
We
deduce that

\forall a\in A,\quad\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{0},a)\cdot Z_{t_{0}}^{1}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{0},a)\cdot Z_{t_{0}}^{2}\bigr].

Recalling the notation (
4.5
), we deduce from

Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
that,
{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely,

\widehat{Z}_{t_{0}}^{1}=\widehat{Z}_{t_{0}}^{2}
.

Step 4 - Stability argument and conclusion.

The point is to propagate the
identity

\widehat{Z}_{t_{0}}^{1}=\widehat{Z}_{t_{0}}^{2}

to any time
t\in(t_{0},T]
.
First,
we address the regularity
of
the coefficients
b
and
\nabla_{x}b

(in
(
4.4
))
with respect to the measure argument.
We claim that there exists
C>0
such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

(4.6)

|b(x,\nu_{t}^{2})-b(x,\nu_{t}^{1})|+|\nabla_{x}b(x,\nu_{t}^{2})-\nabla_{x}b(x,\nu_{t}^{1})|\leq C\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{t}^{2}-X_{t}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}|\bigr]\bigr).

In a nutshell, the inequality
(
4.6
)
follows from the integrability properties of

{\bm{\nu}}^{1}

and

{\bm{\nu}}^{2}

stated in
Proposition
2.9
and the assumptions on
b

stated in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
.
We provide a sketch of the proof
at the end of the paragraph.

Thanks to the equations satisfied by
{\bm{X}}^{1}
and
{\bm{X}}^{2}
, to
(
4.6
)
and to the Lipschitz property of

b
in
x
(locally in
a
, the gradient in

x
growing at most at a quadratic rate in

a
), we then find a constant
C>0
such that,
\mathbb{P}-
almost-surely,

(4.7)

|X_{t}^{2}-X_{t}^{1}|\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{s}^{2}-X_{s}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{s}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{s}^{1}|\bigr]\bigr)ds,\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

Using the identity
\widehat{Z}^{1}_{t_{0}}=\widehat{Z}^{2}_{t_{0}}
, the equations for
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{1}
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{2}
(seen forward in time) lead to

|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}|\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\bigl(|X_{s}^{2}-X_{s}^{1}|+\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{s}^{2}-X_{s}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigr[|\widehat{Z}_{s}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{s}^{1}|\bigr]\bigr)|\widehat{Z}^{2}_{s}|ds,\quad t\in[t_{0},T],

and so, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

(4.8)

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}|\bigr]\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{s}^{2}-X_{s}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigr[|\widehat{Z}_{s}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{s}^{1}|\bigr]\bigr)\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}^{2}_{s}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}ds,\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

We make the following observation: since
(\nabla_{x}b(X_{t}^{i},\nu_{t}^{i}))_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

is bounded, there exists
a constant
C>0
such that
\sup_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}|Z_{t}^{i}|\leq C|Z_{T}^{i}|=C|\nabla_{x}L(X_{T}^{i},Y_{0})|
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Recalling that

({\bm{X}}^{i})_{i=1,2}

are square-integrable (since
({\bm{\gamma}}^{i})_{i=1,2}

take values in

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
),
we deduce that
{\mathbb{E}}[\sup_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}|Z_{t}^{i}|^{2}]\leq C
and, by Jensen’s inequality for the conditional expectation

(4.9)

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{Z}^{i}_{t}|^{2}]\leq C.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}|\bigr]\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{s}^{2}-X_{s}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigr[|\widehat{Z}_{s}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{s}^{1}|\bigr]\bigr)ds,\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

Since (
4.7
) is true
\mathbb{P}-
almost-surely, it also holds in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
and we deduce that

(4.10)

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{t}^{2}-X_{t}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{s}^{2}-X_{s}^{1}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{s}^{2}-\widehat{Z}_{s}^{1}|\bigr]\bigr)ds,\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

Combining (
4.10
) and (
4.8
) and then using Grönwall’s lemma, we obtain

\forall t\in[t_{0},T],\quad\mathbb{E}\bigl[|X_{t}^{1}-X_{t}^{2}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}|]=0.

Returning to the equations satisfied by

{\bm{\nu}}^{1}
and

{\bm{\nu}}^{2}
, we deduce in particular that
\nu_{t}^{1}=\nu_{t}^{2}
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
. This easily implies that
(\gamma_{t}^{1},u_{t}^{1})=(\gamma_{t}^{2},u_{t}^{2})
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
.

We now explain the proof of
(
4.6
).
By item (i) in

Assumption (
Regularity
)
,

|b(x,\nu_{t}^{1})-b(x,\nu_{t}^{2})|+|\nabla_{x}b(x,\nu_{t}^{1})-\nabla_{x}b(x,\nu_{t}^{2})|\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(1+|a|^{2})|\nu_{t}^{1}(a)-\nu_{t}^{2}(a)|da,\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

It then remains to study the difference

\nu_{t}^{1}(a)-\nu_{t}^{2}(a)
, which relies itself on the difference

{\mathbb{E}}[b(X_{t}^{1},a)\cdot Z_{t}^{1}-b(X_{t}^{2},a)\cdot Z_{t}^{2}]={\mathbb{E}}[b(X_{t}^{1},a)\cdot\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}-b(X_{t}^{2},a)\cdot\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}]
. Then, using once again item (i) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
,
we obtain

\begin{split}\bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t}^{1},a)\cdot\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}-b(X_{t}^{2},a)\cdot\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}\bigr]\bigr|&\leq C(1+|a|^{2}){\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl(1+|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}|\bigr)|X_{t}^{1}-X_{t}^{2}|+|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}|\Bigr]\\
&\leq C(1+|a|^{2})\Bigl({\mathbb{E}}\bigl[|X_{t}^{1}-X_{t}^{2}|^{2}\bigr]^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{Z}_{t}^{1}-\widehat{Z}_{t}^{2}|\bigr]\Bigr),\end{split}

with the second line following from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
4.9
).
Using the fact that
\ell
grows
at least as
|a|^{4}
together with the bound

|{\mathbb{E}}[b(X_{t}^{i},a)\cdot Z_{t}^{i}]|\leq C(1+|a|)
, we can easily insert the latter bound in the exponential
writing of
\nu_{t}^{i}
, see
(
2.11
),
with a similar argument for the normalizing constants
z_{t}^{i}
.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Proposition of
4.3

The proof of Proposition
4.3
is very similar to the one of Proposition
4.2
.

Proof.

Let

(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})

and

(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{u}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*})=(\nu^{*}_{t},\gamma^{*}_{t},u^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

be a minimizer of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
(and its corresponding curve and multiplier).

Step 1. ODE representation.

Following the proof
of Proposition

4.2
, we introduce the solution

\bm{X}^{*}=(X^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
of the ODE

\frac{d}{dt}X^{*}_{t}=b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad X^{*}_{t_{0}}=X_{0};

where
(X_{0},Y_{0})
is an
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}
-valued random variable, constructed on some
probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
,
with
{\mathbb{P}}\circ(X_{0},Y_{0})^{-1}=\gamma_{0}
.
Moreover, letting
\bm{Z}^{*}=(Z^{*}_{t}:=\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}))_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
, we know that

\frac{d}{dt}Z^{*}_{t}=-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)Z^{*}_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T];\quad Z^{*}_{T}=\nabla_{x}L\bigl(X^{*}_{T},Y_{0}\bigr).

We then introduce
{\bm{\zeta}}=(\zeta_{t}:=\nabla_{x}v_{t}(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}))_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
. Differentiating in
x
the first equation in
(
2.39
) (which is possible thanks to Proposition

2.24
), we obtain

(4.11)

\begin{split}\dot{\zeta}_{t}&=\nabla_{x}\partial_{t}v_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)+\nabla_{xx}^{2}v_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)\\
&=-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)\nabla_{x}v_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\eta_{t}\bigr)\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)-\nabla_{xx}^{2}u^{*}_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\eta_{t}\bigr)\\
&=-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)\zeta_{t}-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\eta_{t}\bigr)Z^{*}_{t}-\nabla_{xx}^{2}u^{*}_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\eta_{t}\bigr).\end{split}

Step 2. Conditioning upon
X_{0}
.

Following again the proof
of Proposition

4.2
, we define the
(time-continuous) collection of
conditional expectations:

\widehat{\zeta}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\zeta_{t}|X_{0}\bigr],\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

Taking conditional expectation in the equation
(
4.11
)
satisfied by
{\bm{\zeta}}
, using the fact that
X^{*}_{t}
is

\sigma(X_{0})
-measurable for any
t\in[t_{0},T]
and recalling (
4.13
), we get, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]

(4.12)

\begin{split}&\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\widehat{\zeta}_{t}=-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)\widehat{\zeta}_{t}-\nabla_{x}b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\eta_{t}\bigr)\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z^{*}_{t}|X_{0}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\nabla_{xx}^{2}u^{*}_{t}\bigl(X^{*}_{t},Y_{0}\bigr)|X_{0}]b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\eta_{t}\bigr).\end{split}

Step 3. Using the discriminating property.

Thanks to the explicit expression
(
2.38
)
for
\eta_{t}
, the condition
\eta_{t_{0}}=0

(combined with the already known condition
\rho_{t_{0}}=0
, see the last line
in (
2.39
)) leads to

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu^{*}_{t_{0}}-\delta_{a})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t_{0}}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t_{0}}(x,y)=0,\quad a\in A,

which can be rewritten as

\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{0},\nu^{*}_{t_{0}}-\delta_{a})\cdot{\zeta}_{t_{0}}\bigr]=0,\quad a\in A.

Taking first
a=0
(and using the assumption
b(x,0)=0
), we see that
\mathbb{E}[b(X_{0},\nu^{*}_{t_{0}})\cdot\zeta_{t_{0}}]=0
and then returning to the identity for a general
a
, we get, by linearity of
b(x,\nu)
with respect to
\nu
,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{0},a)\cdot\zeta_{t_{0}}\bigr]=0,\quad a\in A.

By
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
, we obtain,
{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely,

(4.13)

\widehat{\zeta}_{t_{0}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\zeta_{t_{0}}|X_{0}\bigr]=0.

Step 4. Stability argument.

Notice
from
(
2.38
)
that for any
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
\eta_{t}
can be rewritten as

(4.14)

\begin{split}\eta_{t}(a)&=\frac{\nu^{*}_{t}(a)}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl(b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)-b\bigl(X^{*}_{t},a\bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\widehat{\zeta}_{t}\bigr]+\frac{\nu^{*}_{t}(a)}{\epsilon}\bigl\langle\bigl(b(\cdot,\nu^{*}_{t})-b(\cdot,a)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle.\end{split}

Using Proposition
2.9
together with the integrability of
\nu_{t}^{*}
we infer that

\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d|\eta_{t}|(a)\leq C\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{t}|\bigr]+\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\Bigr).

In particular using
Assumption (
Regularity
)
we deduce the upper bound

|b(x,\eta_{t})|+|\nabla_{x}b(x,\eta_{t})|\leq C\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{t}|\bigr]+\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\Bigr),

for some
C>0
and for all
(t,x)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
. Recalling the definition of
\bm{Z}^{*}
, the estimate
\sup_{\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|u^{*}_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2,1}}<+\infty
from Proposition
2.6
and the integrability of
\gamma_{t}\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
we easily show that

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\mathbb{E}\bigl[|Z_{t}^{*}|\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\nabla_{xx}^{2}u_{t}^{*}(X_{t}^{*},Y_{0})|\bigr]<+\infty,

and then, using the equation (
4.12
), seen forward in time from the initial condition
\widehat{\zeta}_{t_{0}}=0
and applying Grönwall’s Lemma we find that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{t}|\bigr]\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{s}|\bigr]+\left\|\rho_{s}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\Bigr)ds.

However, using the explicit formula for
\bm{\rho}
from Proposition
2.19
we easily obtain

\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\int_{A}(1+|a|)d|\eta_{s}|(a)ds.

Combined together this leads to

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{t}|\bigr]+\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{s}|\bigr]+\left\|\rho_{s}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}\Bigr)ds.

We deduce from Grönwall’s Lemma again that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{\zeta}_{t}|\bigr]=\left\|\rho_{t}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{3})^{*}}=0,\quad t\in[t_{0},T].

Getting back to the equation (
4.14
) we deduce that
\eta_{t}=0
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
.
Then, by
(
2.39
), we get
(\rho_{t},v_{t})=0
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
.
∎

4.4.
Topological Properties of the Set
\mathcal{O}
.

We now prove some further topological properties for the set
\mathcal{O}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

The set
\mathcal{O}
is open in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
.

Proof.

Toward a contradiction, suppose that there exist
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
and a sequence
(t_{0}^{n},\gamma^{n}_{0})_{n\geq 1}\notin\mathcal{O}
converging in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
to
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
. Up to subsequences, there are two possibilities:

•

For all
n\geq 1
, there are two distinct minima of
J((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot)
.

•

For all
n\geq 1
, there is a unique minimum for
J((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot)
but it is not stable.

Case 1.

For all
n\geq 1
, let
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
and
\bm{\nu}^{n}
be
two distinct minima of
J((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot)
with associated curve and multiplier
(\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})
and
(\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})
respectively. We define the integrated relative entropy
\lambda_{n}^{2}:=\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu_{t}^{*,n})dt
and the new variables
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n}):=\lambda_{n}^{-1}(\bm{\nu}^{n}-\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\gamma}^{n}-\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{n}-\bm{u}^{*,n})
. By Lemma
6.7
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(4.15)

\lambda_{n}^{2}\leq C\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|\gamma^{*,n}_{t}-\gamma_{t}^{n}\right\|^{2}_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}.

In particular, for the same
C>0
as above and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(4.16)

\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}\geq 1/\sqrt{C}.

By Lemma
2.15
(recalling that
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
), we know that the two sequences
(\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\bm{\nu}^{n},\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converge to
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
, in the same sense as specified in Lemma
2.15
. In particular, by (
4.15
),
\lambda_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
. We are precisely in the framework of Section
3
(see
Property (
{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
)
) and we can apply the results of Propositions
3.5
and
3.8
to find a triple
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})\in\mathcal{A}^{l}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{R}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
solution to the linearized system (
2.38
)-(
2.39
) such that
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})
converges, up to a subsequence, to
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
in the sense of Proposition
3.5
. Since
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
belongs to
\mathcal{O}
, the limit triple
(\bm{\nu},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
is necessarily equal to
(0,0,0)
. In particular, by Proposition
3.5
again,
\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}=0
. This is in contradiction with (
4.16
).

Case 2.
We now address the case where, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the functional
J((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot)
admits a unique minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}:=(\nu^{*,n}_{t})_{t\geq t_{0}^{n}}
, which is not stable.
This means that we can find
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n}):=(\eta_{t}^{n},\rho_{t}^{n},v_{t}^{n})_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}
a non-trivial solution to the linearized system around
(\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})
where
\bm{\gamma}^{*,n}:=(\gamma_{t}^{*,n})_{t\geq t_{0}^{n}}
is the trajectory associated to
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
and
\bm{u}^{*,n}:=(u_{t}^{*,n})_{t\geq t_{0}^{n}}
the associated multiplier. By a stability argument similar to Lemma
6.6
,
\bm{\rho}^{n}
cannot be identically
0
(otherwise we would have
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})=(0,0,0)
). We look at the system satisfied by

\frac{\eta_{t}^{n}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad\frac{\rho_{t}^{n}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad\frac{v_{t}^{n}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad t\in[t_{0}^{n},T],\quad\quad\lambda_{n}:=\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]}\left\|\rho_{t}^{n}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{2}^{2})^{*}}>0,

and then proceed similarly as in the proof of the first step to obtain a contradiction by taking
n\rightarrow+\infty
and getting a non-trivial solution to the linearized system.
∎

The next result shows that optimal solutions
\bm{\nu}^{*}
for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
are isolated when
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
: there is a neighborhood of
\bm{\nu}^{*}
with no other critical point.

Proposition 4.5
.

Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{O}
with optimal solution
\bm{\nu}^{*}
and associated curve and multiplier
(\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{v}^{*})
. For some
r>0
, there is no other solution
(\bm{\nu},\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
to (
2.11
) - (
2.12
) with
\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)dt\leq r^{2}.

Proof.

Otherwise we can find a sequence
(\bm{\nu}^{n},\bm{\gamma}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))\times\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2})
solution to (
2.11
)-(
2.12
) but distinct from
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})
such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\Bigl\{\lambda_{n}^{2}:=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)dt\Bigr\}=0,

and
\lambda_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We are precisely in the framework of Section
3
with
(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})=(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}=\bm{\nu}^{*}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
therein. The rest of the proof is identical to the first case in the proof of Proposition
4.4
.
∎

4.5.
Discriminating Property and the Universal Approximation Theorem

In this subsection we explain the link between the discriminating property
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
and the universal approximation theorem within the prototypical example
1.1
. First we state a useful, but strictly equivalent form of
Assumption (
Discriminating Property
)
.

Discriminating Property - Equivalent Formulation.
For any probability measure

\pi
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
such that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}|z|d\pi(x,z)<\infty
, the following
implication holds true:

\biggl(\forall a\in A,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}\Bigl[b(x,a)\cdot z\Bigr]d\pi(x,z)=0\biggr)\Rightarrow\biggl({\rm for}\ \pi_{\rm x}\textrm{\rm-a.e.}\ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}},\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}z\pi(x,dz)=0\biggr),

where
\pi_{\rm x}
denotes the first marginal law of

\pi
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
(i.e.,

\pi_{\rm x}:=\pi\circ((x,z)\mapsto x)^{-1}
)
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\mapsto\pi(x,\cdot)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}})
is a measurable mapping obtained by disintegrating

\pi
with respect to the first marginal (i.e.,
for any two Borel subsets
E_{\rm x}
and
E_{\rm z}
of

{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
,
\pi(E_{\rm x}\times E_{\rm z})=\int_{E_{\rm x}}\pi(x,E_{\rm z})d\pi_{\rm x}(x)
).

The following statement shows that, within
the prototypical example
1.1
,

b
satisfies the discriminating property
if the activation function
\sigma
satisfies the conclusion of the universal approximation theorem
(notice that
a_{0}
below is a scalar whilst we took it as
a vector in
(
1.8
)):

Lemma 4.6
.

Within the prototypical example
1.1
, assume that
the closure, for the supremum norm over

{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
, of the linear span of the
set
\{x\mapsto a_{0}\sigma(a_{1}\cdot x+a_{2}),\ (a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\}
contains the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}})

of continuous functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
vanishing at
\infty
, then

b
in
(
1.8
) satisfies
Assumption
(
Discriminating Property
)
.

In words, the assumption of Lemma

4.6
may be formulated as follows: for any function
f\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}})
and any
\epsilon>0
,
there exist
an integer

m\geq 1

and a tuple
(a^{j}:=(a_{0}^{j},a_{1}^{j},a_{2}^{j}))_{1\leq j\leq m}\in({\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}})^{m}
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}\biggl|f(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{0}^{j}\sigma(a_{1}^{j}\cdot x+a_{2}^{j})\biggr|\leq\epsilon.

Compared to the standard formulation of the universal approximation result, the above approximation property is slightly unusual because the state space (over which the supremum norm is taken) is non-compact. However, most of the classical activation functions
\sigma
satisfy the above statement, see for instance
Itô

[
27
]
. In particular, all our assumptions are satisfied if
\sigma
is the hyperbolic tangent or the logistic function.

Proof.

Take a probability measure

\pi
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}
such that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}|z|d\pi(x,z)<\infty
. Assume that

(4.17)

\forall a\in A,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}\Bigl[b(x,a)\cdot z\Bigr]d\pi(x,z)=0.

We first prove that, for any continuous function

f
from
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
vanishing at infinity,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}[f(x)\cdot z]d\pi(x,z)=0
.
To do so, it suffices to prove that,
for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(4.18)

\biggl|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}\bigl[f(x)\cdot z\bigr]d\pi(x,z)\biggr|\leq\varepsilon.

By the universal approximation property,
we know that, for any coordinate

i\in\{1,\cdots,d_{1}\}
,
there exist
an integer
m_{i}\geq 1

and a tuple

(a_{0}^{i,j},a_{1}^{i,j},a_{2}^{i,j})_{1\leq j\leq m_{i}}\in({\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}})^{m_{i}}
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}\biggl|\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{0}^{i,j}\sigma(a_{1}^{i,j}\cdot x+a_{2}^{i,j})-f^{i}(x)\biggr|\leq\varepsilon,

where
f^{i}
denotes the
i
th coordinate of
f
. Writing
e^{i}
for the
i
th vector
of the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
, the above can be reformulated as

\begin{split}&\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}b\biggl(x,\bigl(a_{0}^{i,j}e^{i},a_{1}^{i,j},a_{2}^{i,j}\bigr)\biggr)-f(x)\biggr|=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{0}^{i,j}\sigma(a_{1}^{i,j}\cdot x+a_{2}^{i,j})e^{i}-f(x)\biggr|\leq c\varepsilon,\end{split}

for a constant
c
only depending on
d_{1}
.
And therefore, (
4.17
) leads to
(
4.18
). As announced, we deduce that, for any
f\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}})
,
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}[f(x)\cdot z]d\pi(x,z)=0
.
Since
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}|z|d\pi(x,z)<\infty
, the latter integral is well-defined and, in fact, by a standard approximation argument, the identity
is true for any bounded and measurable function

f
from
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
into itself. In the end, we have shown that, for any
such
f
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}\biggl[f(x)\cdot\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}z\pi(x,dz)\biggr)\biggr]d\pi_{\rm x}(x)=0.

which shows that, for
\pi_{\rm x}
-almost every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
,
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}}z\pi(x,dz)=0
.
∎

5.
Local Polyak–Lojasiewicz Condition

The main purpose of this section is
to state and prove a rigorous version of Meta-Theorem
1.4
. For
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, a control
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
and associated pair
(\bm{\gamma},\bm{u})
solution to the forward-backward system (
2.20
), we associate the following functional:

(5.1)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)\\
&:=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\nu_{t}(a)+\epsilon\nabla_{a}\ell(a)+\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}(a)dt.\end{split}

It is implicitly understood the left-hand side is equal to
+\infty
if,
for
t
in a non-null Borel subset of
[t_{0},T]
,
\nu_{t}
is not absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure or if
\nu_{t}
is absolutely continuous but the root
\sqrt{\nu_{t}}
does not belong to
H^{1}(A)
(the subset of
L^{2}(A)
with weak derivative in
L^{2}(A)
). There is another interpretation
of
{\mathcal{I}}
, which is very useful in the proof
of Theorem

5.1
.
For
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})

we recall the notation
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}]\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
introduced in (
2.19
):

(5.2)

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}](a):=\frac{1}{z_{t}}\exp\biggl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\biggr),\quad a\in A,\quad t\in[t_{0},T],

where
z_{t}

is a normalization constant
(similar to

z^{*}_{t}
in
Remark

2.8
).
In particular,
taking the logarithm in
(
5.2
),
we can rewrite
\mathcal{I}
as a Fischer information:

(5.3)

\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)=\epsilon^{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}}{\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}]}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}(a)dt.

We also recall, see Lemma
2.11
, that
\Gamma[\bm{\nu}]
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality.

At this stage, we notice that, for any
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
,
\mathcal{I}((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})=0
if and only if
({\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\gamma}},{\bm{u}})
solves the system of optimality conditions (
2.11
)-(
2.12
).
The result established in this section is to push the latter observation further.
In words,
the rigorous version of Meta-Theorem

1.4

stipulates that, for
{\bm{\nu}}
in the neighborhood of a stable minimizer

\bm{\nu}^{*}
(in the sense of Definition

2.25
),
the functional
{\mathcal{I}}((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})
grows at least like the difference between

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})

and

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*})
.
This principle is referred to as a local Polyak–Lojasiewicz condition.
It
takes the following form:

Theorem 5.1
.

For every compact subset
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{O}
, there exist
r,c>0
such that, for all
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in\mathcal{K}
with associated stable solution
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
and
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
, it holds

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{*}_{t}\bigr)dt\leq r^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)\geq c\Bigl(J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\Bigr).

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
5.1
which is divided in a series of steps.

Step 1. Contradicting the statement.

Generally speaking, we argue by contradiction.
The first point is thus to notice that if the conclusion of Theorem
5.1

does not hold, then there exists
a sequence of tuples
(r_{n},c_{n},t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n},\bm{\nu}^{*,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
satisfying the
following

Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
).

(i)

The sequences
(r_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
(c_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are positive valued sequences converging to
0
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
;

(ii)

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})
belongs to
\mathcal{K}
(and therefore to
\mathcal{O}
); as such,
J((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\cdot)

has a unique stable solution denoted
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}=(\nu_{t}^{*,n})_{t_{0}^{n}\leq t\leq T}
;

(iii)

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0}^{n})
such that

(5.4)

\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu_{t}^{*,n}\bigr)dt\leq r_{n}^{2}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)<c_{n}\Bigl(J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)\Bigr);

(iv)

The sequence
(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})

toward some
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
in
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Notice that item (iv) is somewhat for free since
\mathcal{K}
is a
compact subset of
{\mathcal{O}}
. Notice in particular that
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
is assumed to be in
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Because
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
belongs to
{\mathcal{O}}
,
we can apply Lemma
2.15

and deduce

Lemma 5.2
.

Under
Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)
,
denote by
\bm{\gamma}^{*,n}
and

\bm{u}^{*,n}
the curve and multiplier associated with
each
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
. Then, the sequence
(\bm{\nu}^{*,n},\bm{\gamma}^{*,n},\bm{u}^{*,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
