converges strongly (i.e., in the same sense as in the statement of
Lemma
2.15
) toward
(\bm{\nu}^{*},\bm{\gamma}^{*},\bm{u}^{*})=(\nu^{*}_{t},\gamma^{*}_{t},u^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
,
with
\bm{\nu}^{*}
denoting
the (unique) minimizer of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
,
and
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
and
\bm{u}^{*}

denoting the corresponding curve and multiplier.

So far, we have just used items (i), (ii) and (iv) in

Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)
. We now make use of
item (iii). For a given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we call
({\bm{\gamma}}^{n},{\bm{u}}^{n})
the solution to (
2.20
) associated with
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}
. Also, we introduce the integrated relative entropy

(5.5)

\lambda_{n}^{2}:=\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{n}|\nu_{t}^{*,n}\bigr)dt.

Notice that the second equation in (
5.4
) implies that

{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\neq\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
and therefore
\lambda_{n}>0
. On the other hand, the first equation in (
5.4
) together with the fact that the sequence
(r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
0
shows that
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
0
as well. Together with Lemma
5.2
this shows that we are precisely in the setting of Section
3
. In particular,
Property
(\mathcal{Q}_{0})
therein holds.

The first step toward a contradiction is the following observation.

Lemma 5.3
.

Under
Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)

and with the notation (
5.5
) for
\lambda_{n}
,
we have

(5.6)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)=0.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
we know that

J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{n}\bigr)-J\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)=O(\lambda_{n}^{2})

as
n\rightarrow+\infty.
Dividing the second equation in (
5.4
) by
\lambda_{n}^{2}
and recalling that
c_{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
we get the result.
∎

Since
\lambda_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we can introduce the normalized variables

(5.7)

\eta_{t}^{n}:=\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}-\nu^{*,n}_{t}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad\rho_{t}^{n}:=\frac{\gamma^{n}_{t}-\gamma^{*,n}_{t}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad v_{t}^{n}:=\frac{u_{t}^{n}-u^{*,n}_{t}}{\lambda_{n}},\quad t\in[t_{0}^{n},T].

We can now apply Proposition
3.5
in Section
3
to find
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})\in\mathcal{A}^{l}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{R}(t_{0})\times\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1})
solution to

(5.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\partial_{t}v_{t}-b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}=b(x,\eta_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}&\mbox{\rm in }[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad v_{T}=0&\mbox{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\\
\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\rho_{t}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}(b(x,\eta_{t})\gamma^{*}_{t})&\mbox{\rm in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\qquad\rho_{t_{0}}=0&\mbox{\rm in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\end{array}\right.

such that
(\bm{\eta}^{n},\bm{\rho}^{n},\bm{v}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges, up to a sub-sequence, to
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
in the sense of Proposition
3.5
.

Step 2. Proving that
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}},{\bm{v}})=(0,0,0)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Under
Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)

and with the notations introduced in Step 1,
it holds that

({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}},{\bm{v}})=(0,0,0)
.

Proof.

We are going to show that
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\rho},\bm{v})
is solution to the linearized system (
2.38
)-(
2.39
) and conclude by stability of
\bm{\nu}^{*}
since stable solutions are precisely those for which
(0,0,0)
is the only solution to (
2.38
)-(
2.39
). Following
(
5.3
), we let, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and

t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]
,

(5.9)

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)=\frac{1}{z_{t}^{n}}\exp\biggl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{n}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x,y)\biggr),\quad a\in A,

where
z_{t}^{n}
is a normalization constant.
By Lemma
3.3
,
\left\|\bm{\nu}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})}=\left\|\bm{\nu}^{*,n}+\lambda_{n}\bm{\eta}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0}^{n})}
is bounded independently from
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By the same Lemma,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}^{n}(x,y)
is also bounded independently from
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore we can apply Lemma
2.11
and deduce that
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality with constant independent from
t\in[t_{0}^{n},T]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thanks to (
5.6
)
in the statement of
Lemma

5.3

and to the log-Sobolev inequality
(see Lemma

2.11
, with an additional approximation argument
allowing us to choose
f\equiv\nu_{t}^{n}/\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
),
we deduce that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}(a)}{\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n}),\bm{\nu}^{n}\bigr)=0.

Using Pinsker’s inequality (
[
3
, (5.2.2)]
), this leads to

(5.10)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\biggl(\int_{A}\bigl|\nu_{t}^{n}(a)-\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)\bigr|da\biggr)^{2}dt=0.

We now return back
to the definition of
\bm{\eta}^{n}
in
(
5.7
), from which we obtain the decomposition:

\eta_{t}^{n}(a)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\Bigl(\nu_{t}^{n}(a)-\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)\Bigr)+\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\Bigl(\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)-\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)\Bigr),\quad(t,a)\in[t_{0}^{n},T]\times A.

Introducing the variable

k_{t}(a):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle,\quad(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A

and the constant
c_{t}=\int_{A}k_{t}(a)d\nu_{t}^{*}(a)
we deduce

(5.11)

\begin{split}\int_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}^{T}\biggl(\int_{A}\Bigl|&\eta_{t}^{n}(a)+\nu_{t}^{*}(a)\bigl(k_{t}(a)-c_{t}\bigr)\Bigr|da\biggr)^{2}dt\leq\frac{2}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}^{T}\biggl(\int_{A}\bigl|\nu_{t}^{n}(a)-\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)\bigr|da\biggr)^{2}dt\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+2\biggl\{\int_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}^{T}\biggl(\int_{A}\Bigl|\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}}\bigl(\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}](a)-\nu_{t}^{*,n}(a)\bigr)+\nu^{*}_{t}(a)\bigl(k_{t}(a)-c_{t}\bigr)\Bigr|da\biggr)^{2}dt\biggr\}.\end{split}

The first term in the right-hand side is handled by (
5.10
) and the second term by Proposition
3.7

and we get

(5.12)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}^{T}\biggl(\int_{A}\Bigl|\eta_{t}^{n}(a)+\nu_{t}^{*}(a)(k_{t}(a)-c_{t})\Bigr|da\biggr)^{2}dt=0.

We now apply Proposition
3.5
and specifically the convergence it provides for

(\bm{\eta}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We choose in (
3.11
) therein a smooth test function
f
with compact support included in

(t_{0},T]\times A
.
We deduce from (
5.12
) that

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\varphi_{t}(a)\eta_{t}(a)dadt=-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\varphi_{t}(a)(k_{t}(a)-c_{t})\nu_{t}^{*}(a)dadt

which gives, for almost every

(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A
,

\eta_{t}(a)=-\nu_{t}^{*}(a)(k_{t}(a)-c_{t}).

Together with
(
5.8
),
this means that
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}},{\bm{v}})
is a solution to the linearized system (
2.38
)-(
2.39
) and therefore -since
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
belongs to
\mathcal{O}
- we have
({\bm{\eta}},{\bm{\rho}},{\bm{v}})=(0,0,0)
.

∎

Step 3. Conclusion.

We now complete the proof of
Theorem
5.1
. We use the latter statement to establish:

Proposition 5.5
.

There is no sequence

(r_{n},c_{n},t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n},\bm{\nu}^{*,n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}

satisfying

Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)
, i.e.,

Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)
is empty.
In particular, Theorem

5.1
holds true.

Proof.

Under
Property (
{\mathcal{Q}}
)
, with the notations introduced in

Step 2
we write
\mathcal{I}
as a Fischer information, as in (
5.3
)

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)=\epsilon^{2}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt,\end{split}

which we rewrite

\mathcal{I}\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)=\epsilon^{2}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu_{t}^{*,n}}(a)-\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]}{\nu_{t}^{*,n}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt.

We recall from (
3.5
) in Lemma
3.5
that the probability measure
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]
can be rewritten as

\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}^{n}]\propto\nu_{t}^{*,n}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\lambda_{n}}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\cdot)\cdot d(\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{*,n}\rho_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n})(x,y)\Bigr),

and then, by Young’s inequality

(5.13)

\begin{split}&\epsilon^{2}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt\leq 2\mathcal{I}(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n},{\bm{\nu}}^{n})\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+2\lambda_{n}^{2}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d(\nabla_{x}v_{t}^{n}\gamma_{t}^{n}+\nabla_{x}u^{*,n}_{t}\rho_{t}^{n})(x,y)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu^{n}_{t}(a)dt.\end{split}

From Lemma
5.3
, the second part of Proposition
3.6
and display (
5.13
), we conclude that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt=0.

However, by log-Sobolev inequality for
\bm{\nu}^{*,n}
(see Lemma

2.11
),

\lambda_{n}^{2}=\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}(a)}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}(a)}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt\leq C\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\epsilon\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt,

for some
C>0
independent from
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Dividing by
\lambda^{2}_{n}
and letting
n\rightarrow+\infty
we obtain

1\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{C}{\lambda_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{n}}{\nu^{*,n}_{t}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt=0,

which is the desired contradiction.
∎

6.
Existence of Optimal Controls and First Order Conditions

In this section, we address the optimal control problem (
1.1
).
We first establish the existence of a minimizer
in Subsection

6.1
.
In Subsection

6.2
, we give a rigorous proof of the first order condition. Further properties of the first order system are established in
Subsection

6.3
.

6.1.
Existence of Optimal Solutions

Here our objective is to establish the following statement:

Proposition 6.1
.

For any
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
,
the minimization problem (
1.1
) admits at least one solution.

The proof relies on several preliminary technical lemmas. In particular we will need the properties of solutions to the continuity equation already presented in Lemma
2.4
.

We state the following result (already quoted in (
2.3
)), which provides a bound for the fourth moment of
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
in terms of the cost
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})
.
(We recall that
\nu^{\infty}
, which appears in the statement below,
has been introduced in (
1.6
).)

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that, for any
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and
{\bm{\nu}}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
,

(6.1)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt+\sup_{t_{0}\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T}\biggl\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}|a|^{2}d\nu_{t}(a)dt\biggr\}\leq C\biggl(1+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt\biggr).

As a consequence, there exist two constants
c,C
, with
c>0
, such that, for any
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and any

{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
,

(6.2)

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)\geq-C+c\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt.

In particular, the right-hand side is (uniformly) bounded
on sub-level sets
of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
.

Proof.

We start with the following observation. Letting
g_{d^{\prime}}(a):=(2\pi)^{-d^{\prime}/2}\exp(-|a|^{2}/2)
for
a\in A
(with
d^{\prime}
denoting the dimension of
A
)
and using the non-negativity of the relative entropy between two probability measures,
we deduce that there exists a constant
C
such that, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(A)
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}(\nu|\nu^{\infty})=\mathcal{E}(\nu|g^{d^{\prime}})+\int_{A}\log\biggl(\frac{g_{d}^{\prime}(a)}{\nu^{\infty}(a)}\biggr)d\nu(a)&\geq\int_{A}\log\biggl(\frac{g_{d}^{\prime}(a)}{\nu^{\infty}(a)}\biggr)d\nu(a)\\
&\geq-C+\int_{A}\Bigl(\ell(a)-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\Bigr)d\nu(a).\end{split}

Take now
t_{0}\in[0,T]

and
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
.
Apply the above inequality with
\nu=\nu_{t}
.
By
the coercivity condition (ii) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
and then
Young’s inequality, we deduce that (for a possibly new value of the constant
C
)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt\leq C\biggl(1+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt\biggr).

And then, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
we have, for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]

with
t_{1}<t_{2}
,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}|a|^{2}d\nu_{t}(a)dt&\leq\biggl\{\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt\biggr\}^{1/2}\leq C^{1/2}\biggl(1+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt\biggr)^{1/2},\end{split}

from which
(
6.1
)
easily follows.

We turn to the proof of
(
6.2
).
Recall
(
1.2
)
for the definition of

J
. Since the function
L
in the definition of
J
is lower bounded, we deduce that there exists a constant

C
(independent of
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
and
{\bm{\nu}}
)
such that

(6.3)

-C+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt\leq J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr).

Using
(
6.1
),
we complete the proof.
∎

The next statement provides a very useful compactness and continuity result for the controls and their related trajectories and multipliers.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}_{p}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
for some
p\geq 2
and

({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
be
a sequence of elements of

{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
satisfying

(6.4)

\sup_{n\geq 1}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)<+\infty.

Then, (
\bm{\nu}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
admits some weak limit points and any weak limit

{\bm{\nu}}

belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
.
Moreover, denoting by

({\bm{\gamma}}^{n},\bm{u}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
the curves and multipliers corresponding
to
({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
, see equations
(
2.8
) and (
2.10
),
and denoting
by
({\bm{\gamma}},\bm{u})
the curve and multiplier
corresponding to

{\bm{\nu}}
, the following three properties hold true:

(i)

The sequence

({\bm{\gamma}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is bounded in

{\mathcal{C}}^{1/2}([t_{0},T],{\mathcal{P}}_{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}))
and the sequence
(\bm{u}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is bounded in
\mathcal{C}^{1/2}\bigl([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1}\bigr)\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{3}_{2,1})
;
In particular, the sequence

({\bm{\gamma}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is relatively compact in

{\mathcal{C}}^{1/2}([t_{0},T],{\mathcal{P}}_{p-\delta}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}))
for any
\delta\in(0,p-1)
, and
the sequence

(\bm{u}^{n})_{n\geq 1}

and the sequence of its spatial derivatives are relatively compact for the
uniform topology on compact subsets of
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}
;

(ii)

Along any sub-sequence
(\varphi(n))_{n\geq 1}
with
\varphi:\mathbb{N}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{*}
strictly increasing, such that

({\bm{\nu}}^{\varphi(n)})_{n\geq 1}

converges (in the weak sense) to

{\bm{\nu}}
,

({\bm{\gamma}}^{\varphi(n)})_{n\geq 1}
converges in

{\mathcal{C}}([t_{0},T],{\mathcal{P}}_{p-\delta}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}))
to
{\bm{\gamma}}
for any
\delta\in(0,p-1)
and
(\bm{u}^{\varphi(n)})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
\bm{u}
in the following sense: for any closed ball
B
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, it holds

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|u^{\varphi(n)}_{t}-u_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(B)}=0.

(iii)

J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n})
.

Proof.

Step 1.

For each
n\geq 1
,
we insert the bound
(
6.4
)
in the inequality
(
6.3
).
Recalling the formula
(
2.5
), we deduce that

\sup_{n\geq 1}{\mathcal{E}}\bigl({\bm{\nu}}^{n}|{\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\times\nu^{\infty}\bigr)<\infty.

Therefore, by

[
9
, Lemma 2.4]
,
the sequence

({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
(regarded up to a normalization by
T-t_{0}
as a sequence of probability measures on
[0,T]\times A
) has a weakly converging sub-sequence, still denoted by
({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
, with limit point
{\bm{\nu}}
.
Testing the convergence against
a
-independent test functions, we find that the time marginal of
{\bm{\nu}}
is the Lebesgue measure
and we denote by

t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu_{t}\in{\mathcal{P}}(A)

the disintegration of
{\bm{\nu}}
, i.e.

d{\bm{\nu}}(t,a)=d{\nu}_{t}(a)dt
(by normalizing

{\bm{\nu}}
by
T-t_{0}
, we are reduced to the disintegration of
a probability measure, from which we deduce
the

(\nu_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
’s
are indeed probability measures).
By lower-semicontinuity of the entropy, see again

[
9
, Lemma 2.4]
, we deduce that

{\mathcal{E}}({\bm{\nu}}|{\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\times\nu^{\infty})<\infty
.
This shows
that
{\bm{\nu}}
belongs to

{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
.

Step 2.

Using the bound (
6.4
) together with Lemma

6.2
, we
deduce that

(6.5)

\sup_{n\geq 1}\biggl\{\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu^{n}_{t}(a)dt+\sup_{t_{1}<t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt\biggr\}<+\infty.

Property
(i)
is then a consequence of Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
2.6
.

Step 3.
We now prove item
(ii)
in the statement. We start with the analysis of the limit points of the sequence
(\bm{\gamma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
(whose existence is guaranteed by item
(i)
in the statement). With the implicit convention that
the sub-sequence
(\varphi(n))_{n\geq 1}

in item
(ii)
of the statement
is taken as the identity, i.e.
(\varphi(n)=n)_{n\geq 1}
,
we are going to show that, for any
\delta\in(0,p-1)
,
(\bm{\gamma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges in
\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}_{p-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))
toward the unique weak solution
{\bm{\gamma}}
of

(6.6)

\partial_{t}{\gamma}_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}(b(x,{\nu}_{t}){\gamma}_{t})=0,\quad{\gamma}(t_{0})=\gamma_{0}.

We take
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))
as a limit point
and for the ease of notation, do not relabel the subsequence here again.

In order to prove that

\overline{\bm{\gamma}}
satisfies
(
6.6
), we
fix
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}([t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
. For every
n\geq 1
, it holds

(6.7)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{t_{0}}(x,y)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{T}(x,y)d\gamma^{n}_{T}(x,y)-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{n}_{t}(x)dt\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu_{t}^{n})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x)dt\\
&=:I_{1}^{n}-I_{2}^{n}.\phantom{\biggl(}\end{split}

By regularity of
\varphi
, we easily have that

(6.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}I_{1}^{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{T}(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{T}(x,y)-\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{t}(x,y)dt.

We now handle
I_{2}^{n}
, which we rewrite in the form

I_{2}^{n}=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\biggl[\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}^{n}(x,y)\biggr]d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt=:\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}f^{n}(t,a)d\nu_{t}^{n}(a)dt,

with an obvious definition for
f^{n}
. By item (i) in Assumption (
Regularity
),
there exists a constant
C
such that,
for any
n\geq 1
,

|f^{n}(t,a)|\leq C(1+|a|)
.
Moreover,
because
\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{p-\delta}(\gamma_{t}^{n},\overline{\gamma}_{t})\rightarrow 0

as
n\rightarrow+\infty
, it holds,
for any
(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}f^{n}(t,a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{t}(x,y)=:f(t,a),

uniformly on compact subsets of

[0,T]\times A
. By combining the weak convergence of
\bm{\nu}^{n}
toward
\bm{\nu}
together with
the
L^{4}
-bound
(
6.5
), we easily deduce that

\begin{split}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}I^{n}_{2}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{[t_{0},T]\times A}f^{n}(t,a)d{\bm{\nu}}^{n}(t,a)&=\int_{[t_{0},T]\times A}f(t,a)d{\bm{\nu}}(t,a)\\
&=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{t}(x,y)\biggr]d\nu_{t}(a)dt.\end{split}

Inserting
(
6.8
) and the above display in
(
6.7
),
we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{T}(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{T}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\varphi_{t_{0}}(x,y)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl[\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(x,y)+b(x,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}(x,y)\bigr]d\overline{\gamma}_{t}(x,y)dt.

By the uniqueness result established in Proposition

2.4
,
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}
coincides with the (unique) solution
{\bm{\gamma}}
of the equation
(
2.8
). Moreover, the whole sequence
({\bm{\gamma}}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
{\bm{\gamma}}

in

{\mathcal{C}}([t_{0},T],{\mathcal{P}}_{p-\delta}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}))
.

Step 4.
We go on with the analysis of the limit points of
(\bm{u}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
(whose existence is guaranteed by item
(i)
in the statement). We consider
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{C}^{1/2}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})
such that, along a sub-sequence
(\varphi(n))_{n\geq 1}
as in item
(ii)
of the statement,
for all closed ball
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,

(6.9)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|u_{t}^{\varphi(n)}-\overline{u}_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(B)}=0.

For all
(t,x,y)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
and all
n\geq 1
we have

u_{t}^{\varphi(n)}(x,y)=L(x,y)+\int_{t}^{T}b(x,\nu_{s}^{\varphi(n)})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{s}^{\varphi(n)}(x,y)ds.

Using the weak convergence of
(\bm{\nu}^{\varphi(n)})_{n\geq 1}
to
\bm{\nu}
,
together with the convergence (
6.9
) and the uniform bound (
6.5
),
we deduce that, for all
(t,x,y)\in[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,

\overline{u}_{t}(x,y)=L(x,y)+\int_{t}^{T}b(x,\nu_{s})\cdot\nabla_{x}\overline{u}_{s}(x,y)ds,

and,
by the uniqueness result established in Proposition

2.6
, we conclude that the whole sequence
(\bm{u}^{\varphi(n)})_{n\geq 1}
converges to the unique
solution of the transport equation

-\partial_{t}u_{t}-b(x,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}=0\quad\mbox{ in }[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\quad u_{T}=L\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.

This completes the proof of item
(ii)
in the statement.

Step 5.

We now establish item
(iii)
. By Step 1 (weak compactness of the sequence

({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
), we can assume without any loss of generality that

({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}

converges to
{\bm{\nu}}
.
By lower boundedness and continuity of
L
, and by convergence in
d_{p-\delta}
of
(\gamma_{T}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
toward
{\gamma}_{T}

(which follows from
Step 3)
, we deduce that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d{\gamma}_{T}(x,y)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}^{n}(x,y).

Moreover, by
[
9
, Lemma 2.4]

{\mathcal{E}}\bigl({\bm{\nu}}|\textrm{\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\times\nu^{\infty}\bigr)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{E}}\bigl({\bm{\nu}}^{n}|\textrm{\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\times\nu^{\infty}\bigr).

Using the formula
(
2.5
) in order to rewrite the two costs below in terms of the above two entropies, we deduce that

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}\bigr)\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\bigr).

This completes the proof.
∎

We can easily deduce the existence of optimal solutions from the result above.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

We take a minimizing sequence
({\bm{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
J((t_{0},\nu_{0}),\cdot)
.
By Lemma
6.2
, we can find
{\bm{\nu}}

in
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
such that

J((t_{0},\nu_{0}),{\bm{\nu}})\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),{\bm{\nu}}^{n})
. This suffices to conclude.
∎

6.2.
Optimality Conditions: Proof of Theorem
2.7

We go on with proof of the first order optimality conditions. The strategy is to
linearize
the terminal cost around the optimal solution in order to bring ourselves back to the minimization of a (convex) relative entropy for which we now the unique minimizer.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Let
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
be an optimal control
and

\bm{\gamma}^{*}=(\gamma^{*}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
be the corresponding optimal trajectory. For another

{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
and some
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we let
{\bm{\nu}}^{\lambda}:=(1-\lambda)\bm{\nu}^{*}+\lambda{\bm{\nu}}
and, then,
we call
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\lambda}=(\gamma_{t}^{\lambda})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
the solution to the continuity equation
(
2.8
) driven by
{\bm{\nu}}^{\lambda}
.
By optimality of
\bm{\nu}^{*}
, we have, for all
\lambda\in[0,1],

(6.10)

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\leq J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{\lambda}\bigr).

By (linear) convexity of the function
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})\mapsto\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt
(recall that
\nu^{\infty}
has been introduced in (
1.6
)),
we deduce from (
6.10
) that

\displaystyle\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{\lambda}|\nu^{\infty})dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d(\gamma_{T}^{\lambda}-\gamma^{*}_{T})(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\lambda)\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt+\lambda\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d(\gamma_{T}^{\lambda}-\gamma^{*}_{T})(x,y).

Subtracting
\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt
from both sides and dividing by
\lambda
, we get

\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt\leq\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d(\gamma_{T}^{\lambda}-\gamma^{*}_{T})(x,y).

Letting
\lambda\rightarrow 0^{+}
we obtain, thanks to (
2.33
) in Proposition
2.19
,

\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt\leq\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu_{t}-\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)dt,

where
\bm{u}^{*}
is the solution to the transport equation (
2.10
) driven by
\bm{\nu}^{*}
.
Equivalently, this can be rewritten as

(6.11)

\displaystyle\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu^{*}_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)dt

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)dt.

Following the statement, we now introduce, for each
t\in[t_{0},T]
, the
probability density (over
A
):

\nu^{*,\infty}_{t}(a):=\frac{1}{z^{*,\infty}_{t}}\exp\biggl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\biggr),\quad a\in A,

where
z_{t}^{*,\infty}
is the normalization constant

z^{*,\infty}_{t}:=\int_{A}\exp\biggl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)\biggr)da.

By (i) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
and because
\gamma^{*}_{t}
belongs to
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})

and

\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}
has linear growth (see Proposition

2.6
),
the integral inside the exponential appearing in the definition of

\nu_{t}^{*,\infty}
is finite
and less than
C(1+|a|)

for a constant
C
independent of
t
.
In particular, one can easily prove that

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}{\mathcal{E}}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{*,\infty}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt<+\infty.

From the latter definition, we deduce that

\bm{\nu}^{*,\infty}:={\rm Leb}_{[t_{0},T]}\otimes(\nu^{*,\infty}_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})
(see Definition
2.1
). We then rewrite the right-hand side of
(
6.11
) in the form

(6.12)

\begin{split}&\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\nu_{t}(a)dt\\
&=\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*,\infty}\bigr)dt+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\log\frac{z^{\infty}}{z_{t}^{*,\infty}}dt,\end{split}

and, similarly, for the left-hand side of (
6.11
)

(6.13)

\begin{split}&\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\gamma^{*}_{t}(x,y)d\nu^{*}_{t}(a)dt\\
&=\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*,\infty}\bigr)dt+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\log\frac{z^{\infty}}{z_{t}^{*,\infty}}dt,\end{split}

where
z^{\infty}=\int_{A}e^{-\ell(a)}da
is the normalizing constant for
\nu^{\infty}
.
We deduce from (
6.11
)
that
\bm{\nu}^{*}
is a minimizer of the function

{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathcal{A}}(t_{0})\mapsto\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*,\infty})dt=\epsilon{\mathcal{E}}({\bm{\nu}}|\bm{\nu}^{*,\infty})
.
However,
by strict convexity of the relative entropy, the latter function has a unique minimizer, which is

\bm{\nu}^{*,\infty}
.
This proves that

{\bm{\nu}}^{*}=\bm{\nu}^{*,\infty}
, and we deduce that, for almost every

t\in[t_{0},T]
,
\nu^{*}_{t}
is equal to
\nu_{t}^{*,\infty}
.
Collecting the equations for
\bm{\nu}^{*}
,
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
and
\bm{u}^{*}
, we get the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
).

It remains to justify that the density
(t,a)\mapsto\nu_{t}^{*,\infty}(a)
is jointly continuous. This follows from the explicit formula (
2.11
),
assumptions (i) and (ii) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
together with the estimates on
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
and
\bm{u}^{*}
proven in
Lemma

2.4

and
Proposition
2.6
.
∎

6.3.
Additional Regularity and First Stability Results

We first prove that solutions of the system of optimality conditions satisfy further regularity properties.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

Step 1.

Throughout the proof,
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}},\Lambda_{\gamma_{0},\bm{\nu}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow[1,+\infty)
denote two non-decreasing functions, respectively of
\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt
and of
\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|+|y|)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)
and are allowed to change from line to line. Below, the last two arguments are not explicitly written as inputs of the two functions but are implicitly understood. Therefore, we simply write
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}}
and
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0},\bm{\nu}}
.
By Proposition

2.6

, we know that
{\bm{u}}
satisfies

(6.14)

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}},

while Proposition
2.4
gives

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|+|y|)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}.

Thanks to the representation formulas
(
2.11
)
and (
2.13
)
(with
\bm{\nu}^{*}
replaced by

{\bm{\nu}}
)
and to the assumptions (i) and (ii) (on
b
and
\ell
) in

Assumption (
Regularity
)
,
we deduce that, for all
(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A
,

(6.15)

\bigl[\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}\bigr]^{-1}e^{-4\ell(a)/3}\leq\nu_{t}(a)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}e^{-3\ell(a)/4}.

This proves the first claim in the statement.

Step 2.
In order to prove the second claim, we consider a pair of random variables
(X_{0},Y_{0})\sim\gamma_{0}
supported on some probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. We call
(X_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
the solution to
the ODE

\dot{X}_{t}=b(X_{t},\nu_{t}),\quad t\in[t_{0},T];\quad X_{t_{0}}=X_{0}.

By Proposition
2.4
, we know that
\gamma_{t}=\mathbb{P}\circ(X_{t},Y_{0})^{-1}
,
for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
.
Letting
Z_{t}:=\nabla_{x}u_{t}(X_{t},Y_{0})
for
t\in[t_{0},T]
,
the representation formulas
(
2.11
)
and (
2.13
)
(for

{\bm{\nu}}
)
can be rewritten in the form

(6.16)

\begin{split}&\nu_{t}(a)=\frac{1}{z_{t}}\exp\biggl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{t},a)\cdot Z_{t}\bigr]\biggr),\quad(t,a)\in[t_{0},T]\times A,\\
&z_{t}=\int_{A}\exp\biggl(-\ell(a)-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{t},a)\cdot Z_{t}\bigr]\biggr)da,\quad t\in[t_{0},T].\end{split}

By Lemma
A.13

,
(Z_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
solves the backward ODE

\dot{Z}_{t}=-\nabla_{x}b(X_{t},\nu_{t})Z_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T];\quad Z_{T}=\nabla_{x}L(X_{T},Y_{0}).

Thanks to (
6.15
) and to the
growth assumptions on
b
and
\nabla_{x}b
, see
(i) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
, we get,
\mathbb{P}-
almost-surely
and for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

|b(X_{t},\nu_{t})|+|\nabla_{x}b(X_{t},\nu_{t})|\leq C\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d\nu_{t}(a)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}.

Getting back to the definition of
(Z_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
, together with the estimate (
6.14
) we get

\displaystyle|Z_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}}(1+|X_{t}|+|Y_{0}|)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}}(1+|X_{0}|+|Y_{0}|)

where we used that

|X_{t}-X_{0}|=\biggl|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}b(X_{s},\nu_{s})ds\biggr|\leq C\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|d\nu_{s}(a)ds\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}}.

We deduce that,

\mathbb{P}-
almost-surely and for all
(t_{1},t_{2})\in[t_{0},T]
,

|X_{t_{2}}-X_{t_{1}}|+|Z_{t_{2}}-Z_{t_{1}}|\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+|X_{0}|+|Y_{0}|)|t_{2}-t_{1}|.

Using the growth assumptions on
b
and
\nabla_{x}b
again, this leads to

\bigl|\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{t_{2}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{2}}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(X_{t_{1}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{1}}\bigr]\bigr|\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+|a|^{2})|t_{2}-t_{1}|,\quad(t_{1},t_{2},a)\in[t_{0},T]^{2}\times A.

Using
(
6.15
),
we also have

z_{t}\geq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}^{-1}

for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
. Moreover, thanks to
the explicit formulas in (
6.16
),
we also deduce

\begin{split}\bigl|z_{t_{2}}-z_{t_{1}}\bigr|&\leq\int_{A}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)\Bigr)\biggl|\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t_{2}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{2}}\bigr]\Bigr)-\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t_{1}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{1}}\bigr]\Bigr)\biggr|da\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{A}\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)+\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+|a|)\Bigr)\Bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t_{2}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{2}}\bigr]-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t_{1}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{1}}\bigr]\Bigr|da\\
&\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|\int_{A}\bigl(1+|a|^{2}\bigr)\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)+\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+|a|)\Bigr)da\\
&\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|.\end{split}

Similarly,

\begin{split}\bigl|z_{t_{2}}\nu_{t_{2}}(a)-z_{t_{1}}\nu_{t_{1}}(a)\bigr|&\leq\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)\Bigr)\biggl|\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t_{2}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{2}}\bigr]\Bigr)-\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[b(X_{t_{1}},a)\cdot Z_{t_{1}}\bigr]\Bigr)\biggr|\\
&\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|\bigl(1+|a|^{2}\bigr)\exp\Bigl(-\ell(a)+\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+|a|)\Bigr).\end{split}

Combining the last two displays with the two bounds
(
6.15
)
and
z_{t_{1}},z_{t_{2}}\geq[\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}]^{-1}
, we deduce that

\bigl|\nu_{t_{2}}(a)-\nu_{t_{1}}(a)\bigr|\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|e^{-3\ell(a)/4},

i.e.,

(6.17)

\bigl\|e^{3\ell/4}\bigl[\nu_{t_{2}}-\nu_{t_{1}}\bigr]\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|.

In particular,

(6.18)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}\bigl(\nu_{t_{2}}|\nu_{t_{1}}\bigr)&=\int_{A}\log\biggl(1+\frac{\nu_{t_{2}}(a)-\nu_{t_{1}}(a)}{\nu_{t_{1}}(a)}\biggr)d\nu_{t_{2}}(a)\\
&\leq\int_{A}\frac{|\nu_{t_{2}}(a)-\nu_{t_{1}}(a)|}{\nu_{t_{1}}(a)}d\nu_{t_{2}}(a)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}|t_{2}-t_{1}|\int_{A}\frac{\nu_{t_{2}}(a)}{\nu_{t_{1}}(a)}e^{-3\ell(a)/4}da.\end{split}

By
(
6.15
), we complete the proof
of the penultimate display in the statement. We proceed similarly to justify that

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}

and complete the proof of (
2.18
).

Step 3.

By
Lemma
A.6
with
l=3
,
p=2
and
q=1

(the continuity of
t\mapsto\nu_{t}
here following from the second step right above), we get the regularity properties of

u
, as stated in (
2.15
)–(
2.16
).
∎

We go on with the proof of the log-Sobolev inequality satisfied by the probability measures
(\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}])_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
when
\bm{\nu}
is an element of
\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
.

Proof of Lemma
2.11
.

For
R>0
, let
\varphi^{R}:A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a cutoff function satisfying

(6.19)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|\varphi^{R}(a)|+|\nabla\varphi^{R}(a)|+|\nabla^{2}\varphi^{R}(a)|\leq c,&\quad{\rm for}\ a\in A,\\
\varphi^{R}(a)=0,&\quad{\rm if}\ |a|\geq R+1,\\
\varphi^{R}(a)=1,&\quad{\rm if}\ |a|\leq R,\end{array}\right.

for some
c>0
independent of
R>0
. Let us rewrite
the argument inside the exponential
factor in
(
2.19
)
as

(6.20)

\ell(a)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)=\ell_{t}^{1}(a)+\ell_{t}^{2}(a),

with

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\ell_{t}^{1}(a):=\ell(a)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\bigl(1-\varphi^{R}(a)\bigr),&\quad a\in A,\\
\displaystyle\ell_{t}^{2}(a):=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\varphi^{R}(a),&\quad a\in A.\end{array}\right.

The aim is to show that, for a suitable choice of the function

\varphi^{R}
(subject to the constraint (
6.19
)), the term
\ell^{1}
is strongly convex
in
a
uniformly with respect to the parameter

t
. To do so, we recall from Assumption

(Regularity)
that
|\nabla^{2}_{aa}b(x,a)|\leq C(1+|a|)(1+|x|^{2})
, for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}
and
a\in A
. This says that

\Bigl|\nabla_{aa}^{2}\bigl(\ell_{t}^{1}-\ell\bigr)(a)\Bigr|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle C(1+|a|)\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}}\bigl(1+|x|^{2}\bigr)(1+|x|+|y|)d\gamma_{t}(x,y),&\quad\textrm{\rm if}\ |a|>R,\\
\displaystyle 0,&\quad\textrm{\rm if}\ |a|\leq R.\end{array}\right.

In particular, thanks to Propositions

2.4
with
p=3

and
2.6
, there exists a non decreasing function
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

\Bigl|\nabla_{aa}^{2}\bigl(\ell_{t}^{1}-\ell\bigr)(a)\Bigr|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle(1+|a|)\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}},&\quad\textrm{\rm if}\ |a|>R,\\
\displaystyle 0,&\quad\textrm{\rm if}\ |a|\leq R.\end{array}\right.

where, following
the notation used in the proof of
Proposition

2.9
,

\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}
is a shorthand notation for

\Lambda\biggl(\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr).

We now use the fact that
\nabla^{2}_{aa}\ell
grows at least quadratically fast, see (
1.7
).
Therefore, we can find
R:=R_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}>0
, depending
on
(\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0})
only through an upper bound for
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}
such that, choosing this
R
in the definition of
\ell^{1}
, we have

\nabla^{2}_{aa}\ell_{t}^{1}(a)\geq\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{aa}^{2}\ell(a),\quad\forall a\in A,\quad\forall t\in[t_{0},T],

and, by assumption (
1.7
), there is
\mu>0
(depending only on
\ell
) such that

\nabla_{aa}^{2}\ell_{t}^{1}(a)\geq\mu I_{d^{\prime}},\quad\forall a\in A,\quad\forall t\in[t_{0},T].

Therefore, by
[
3
, Corollary 5.7.2]
, the probability density (on
A
) that is proportional to
\exp{(-\ell_{t}^{1}(a))}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality with some constant
C
depending only on
\mu
. With the same choice of parameter
R
in the definition of
\ell^{2}
, we get, using again Propositions

2.4
and
2.6
,

\displaystyle|\ell_{t}^{2}(a)|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+R_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}})\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(1+|x|)d\gamma_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+R_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}).

We can conclude from the decomposition (
6.20
) and Holley-Stroock’s perturbation property
(see

[
3
, Proposition 5.1.6]
)
that
\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}]
satisfies a Log-Sobolev inequality with constant

C_{\rm LSI}(\Gamma_{t}[\bm{\nu}])\leq Ce^{\Lambda_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}}(1+R_{\bm{\nu},\gamma_{0}})}

for some
C
depending only on
\mu
.
∎

We continue with the proof of Lemma
2.13
.

Proof of Lemma
2.13
.

To estimate the first term in the definition of the norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}
, we rely on a generalization of Pinsker inequality stated in
[
6
, Theorem 2.1]
and recalled in Appendix
A.3
for convenience. It gives, for all
\kappa>0
,
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0})
and
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

\displaystyle\int_{A}(1+|a|^{4})

\displaystyle|\nu_{t}(a)-\nu^{*}_{t}(a)|da

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\biggl[\frac{3}{2}+\log\biggl(\int_{A}\exp\Bigl\{\frac{2}{\kappa}(1+|a|^{4})\Bigr\}d\nu^{*}_{t}(a)\biggr)\biggr]\biggl[\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)\biggr].

Thanks to estimate (
2.17
) and the growth assumption on
\ell
, we can choose
\kappa>0
large enough depending only on
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)
, such that
\int_{A}\exp\{(2/\kappa)(1+|a|^{4})\}d\nu^{*}_{t}(a)
is bounded independently
of
t\in[t_{0},T]
. Integrating in time and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we
can find a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{4})|\nu_{t}(a)-\nu_{t}^{*}(a)|dadt

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}\biggl[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)^{1/2}dt+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)dt\biggr]

(6.21)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}\biggl[\sqrt{T-t_{0}}\biggr(\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)dt\biggr)^{1/2}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)dt\biggr],

where
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}:=\Lambda(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)).

Finally, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
, we can use the second item in the generalized Pinsker inequality proven in
[
6
, Theorem 2.1]

–see Appendix
A.3
– to justify that, for every
c>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})|\nu_{t}(a)-\nu_{t}^{*}(a)|dadt

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{c}\left\|c(1+|a|^{2})(\nu_{t}-\nu_{t}^{*})\right\|_{TV}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{c}\biggl(1+\log\int_{A}e^{c^{2}(1+|a|^{2})^{2}}d\nu^{*}_{t}(a)\biggr)^{1/2}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*}\bigr)^{1/2},

where
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{TV}
is the total variation norm. In particular, recalling the exponential bound (
2.17
), we can choose
c>0
depending only on
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)
such that the integral term in the right-hand side is bounded independently of
t\in[t_{0},T]
. If we integrate (
6.22
) in time and use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we find, for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[t_{0},T]
with
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
and for possibly another value of the non-decreasing function
\Lambda
,

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d|\nu_{t}-\nu_{t}^{*}|(a)dt\leq C\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}\Bigl(\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}|\nu_{t}^{*})dt\Bigr)^{1/2}.

Combined with (
6.21
), this completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Before we prove Proposition
2.14
, we first establish the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists
C>0
such that, for any
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and any
minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}
of
J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
,

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\nu^{*}_{t}|\nu^{\infty})dt\leq C\biggl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr).

Proof.

Take
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
. Since we can always see
t\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\nu^{\infty}
as a competitor in the control problem, we have

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}^{\infty}(x,y)

where
(\gamma^{\infty}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
is the solution to

\partial_{t}\gamma_{t}^{\infty}+\operatorname{div}_{x}(b(x,\nu^{\infty})\gamma_{t}^{\infty})=0\quad\mbox{ in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}};\quad\gamma^{\infty}_{t_{0}}=\gamma_{0}.

Using the fact that
L
is at most of quadratic growth, we deduce from
Proposition
2.4
that

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*}\bigr)\leq C\biggl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr),

for some
C>0
independent from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
.
∎

We also need
the next result, which we use to compare the metrics
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})^{*}}
and
d_{2}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

For any two probability measures
\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})^{*}}\leq\biggl[1+\sqrt{2}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}\bigr)d\bigl(\gamma^{1}+\gamma^{2}\bigr)(x,y)\biggr)^{1/2}\biggr]d_{2}\bigl(\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\bigr).

Proof.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, with
|\nabla\phi(x,y)|\leq 1+|x|+|y|
for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
. Then, for
any probability space

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
equipped with two couples of random variables
((X^{i},Y^{i}))_{i=1,2}
such that

\mathbb{P}\circ(X^{i},Y^{i})^{-1}=\gamma^{i}
for
i=1,2
(with
\gamma^{1}
and
\gamma^{2}
as in the statement), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\phi(x,y)d(

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}-\gamma^{1})(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\phi(X^{2},Y^{2})-\phi(X^{1},Y^{1})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(1+|(X^{2},Y^{2})|+|(X^{1},Y^{1})|\bigr)\bigl(|(X^{2},Y^{2})-(X^{1},Y^{1})|\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[(1+|(X^{2},Y^{2})|+|(X^{1},Y^{1})|)^{2}\Bigr]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[|X^{2}-X^{1}|^{2}+|Y^{2}-Y^{1}|^{2}\Bigr]^{1/2}

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz inequality at the third line. We get the result by taking the infimum over all possible couples
(X^{1},Y^{1})
and
(X^{2},Y^{2})
with
(X^{i},Y^{i})\sim^{\mathbb{P}}\gamma^{i}
for
i=1,2
(which does impact the left-hand side),
and then the supremum over
\phi
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.

Preliminary step.
By Lemma
6.4
, there exists
C>0
such that, for any
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and any minimizer
\bm{\nu}^{*}=(\nu_{t}^{*})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

of
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
, it holds

(6.23)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{*}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt+\left\|\bm{\nu}^{*}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}\leq C\biggl(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr).

Obviously, the bound for the first term in the left-hand side directly follows from
Lemma
6.4
. Then, the bound for
\left\|\bm{\nu}^{*}\right\|_{\mathcal{D}(t_{0})}

follows from (
6.1
) in Lemma
6.2
.

Main step: local Lipschitz regularity of
U
.
We first address the regularity
of
U
in the measure variable. Let
t_{0}\in[0,T]

and

\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
be fixed,
and
\bm{\nu}^{*}
be an optimal control for
J((t_{0},\gamma^{2}),\cdot)
. We call
(\gamma_{t}^{1})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
and

(\gamma_{t}^{2})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
the solutions to the continuity equation
(
2.8
) driven by the same control
\bm{\nu}^{*}
but starting respectively from
(t_{0},\gamma^{1})
and
(t_{0},\gamma^{2})
(so that

\bm{\gamma}^{2}=(\gamma_{t}^{2})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
is an optimal curve for
J((t_{0},\gamma^{2}),\cdot)
).
Using the optimality of
\bm{\nu}^{*}
for
J((t_{0},\gamma^{2}),\cdot)
, we have

U(t_{0},\gamma^{1})-U(t_{0},\gamma^{2})\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}^{1}(x,y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}^{2}(x,y).

In order to handle the right-hand side,
we use a duality argument. We call

u:[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the solution to the backward equation (
2.10
) with control
\bm{\nu}^{*}
. By Lemma
A.12

(with
{\bm{\nu}}^{1}
and
{\bm{\nu}}^{2}
being both equal to
{\bm{\nu}}^{*}
therein,
so that the second line
in
(
A.29
)
disappears), we deduce that

\displaystyle U(t_{0},\gamma^{1})-U(t_{0},\gamma^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}u_{t_{0}}(x,y)d\gamma^{1}(x,y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}u_{t_{0}}(x,y)d\gamma^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|u_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1}}\left\|\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})^{*}}\phantom{\biggl)}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma^{2}(x,y)\biggr)\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\bigl(\gamma^{1}+\gamma^{2}\bigr)(x,y)\biggr]^{1/2}d_{2}(\gamma^{2},\gamma^{1}),

for some non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, independent of
t_{0}
,
\gamma^{1}
and
\gamma^{2}
. To get the last line, we used
Proposition
2.6
together with the preliminary step
in order to bound

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|u_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1}}

and then Lemma
6.5

in order to bound
\|\gamma^{1}-\gamma^{2}\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})^{*}}
.
Exchanging the roles of
\gamma^{1}
and
\gamma^{2}
, we conclude that

(6.24)

\begin{split}&\bigl|U(t_{0},\gamma^{1})-U(t_{0},\gamma^{2})\bigr|\leq\Lambda\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\bigl(\gamma^{1}+\gamma^{2}\bigr)(x,y)\biggr)d_{2}(\gamma^{2},\gamma^{1}),\end{split}

for some possibly different function
\Lambda
independent of
(t_{0},(\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}))\in[0,T]\times(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))^{2}
. This proves the local Lipschitz property in the measure argument.

In order to address the time regularity, we proceed as follows. We fix
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
as well as
t_{1}\in(t_{0},T]
. We take
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
an optimal control for
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
(for simplicity, we remove the superscript
*

in the notation

\bm{\nu}
of the optimal control)
and we denote by
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
the resulting curve. By dynamic programming,
(\nu_{t})_{t\in[t_{1},T]}
is a minimizer of
J((t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}}),\cdot)
, with

U(t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}})

as optimal cost.
As a consequence, we get

(6.25)

\displaystyle U(t_{1},\gamma_{0})-U(t_{0},\gamma_{0})

\displaystyle=U(t_{1},\gamma_{0})-U(t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}})-\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt.

We handle the difference
U(t_{1},\gamma_{0})-U(t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}})
by means of
(
6.24
).
To do so,
we notice from Proposition
2.4
and the preliminary step that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{t_{1}}(x,y)\leq\Lambda(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y))
, for a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
independent of the
choices of
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
,
\gamma_{0}
. And then,
(
6.24
)
yields

\bigl|U(t_{1},\gamma_{0})-U(t_{1},\gamma_{t_{1}})\bigr|\leq\Lambda\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{t_{0}}(x,y)\biggr)d_{2}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t_{1}}).

Inserting the above bound in the expansion
(
6.25
)
and then invoking
(
2.18
) in Proposition
2.9
,
we deduce that

(6.26)

\bigl|U(t_{1},\gamma_{0})-U(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\bigr|\leq\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}\Bigl(d_{2}\bigl(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t_{1}}\bigr)+|t_{1}-t_{0}|\Bigr),

where the constant
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}
is given by
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}=\Lambda(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y))

for a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

independent of
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
and
\gamma_{0}
.

In order to complete the proof, it remains to show that, in
(
6.26
),

d_{2}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t_{1}})

can be bounded by
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}|t_{1}-t_{0}|
. We thus establish in this paragraph
that
the curve
{\bm{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

is Lipschitz continuous in
t
w.r.t. to the distance
d_{2}
.
Using the ODE representation of
{\bm{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

provided by Proposition

2.16
,
we see that it suffices to get a bound on the drift
(x\mapsto b(x,\nu_{t}))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
.
By
(
2.17
) in
Proposition
2.9
, it is enough to get a bound for the term
\Lambda_{{\bm{\nu}},\gamma_{0}}
in (
2.17
).
The latter is an increasing function of

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}(a)dt
, which is bounded by

C(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y))
thanks to the preliminary step,
and of

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}(x,y)
.
Up to a new value of the constant
\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}
(equivalently, for
a possibly different choice of the non-decreasing function
\Lambda

introduced right above), we get

d_{2}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t_{1}})\leq\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}|t_{1}-t_{0}|.

Therefore,
by
(
6.26
),

|U(t_{1},\gamma_{0})-U(t_{0},\gamma_{0})|\leq\Lambda_{\gamma_{0}}|t_{1}-t_{0}|,

which completes the proof of the statement.
∎

The rest of the section is dedicated to the proof of Lemma

2.15
, which requires some preliminary results. We start with the following statement:

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that, for any pair of initial conditions
(t_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{1}),(t_{0}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{2})\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{2}})
, and any pair of solutions
(\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\gamma}^{1},\bm{u}^{1}),(\bm{\nu}^{2},\bm{\gamma}^{2},\bm{u}^{2})
to the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) with respective initial condition
(t_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{1})
and
(t_{0}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{2})
,

\begin{split}&\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]}\Bigl\{\bigl\|e^{3\ell/4}(\nu^{2}_{t}-\nu^{1}_{t})\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}+\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{1}|\nu_{t}^{2}\bigr)+\bigl\|u_{t}^{2}-u_{t}^{1}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2}}\Bigr\}\\
&\leq\sup_{i=1,2}\Lambda\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}|x|^{2}d\gamma_{0}^{i}(x,y)+\int_{t_{0}^{i}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu_{t}^{i}(a)dt\biggr)\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]}\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}^{1}\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2})^{*}}.\end{split}

Proof.

Throughout the proof, the value of the
non-decreasing function
\Lambda:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow[1,+\infty)
is allowed
to change from line to line.
To simplify the notations, we just denote the quantity
\sup_{i=1,2}\Lambda(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}d\gamma_{0}^{i}(x,y)+\int_{t_{0}^{i}}^{T}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{4})d\nu_{t}^{i}(a)dt)
by
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}.

Let us write
\delta u_{t}:=u_{t}^{2}-u_{t}^{1}
for
t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]
, and let

(X_{s}^{2,t,x})_{t\leq s\leq T}
be the solution to

\dot{X}_{s}^{2,t,x}=b(X_{s}^{2,t,x},\nu_{t}^{2}),\quad s\in[t,T];\quad X_{t}^{2,t,x}=x.

By Lemma
A.12
(with
{\bm{\gamma}}^{1}

therein –not to be confused with the current choice of

{\bm{\gamma}}^{1}
– being understood as

(\delta_{(X_{s}^{2,t,x},y)})_{t\leq s\leq T}
with initial condition
\delta_{(x,y)}
at time
t
, which is
a very specific
example of application of Proposition
2.4

when the continuity equation is initalized from a delta mass), and
\bm{\varphi}^{2}
in Lemma
A.12
being understood as
\bm{u}^{1}
)
we obtain

\begin{split}u_{t}^{1}(x,y)&=L(X_{T}^{2,t,x},y)-\int_{t}^{T}b(X_{s}^{2,t,x},\nu_{s}^{2}-\nu_{s}^{1})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{s}^{1}(X_{s}^{2,t,x},y)ds\\
&=u_{t}^{2}(x,y)-\int_{t}^{T}b(X_{s}^{2,t,x},\nu_{s}^{2}-\nu_{s}^{1})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{s}^{1}(X_{s}^{2,t,x},y)ds,\end{split}

which gives

(6.27)

\delta u_{t}(x,y)=-\int_{t}^{T}b(X_{s}^{2,t,x},\nu_{s}^{2}-\nu_{s}^{1})\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{s}^{1}(X_{s}^{2,t,x},y)ds.

Since
\|X_{s}^{2,t,\cdot}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1,b}}
and
\|\nabla_{x}u_{s}^{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}}

are bounded independently of
s
and
n
(see Propositions
A.1
and
2.6
), we get, by the growth assumption on
b
in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
, for all
t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]
,

(6.28)

\|\delta u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}}\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{3})|\nu_{s}^{2}(a)-\nu_{s}^{1}(a)|dads.

Getting back to the expressions
for
\nu_{t}^{1}
and
\nu_{t}^{2}
, see
(
2.11
),
we observe that the difference between the two arguments in the exponential form of the Gibbs density
can be estimated as follows:

\begin{split}&\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl[b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}{u}^{1}_{t}(x,y)\bigr]d{\gamma}_{t}^{1}(x,y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl[b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}{u}^{2}_{t}(x,y)\bigr]d{\gamma}_{t}^{2}(x,y)\biggr|\\
&\leq\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl[b(x,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}{u}^{1}_{t}(x,y)\bigr]d\bigl({\gamma}_{t}^{1}-{\gamma}_{t}^{2}\bigr)(x,y)\biggr|\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\biggl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot\bigl[\nabla_{x}{u}^{1}_{t}(x,y)-\nabla_{x}{u}^{2}_{t}(x,y)\bigr]d{\gamma}_{t}^{2}(x,y)\biggr|.\end{split}

Using (i) in
Assumption (
Regularity
)
together with
Proposition
2.6
, we
can bound the first term by

\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}(1+|a|^{3})\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}^{1}\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2})^{*}}
. Moreover,
we can invoke Proposition

2.4

to
bound the second term by

\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}(1+|a|)\|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}
.
Following the proof of (
6.17
), we obtain, for
(t,a)\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]\times A
,

(6.29)

|\nu_{t}^{2}(a)-\nu_{t}^{1}(a)|\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}\Bigl(\|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}+\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}^{1}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2})^{*}}\Bigr)\exp\Bigl(-\frac{3}{4}\ell(a)\Bigr).

Injecting (
6.28
) into (
6.29
) and using Grönwall’s Lemma,
we get

\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]}\bigl\|e^{3\ell/4}\bigl[\nu^{2}_{t}-\nu^{1}_{t}\bigr]\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]}\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}^{1}\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2})^{*}}.

Using (
6.28
) again, we deduce that

(6.30)

\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]}\Bigl\{\bigl\|e^{3\ell/4}\bigl[\nu^{2}_{t}-\nu^{1}_{t}\bigr]\Bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|u_{t}^{2}-u_{t}^{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2}}\Bigr\}\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{1},\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}^{1},\gamma_{0}^{2}}\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{1}\vee t_{0}^{2},T]}\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}^{1}\|_{(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{2})^{*}}.

It remains to estimate the relative entropy term,
but this follows from the same argument as in (
6.18
).
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Using log-Sobolev inequality,
we can also obtain the following estimate when
two solutions to the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
start from a common initial position
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
in
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}).

Lemma 6.7
.

There is a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
and any two solutions
(\bm{\nu}^{i},\bm{\gamma}^{i},\bm{u}^{i})
,
i=1,2
to the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) with
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
as common initial condition,
it holds

\begin{split}&\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{2}|\nu_{t}^{1})\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\Lambda\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}(x,y)+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d(\nu_{t}^{1}+\nu_{t}^{2})(a)\biggr)\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|\gamma_{t}^{2}-\gamma_{t}^{1}\right\|^{2}_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}.\end{split}

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

By the log-Sobolev inequality stated in Lemma
2.11

(with
{\bm{\nu}}
being taken as

{\bm{\nu}}^{2}
,
in which case
\Gamma({\bm{\nu}})
is also equal to

{\bm{\nu}}^{2}
, and
f
being taken as
\nu_{t}^{1}/\nu_{t}^{2}
), there is a non-decreasing function
\Lambda:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{1}|\nu_{t}^{2})\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}}\int_{A}\Bigl|\nabla_{a}\log\frac{\nu_{t}^{1}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}(a)\Bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{1}(a)

where
\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}}
is a short-hand notation for
\Lambda\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})^{3/2}d\gamma_{0}(x,y)+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu^{2}_{t}(a)dt\bigr)
. Recalling the equations satisfied by
\bm{\nu}^{1}
and
\bm{\nu}^{2}
(or equivalently (
2.19
)), and using the regularity assumption on
b
this leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\nu_{t}^{1}|\nu_{t}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\int_{A}\bigl|\nabla_{a}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}b(x,a)\cdot d\bigl(\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{1}\gamma_{t}^{1}-\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{2}\gamma_{t}^{2})(x,y)\bigr|^{2}d\nu_{t}^{1}(a)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\Lambda_{\bm{\nu}^{2},\gamma_{0}}\biggl(\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{1}-\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{2}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{t}^{1}(x,y)+\left\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{2}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}}\left\|\gamma_{t}^{1}-\gamma_{t}^{2}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{2})^{*}}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\times\int_{A}(1+|a|^{6})d\nu_{t}^{1}(a).

It remains to use the previous Lemma
6.6
together with Proposition
2.9
to conclude.
∎

Using the estimates from Lemma
6.6
, we can now prove Lemma
2.15
.

Proof of Lemma
2.15
.

Throughout the proof, we take for granted the notations introduced in the statement of Lemma
2.15
.
Following (
6.23
) in the preliminary step of the proof of Proposition
2.14
together with the fact that
(\gamma_{0}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges toward
\gamma_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}})
, we have

(6.31)

\sup_{n\geq 1}\Bigl\{\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\int_{A}|a|^{4}d\nu^{*,n}_{t}(a)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\gamma_{0}^{n}(x,y)\Bigr\}<+\infty.

By extending

\bm{\nu}^{*,n}

to the entire
[t_{0},T]
if necessary, letting in this case

\nu^{*,n}_{t}=\nu^{*,n}_{t_{0}^{n}}
for
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}^{n})
,
we obtain, for any
n\geq 1
, an admissible control
\overline{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\in\mathcal{A}(t_{0}\wedge t_{0}^{n})

(the fact that
it satisfies
(
2.1
)
follows from
(
2.18
), applied to

{\bm{\nu}}^{*,n}
). Taking
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
as an initial condition for
each
n\geq 1
, we then let
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{n}=(\overline{\gamma}^{n}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
the resulting curve starting from
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
(which curve should not be confused with

{\bm{\gamma}}^{*,n}
).

Step 1.
We first claim that
(\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
\bm{\gamma}^{*}
in
\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}))
. Our strategy is to prove that
(\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a minimizing sequence for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot)
and then to use Lemma
6.3
. To this end, we express the cost of
\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n}
as

(6.32)

\displaystyle J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\overline{\nu}_{t}^{n}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{T}^{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle=J\bigl((t^{n}_{0},\gamma^{n}_{0}),\bm{\nu}^{*,n}\bigr)+\epsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\overline{\nu}_{t}^{n}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt-\epsilon\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{*,n}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{T}^{n}(x,y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}^{*,n}(x,y).

On the one hand, using the estimate (
2.18
) in Proposition
2.9

and the fact that

\overline{\bm{\nu}}^{n}
and

{\bm{\nu}}^{*,n}

coincide on
[t_{0}^{n},T]
,
we find a constant
C>0
that is independent of
n\geq 1
thanks to (
6.31
), such that

(6.33)

\Bigl|\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\overline{\nu}_{t}^{n}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt-\int_{t_{0}^{n}}^{T}\mathcal{E}\bigl(\nu_{t}^{*,n}|\nu^{\infty}\bigr)dt\Bigr|\leq C|t_{0}^{n}-t_{0}|.

On the other hand, introducing
\bm{\overline{u}}^{n}:[t_{0}^{n}\wedge t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the solution to the backward equation associated to the control
\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n}

and observing that
\bm{\overline{u}}^{n}
coincides with
{\bm{u}}^{*,n}

on
[t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0},T]
,
we get by the duality relation stated in Lemma
A.12
(applied twice, once
with
(t_{0},\gamma_{0})
as initial condition and

{\bm{\nu}}^{1}={\bm{\nu}}^{2}=\overline{\bm{\nu}}^{n}

as control, and once with

(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})
as initial condition and

{\bm{\nu}}^{1}={\bm{\nu}}^{2}={\bm{\nu}}^{\star,n}

as control),

(6.34)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\overline{\gamma}_{T}^{n}(x,y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}L(x,y)d\gamma_{T}^{*,n}(x,y)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\overline{u}_{t_{0}}^{n}(x,y)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\overline{u}_{t_{0}^{n}}^{n}(x,y)d\gamma_{0}^{n}(x,y)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl[\overline{u}_{t_{0}}^{n}(x,y)-\overline{u}^{n}_{t^{n}_{0}}(x,y)\bigr]d\gamma_{0}(x,y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\overline{u}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}}(x,y)d(\gamma_{0}-\gamma_{0}^{n})(x,y).\end{split}

The estimate from Proposition
2.6
gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\bigl[\overline{u}_{t_{0}}^{n}(x,y)-\overline{u}^{n}_{t^{n}_{0}}(x,y)\bigr]d\gamma_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\overline{u}_{t_{0}}^{n}-\overline{u}_{t_{0}^{n}}^{n}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}_{1}}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|+|y|)d\gamma_{0}(x,y)\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{|t_{0}^{n}-t_{0}|}.

Moreover, by Lemma
6.5
,
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\overline{u}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}}(x,y)d(\gamma_{0}-\gamma_{0}^{n})(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\overline{u}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1}}\left\|\gamma_{0}-\gamma_{0}^{n}\right\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1})^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\overline{u}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1}}\biggl[1+\sqrt{2}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})d\bigl(\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{0}^{n}\bigr)(x,y)\biggr)^{1/2}\biggr]d_{2}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}^{n}).

Thanks to Proposition
2.6
and
(
6.31
) again,
we can bound

\|\overline{u}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2,1}}

by a constant independent of
n
. And then, the left-hand side is less than

Cd_{2}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}^{n})
,
for some
C>0
independent of
n\geq 1
. Thanks to the last two displays, we deduce that the left-hand side in
(
6.34
)
is less than
C(\sqrt{|t_{0}^{n}-t_{0}|}+d_{2}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}^{n}))
.
Combining this bound together with (
6.32
) and
(
6.33
), and recalling the definition of the value function
U
in
(
2.22
), we obtain

J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)\leq U(t_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}^{n})+C\sqrt{|t_{0}^{n}-t_{0}|}+d_{2}(\gamma_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0}).

Using Proposition
2.14
, we get

\limsup_{n\rightarrow+\infty}J\bigl((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n}\bigr)\leq U(t_{0},\gamma_{0}).

This implies that
(\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a minimizing sequence for
J((t_{0},\gamma_{0}),\cdot).
By uniqueness, we deduce from the compactness argument of Lemma
6.3
that
(\bm{\overline{\nu}}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges weakly to
\bm{\nu}^{*}
. By Lemma
6.3
again, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}d_{2}\bigl(\overline{\gamma}_{t}^{n},\gamma^{*}_{t}\bigr)=0.

Step 2.
Now we claim that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0},T]}d_{2}(\overline{\gamma}_{t}^{n},\gamma_{t}^{*,n})=0.

Indeed,
because the continuity
equations for both
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{n}
and

{\bm{\gamma}}^{*,n}
are driven by the same vector field after

t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}

(which vector field is Lipschitz continuous uniformly in
n\geq 1
),
it suffices, by
an elementary stability argument,
to compare

\overline{\gamma}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}

and

{\gamma}^{*,n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}
, and thus to bound

d_{2}(\overline{\gamma}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}},{\gamma}^{*,n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}})
.
Obviously,

d_{2}\bigl(\overline{\gamma}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}},{\gamma}^{*,n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}\bigr)\leq d_{2}\bigl(\overline{\gamma}^{n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}},{\gamma}_{0}\bigr)+d_{2}\bigl({\gamma}_{0},\gamma_{0}^{n}\bigr)+d_{2}\bigl(\gamma_{0}^{n},{\gamma}^{*,n}_{t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0}}\bigr).

We recall that
\gamma_{0}
is the initial condition of

\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{n}
and

\gamma_{0}^{n}
the initial condition of

{\bm{\gamma}}^{*,n}
.
Following the last paragraph in the proof of Proposition
2.14
, one can prove that
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{n}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}^{*,n}
are time Lipschitz continuous, uniformly in
n\geq 1
.
We deduce that

\sup_{t\in[t_{0}^{n}\vee t_{0},T]}d_{2}(\overline{\gamma}_{t}^{n},\gamma_{t}^{*,n})\leq C\bigl(|t_{0}^{n}-t_{0}|+d_{2}(\gamma_{0}^{n},\gamma_{0})\bigr),

for some
C>0
independent of
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. The claim follows easily.

Conclusion.
It remains to apply Lemmas
6.6
and
6.5
to conclude.

∎

7.
The Linearized Continuity and Transport Equations

The goal of this section is to address the well-posedness of the linearized continuity equation (
2.26
) as well as the linearized transport equation (
2.36
) and, in particular, to prove Propositions
2.19
and
2.21
. The corresponding formulation of these results are given in Propositions
7.7
,

7.9
and
7.11
for the linearized continuity equation, and in Proposition
7.14
for the linearized transport equation. These results are essential for establishing the second-order optimality conditions of Theorem
2.39
, which is the purpose of Section
8
.

The current section is somewhat lengthy and technical, and may be skipped on a first reading.

7.1.
The Linearized Continuity Equation

The focus of this subsection is the linearized equation (
2.26
), that we rewrite here:

(7.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{t}+\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\nu_{t})\rho_{t}\bigr)=-\operatorname{div}_{x}\bigl(b(x,\eta_{t})\gamma_{t}\bigr)&\quad\mbox{in }(t_{0},T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}},\\
\displaystyle\rho_{t_{0}}=0&\quad\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}.\end{array}\right.

We also recall that the notion of solution is given in Subsection
2.4
.

7.1.1.
A preliminary measurability result

Proposition 7.1
.

Take
\varphi:[t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\nabla_{x}\varphi\in\mathcal{C}\bigl([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2})\cap\mathcal{L}^{\infty}([t_{0},T],\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1})

and
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{C}([t_{0},T],(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1})^{*})
such that
\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi;\rho_{t}\rangle|
is finite. Then,

(1)

For all
t\in[t_{0},T]
, the map
a\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\bigr\rangle
is measurable and, for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(A)
such that
(1+|a|^{2})\nu
belongs to
\mathcal{M}(A)
,
\int_{A}|\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle|d|\nu|(a)
is finite and we have

\int_{A}\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle d\nu(a)=\langle b(\cdot,\nu)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle.

(2)

For all
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
, the map
t\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle
is measurable and coincides with
t\mapsto\int_{A}\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle d\nu_{t}(a)
. Moreover, it satisfies

(7.2)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\bigl|\langle b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle\bigr|dt\leq C_{b}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi;\rho_{t}\rangle|\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left\|\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{A}(1+|a|^{2})d|\nu_{t}|(a)dt,

for a constant
C_{b}>0
depending only on the vector field
b
.

Regarding point (2) in the statement, observe that, as a consequence of the first point, the function
t\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle
is a priori well-defined only at times
t
where
\nu_{t}
belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{1+|a|^{2}}
. By the definition of elements of
\mathcal{D}(t_{0})
, this set is measurable and has full Lebesgue measure. Therefore, it is indeed possible to find a measurable version of the function
t\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,\nu_{t})\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t};\rho_{t}\rangle
and our result says that
the bound in equation (
7.2
) holds.

As for the proof of Proposition
7.1
, it relies on a series of intermediate steps. We start with a suitable approximation of the vector field
b
, based on the following notations. For every
\epsilon>0
we consider a partition of
A
into Borel subsets
A=\sqcup_{p\geq 1}A_{p}(\epsilon)
such that for every
integer

p\geq 1
, there is
a_{p}(\epsilon)\in A_{p}(\epsilon)
such that
A_{p}(\epsilon)\subset B(a_{p}(\epsilon),\epsilon)
(the ball of center
a_{p}(\epsilon)
and radius
\epsilon
). For
\epsilon>0
and
P\geq 1
, we define

b^{\epsilon,P}(a,x):=\sum_{p=1}^{P}b(x,a_{p}(\epsilon))\mathbf{1}_{A_{p}(\epsilon)}(a),\quad b^{\epsilon}(a,x)=\sum_{p\geq 1}b(x,a_{p}(\epsilon))\mathbf{1}_{A_{p}(\epsilon)}(a).

Lemma 7.2
.

There is
C>0
, independent of
a\in A
and
\epsilon>0
, such that

(7.3)

\left\|b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)-b(\cdot,a)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}\leq C\epsilon(1+|a|^{2}).

Moreover, for all
a\in A
,

(7.4)

\lim_{P\rightarrow+\infty}\left\|b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,a)-b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{b}}=0.

Proof.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and all
a\in A
,
b^{\epsilon}(x,a)=b(x,a_{p}(\epsilon))
if
a\in A_{p}(\epsilon).
By assumption on
b
and
\nabla_{x}b
we deduce that

|b^{\epsilon}(x,a)-b(x,a)|+|\nabla_{x}b^{\epsilon}(x,a)-\nabla_{x}b(x,a)|\leq C(1+|x|)(1+|a|^{2})\epsilon

which leads to (
7.3
).
For the second estimate, we have

\left\|b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,a)-b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{b}}\leq\sum_{p=P+1}^{+\infty}\left\|b(\cdot,a_{p}(\epsilon))\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{b}}\mathbf{1}_{A_{p}(\epsilon)}(a)\leq C\sum_{p=P+1}^{+\infty}(1+|a_{p}(\epsilon)|^{2})\mathbf{1}_{A_{p}(\epsilon)}(a).

For every
a\in A
, there is
p_{0}
such that
\mathbf{1}_{A_{p}(\epsilon)}(a)=0
whenever
p\geq p_{0}
and we easily deduce that (
7.4
) holds for every
a\in A
.
∎

Item (1) in the statement of Proposition

7.1
is a consequence of the following lemma:

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function such that
\nabla_{x}\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}
and let
\rho
be en element of
\bigl(\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1}\bigr)^{*}
such that
\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi;\rho\rangle|
is finite. Then
a\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle
is Borel-measurable and, for any
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(A)
such that
(1+|a|^{2})\nu
belongs to
\mathcal{M}(A)
,
\int_{A}|\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle|d|\nu|(a)
is finite and it holds

(7.5)

\int_{A}\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle d\nu(a)=\langle b(\cdot,\nu)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle.

Proof.

For every
a\in A
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl|\langle b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle-\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|[b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)-b(\cdot,a)]\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi;\rho\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)-b(\cdot,a)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}\left\|\nabla_{x}\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi;\rho\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon(1+|a|^{2})\left\|\nabla_{x}\varphi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{1}}\sup_{\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}_{1},\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}_{2}}\leq 1}|\langle\phi;\rho\rangle|,

which shows that

(7.6)

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{a\in A}\frac{\bigl|\langle b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle-\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle\bigr|}{1+|a|^{2}}=0.

Similarly, we can prove that, for every
\epsilon>0
and for every
a\in A

(7.7)

\lim_{P\rightarrow+\infty}\langle b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle=\langle b^{\epsilon}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle.

For every
\epsilon>0
and every
P\geq 1
, we have, by linearity of the duality bracket,

(7.8)

\langle b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle=\sum_{p=1}^{P}\langle b(\cdot,a_{p}(\epsilon))\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle\mathbf{1}_{A_{p}(\epsilon)}(a),\quad a\in A.

In particular,
a\mapsto\langle b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle
is Borel measurable for all
\epsilon>0
and
P\geq 1
and so is the map
a\mapsto\langle b(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle
as the point-wise limit of a sequence of measurable functions. From (
7.8
) and thanks again to the linearity of the duality bracket, we also deduce that, for all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(A)
,

(7.9)

\int_{A}\langle b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,a)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle d\nu(a)=\langle b^{\epsilon,P}(\cdot,\nu)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi;\rho\rangle.

By assumption on
b
, see in particular (
1.24
) in Lemma
1.5
, there is
C>0
such that, for all
\epsilon>0
, all
P>0
and all
